# Machine Learning and Adaptive Decision Methods — integrated prose scaffold

**Status: refreshed 2026-10-18; prose planning only.** Preserve ML-01–83 and all twelve original prerequisite IDs; add P-SDE-03 as a new probability supplier pair. ML-84–93 add ten pairs; the category now proposes 100 A/B pairs (93 ML plus seven category-local prerequisite pairs), with five additional published prerequisite pairs reused in their existing homes and the new P-SDE-03 probability prerequisite pair. Historical six-reader dossiers and the 2026-10-12 audit remain inherited evidence, not new reading or proof acceptance. Current supplier reconciliation and new source-reading limits are recorded in `research/scaffold-refresh-2026-10-18/machine-learning/`. No items or pages are authored here.

Category 4 may consume published mathematics and the earlier statistical
categories 1–3. Every dependency below points to a previous ML pair or an
earlier category/item. There is no reverse dependency from categories 1–3 to
Category 4. In particular, ML owns its learning-specific optimization,
training, and online-decision specializations; generic published numerical and
Bayesian interfaces are reused where their exact hypotheses fit.
Examples companions contain worked examples and counterexamples only and are
never suppliers. An operational procedure is proposed as an `algorithm` item
with an `alg-` ID and a `## Definition`; a guarantee about that procedure is a
separate result item. Approximation and convergence theorems are mathematical
claims with their full topology, architecture, data, distribution, scaling,
and numerical assumptions. Experimental performance, including foundation
model scaling, remains empirical evidence and never supplies a theorem.

## Pair inventory and dependency-ordered designs

### ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization

**Pages:** prediction-problems-risks-and-empirical-minimization / prediction-problems-risks-and-empirical-minimization-examples. **Scope:** Define measurable instance and label spaces with product sigma-algebra, joint law $P$, measurable predictor class, nonnegative measurable loss, population risk $R_P(f)=E_P[\ell(f(X),Y)]$ as an extended nonnegative integral, empirical risk $R_m(f)=m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^m\ell(f(X_i),Y_i)$, class Bayes risk $R_P^*(\mathcal F)=\inf_{f\in\mathcal F}R_P(f)$ and unrestricted Bayes risk $R_P^*=\inf_fR_P(f)$ over measurable predictors, excess risk $R_P(f)-R_P^*(\mathcal F)$ when finite, and exact versus approximate ERM. Define excess-risk differences only when risks being subtracted are finite. A conditional Bayes rule requires a regular conditional label law (for example, standard-Borel label target) and an attained measurable pointwise minimizer; do not infer conditional laws on arbitrary measurable spaces. The published standard-Borel conditional-law existence theorem and the published L2 conditional-projection theorem each assume AC, so state/carry AC when those interfaces are used; the finite-label conditional-expectation route may be stated directly. Proposed interfaces: `def-supervised-learning-problem`, `def-population-and-empirical-risk`, `def-bayes-predictor-and-excess-risk`, `def-empirical-risk-minimizer`, `alg-finite-list-empirical-risk-minimizer`, `alg-approximate-erm`. The finite-list algorithm takes the observed sample and an ordered finite nonempty list of measurable predictors and finite tolerance $\eta\ge0$, evaluates every empirical risk in $[0,+\infty]$, and returns the first minimizer if all empirical risks are $+\infty$; otherwise it returns the first predictor with finite empirical risk at most the finite list minimum plus $\eta$. This defines exact ERM when $\eta=0$, avoids the undefined expression $+\infty+\eta$, and is measurable by finite comparisons of extended-real measurable functions. An infinite class requires a separately stated measurable-selector premise, not a generic executable procedure. Distinguish training risk, population risk, prediction error, and parameter error. **Proof route:** measurability and integration for risk definitions; pointwise conditional minimization only under the conditional-law and selector premises; no existence theorem without compactness/continuity or stated attainment/selection. **Examples:** binary 0–1 loss, squared loss conditional mean, absolute loss conditional median, nonattained infimum. **Requires:** `probability-spaces-random-variables-and-expectation` (`def-law-or-distribution-of-a-random-element`, `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, `thm-existence-of-regular-conditional-distributions-for-standard-borel-targets`, `thm-conditional-expectation-is-the-l2-orthogonal-projection` when used, with their AC premises); earlier MSF-01 — Statistical Experiments, Models and Identifiability (`def-statistical-experiment`, `def-parametric-statistical-model`); MSF-03 — Loss, Risk and Decision Theory (`def-loss-function-and-risk`, `thm-bayes-rule-minimizes-prior-risk`). **Sources:** Mohri, Rostamizadeh, and Talwalkar, *Foundations of Machine Learning*, 2e, Chapter 2 §§2.1–2.4, Theorems 2.5 and 2.13 with complete proofs, physical PDF pp. 26–45; §2.4 for iid supervised setup. The realizable proof is the bad-consistent-hypothesis event and $(1-\epsilon)^m\le e^{-m\epsilon}$; the agnostic result is Hoeffding plus a finite-class union bound. Source claims are limited to finite $H$, iid samples, and their stated bounded loss.

### ML-02 — Finite-class PAC learning and ERM guarantees

**Pages:** finite-class-pac-learning-and-erm / finite-class-pac-learning-and-erm-examples. **Scope:** For binary classifiers with zero-one risk, for $0<\epsilon,\delta<1$, call $A$ $(\epsilon,\delta)$-PAC realizable if for every iid law with a target $h^*\in H$ of zero risk, $P_S[R(A(S))\le\epsilon]\ge1-\delta$; call it agnostic $(\epsilon,\delta)$-PAC if for every iid law $P$, $P_S[R(A(S))-\inf_{h\in H}R(h)\le\epsilon]\ge1-\delta$. Include learner randomization in these probabilities if present. Using the choice-free published `thm-finite-class-uniform-deviation`, for finite nonempty $H$, iid observations, and losses in $[0,1]$, state the two-sided uniform deviation $|R(h)-\widehat R(h)|\le\sqrt{(\log|H|+\log(2/\delta))/(2m)}$ simultaneously for all $h$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. For exact ERM, excess over $\inf_{h\in H}R(h)$ is at most twice this deviation; an $\eta$-approximate ERM adds $\eta$. In the realizable binary case, if the target lies in $H$ and the learner returns a consistent member, then error is at most $\epsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$ whenever $m\ge(\log|H|+\log(1/\delta))/\epsilon$. Keep the realizable and noisy/agnostic rates distinct. State measurability and existence, or use an infimum and measurable approximate selector; the finite-list algorithm in ML-01 gives an operational selector. Proposed interfaces: `def-realizable-and-agnostic-pac`, `thm-finite-class-agnostic-erm-bound`, `thm-finite-class-realizable-pac-bound`, `thm-finite-class-sample-complexity`. **Proof route:** Hoeffding for each fixed hypothesis, union bound, two-sided ERM comparison; realizable failure uses $(1-\epsilon)^m\le e^{-m\epsilon}$. **Examples:** finite lookup tables, duplicate predictors, and why zero training error alone is not a generalization theorem. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; MSF-18 — Concentration (`thm-hoeffding-independent-bounded-sums`) or the explicit finite-class deviation clause. **Sources:** Mohri, Rostamizadeh, and Talwalkar, *Foundations of Machine Learning*, 2e, Chapter 2 §§2.1–2.4, Theorems 2.5 and 2.13 with complete proofs, physical PDF pp. 26–45; Appendix D concentration proofs, pp. 454–465. The source uses finite $H$ and iid samples; the deviation claim here uses losses in $[0,1]$.

### ML-03 — VC dimension, growth functions, and classification sample complexity

**Pages:** vc-dimension-growth-and-pac-classification / vc-dimension-growth-and-pac-classification-examples. **Scope:** Define a measurable instance space $\mathcal X$, nonempty binary class $H\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal X}$, and growth function $\Pi_H(m)=\sup_{x_1,\ldots,x_m\in\mathcal X}|\{(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_m)):h\in H\}|$; a finite indexed sample is shattered exactly when this trace has size $2^m$, and $\mathrm{VCdim}(H)=\sup\{m:\Pi_H(m)=2^m\}$, possibly $+\infty$. Reindex sample points by their indices, so repeated points are handled correctly; the existing finite set-family shattering definition applies only after that reindexing. For finite $d=\mathrm{VCdim}(H)$ with $0\le d\le m$, Sauer gives at most $\sum_{j=0}^d\binom mj$ traces; for $1\le d\le m$ this is at most $(em/d)^d$. Use Mohri et al.'s exact Corollary 3.19 display for $1\le d\le m$: $R(h)\le\widehat R_S(h)+\sqrt{2d\log(em/d)/m}+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/(2m)}$ for all $h$ with probability $1-\delta$, under the source's measurable-supremum assumption or a stated outer-probability version. Treat $d=0$ separately. Derive realizable $O((d\log(1/\epsilon)+\log(1/\delta))/\epsilon)$ and agnostic $O((d+\log(1/\delta))/\epsilon^2)$ upper sample-complexity rates; give explicit universal constants in the authored theorem. An optimal realizable upper bound without $\log(1/\epsilon)$ needs a separate source/proof. Do not include the unreliable minimax lower displays. Proposed interfaces: `def-shattered-set-and-vc-dimension`, `def-growth-function`, `thm-sauer-shelah-growth-bound`, `thm-vc-uniform-convergence`, `thm-vc-realizable-sample-complexity`, `thm-vc-agnostic-sample-complexity`. **Proof route:** restrict to the finite trace family on a sample, apply `thm-sauer-shelah`, then double-sampling or symmetrization and concentration; state countability/separability or outer probability. **Examples:** thresholds, intervals, affine halfspaces, an infinite class with finite VC dimension. **Requires:** ML-02; `def-shattering-and-vc-dimension`, `thm-sauer-shelah`; MSF-18 — Concentration; MSF-19 — Empirical-Process Complexity (`thm-vc-covering-entropy`, `thm-finite-class-uniform-deviation`). **Sources:** Mohri et al., Chapter 3, Theorems 3.3, 3.5, 3.17 and Corollary 3.19, physical PDF pp. 46–76; Bartlett–Mendelson, “Rademacher and Gaussian Complexities,” JMLR 3 (2002), Theorems 5, 7–9, 12 and Appendices A–B. The introductory Bartlett–Mendelson Theorem 1 display omits a confidence term and is excluded. Mohri's official second-edition errata corrects the Theorem 3.17 proof's final equality to an inequality.

### ML-04 — Rademacher complexity and uniform generalization

**Pages:** rademacher-complexity-and-generalization / rademacher-complexity-and-generalization-examples. **Scope:** Use Mohri et al.'s normalization throughout: for $S=(z_1,\ldots,z_m)$ and loss class $G\subseteq[0,1]^Z$, define $\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)=E_\sigma[\sup_{g\in G}m^{-1}\sum_i\sigma_i g(z_i)]$ for independent uniform signs $\sigma_i\in\{-1,+1\}$ and $\mathfrak R_m(G)=E_{S\sim P^m}\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)$. Assume the suprema are measurable or state outer probability. Assume the Axiom of Choice (AC) for the following concentration-based generalization and ERM bounds, as required by the reused McDiarmid supplier. With probability at least $1-\delta$, simultaneously for all $g\in G$, give the one-sided bounds $Pg\le P_mg+2\mathfrak R_m(G)+\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/(2m)}$ and $Pg\le P_mg+2\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)+3\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)/(2m)}$. Use $\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(1-G)=\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)$, apply the same bounds to $1-G$, and take a union bound for two-sided deviation: with probability $1-\delta$, $\sup_g|Pg-P_mg|\le2\mathfrak R_m(G)+\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)/(2m)}$, or empirically $2\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)+3\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)/(2m)}$. Hence an $\eta$-approximate ERM in this same class has excess at most $4\mathfrak R_m(G)+2\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)/(2m)}+\eta$, or empirically $4\widehat{\mathfrak R}_S(G)+6\sqrt{\log(4/\delta)/(2m)}+\eta$. For scalar coordinate maps $\Phi_i$ that are $L$-Lipschitz, state Mohri Lemma 5.7 in this nonabsolute convention: $E_\sigma\sup_{h\in H}\sum_i\sigma_i\Phi_i(h(z_i))\le L E_\sigma\sup_{h\in H}\sum_i\sigma_i h(z_i)$, for a finite/countable or separably measurable class with finite measurable suprema. No zero-at-zero assumption is needed because the fixed coordinate offsets cancel under the signs. Keep the separate two-sided absolute-sup contraction factor $2L$ distinct; never mix Bartlett–Mendelson's alternate normalization. Proposed interfaces: `def-rademacher-complexity`, `thm-rademacher-symmetrization-bound`, `thm-rademacher-contraction-inequality`, `thm-rademacher-uniform-generalization`, `cor-erm-excess-risk-from-uniform-deviation`. **Proof route:** independent ghost sample, Jensen, Rademacher sign symmetrization, bounded-difference tail; for lower deviation apply the theorem to complemented losses, then use the ERM comparison. **Examples:** finite class, linear predictors on a bounded ball, and a loose bound whose radius dependence is visible. **Requires:** ML-01; ML-02; MSF-19 — Empirical-Process Complexity (`def-empirical-process-and-rademacher-process`, `thm-independent-process-symmetrization`, `thm-rademacher-contraction`, `thm-finite-class-uniform-deviation`); MSF-18 — Concentration (`thm-mcdiarmid-bounded-differences`). **Sources:** Mohri et al., *Foundations of Machine Learning*, 2e, Definition 3.1, Theorem 3.3 and proof, physical PDF pp. 30–33, for the declared normalization and constants; Bartlett and Mendelson, “Rademacher and Gaussian Complexities: Risk Bounds and Structural Results,” JMLR 3 (2002), Theorems 5, 7–9, 12 and Appendices A–B for comparison. Their introductory Theorem 1 display omits a confidence term. The Chapter 3 complement argument is specified here as the two-sided derivation; the one-sided source theorem is not treated as two-sided. For contraction, the source interface is Mohri Lemma 5.7, *Foundations of Machine Learning*, physical PDF pp. 93–94, as checked in the Category 1 audit; use the matching one-sided normalization.

### ML-05 — Algorithmic stability and generalization

**Pages:** algorithmic-stability-and-generalization / algorithmic-stability-and-generalization-examples. **Scope:** Use Bousquet–Elisseeff's deletion convention: a permutation-invariant algorithm $A$ has uniform stability $\beta_m$ if $\sup_{S\in\mathcal Z^m,\,1\le i\le m,\,z\in\mathcal Z}|\ell(A_S,z)-\ell(A_{S\setminus i},z)|\le\beta_m$, where $S\setminus i$ deletes the indexed observation $i$ and the sequence $\beta_m$ is nonincreasing in $m$. Define $R_{\rm loo}(A,S)=m^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^m\ell(A_{S\setminus i},Z_i)$. This is not replace-one stability; deletion stability implies a replace-one loss change at most $2\beta_m$. For iid $S$ with bounded losses, the expected gap obeys $E_S[R(A_S)-\widehat R_S(A_S)]\le2\beta_m$. Assume the Axiom of Choice (AC) for the following high-probability bounds, as required by the reused McDiarmid supplier. If $0\le\ell(A_S,z)\le M$ for every $S,z$, then for each $\delta\in(0,1)$ the separate high-probability bounds are $R(A_S)\le\widehat R_S(A_S)+2\beta_m+(4m\beta_m+M)\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/(2m)}$ and $R(A_S)\le R_{\rm loo}(A_S)+\beta_m+(4m\beta_m+M)\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/(2m)}$, each with probability at least $1-\delta$. For fixed $\lambda>0$, define the RKHS regularized ERM as the attained minimizer of $m^{-1}\sum_i\ell(f,z_i)+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal H}^2$; if $K(x,x)\le\kappa^2$ and $\ell$ is convex and $\sigma$-admissible, meaning $|\ell(f,(x,y))-\ell(g,(x,y))|\le\sigma|f(x)-g(x)|$, then $\beta_m\le\sigma^2\kappa^2/(2\lambda m)$. This condition does not itself establish the separate bounded-loss premise. Proposed interfaces: `def-uniform-algorithmic-stability`, `thm-uniform-stability-expected-generalization`, `thm-uniform-stability-high-probability-generalization`, `thm-strongly-convex-regularized-erm-stability`. **Proof route:** the expected-gap identity replaces a deleted training example by an independent test point; bound the loss change by $\beta_m$ or $2\beta_m$ according to the identity used. For the tail bound, compare delete-one changes of $R$ and empirical risk, then apply bounded differences with coordinate oscillation $4\beta_m+M/m$. **Examples:** strongly convex regularized loss, unstable nearest-neighbor classifier, stability versus training accuracy. **Requires:** ML-01 risk and iid notation; ML-04 only for comparison; MSF-18 — Concentration (`thm-mcdiarmid-bounded-differences`). **Sources:** Bousquet and Elisseeff, “Stability and Generalization,” JMLR 2 (2002), full paper and Appendix proofs, Definitions 6 and 8, Lemma 7, Theorem 12 (printed pp. 504–508), Theorem 22 and Lemma 23 (pp. 514–516). Use this deletion convention and its constants consistently; optimization convergence alone does not imply stability.

### ML-06 — Sample compression and PAC-Bayes bounds

**Pages:** compression-and-pac-bayes-generalization / compression-and-pac-bayes-generalization-examples. **Scope:** Define a deterministic compression map that selects at most $d$ labeled sample entries and a fixed reconstruction map returning any binary hypothesis (not necessarily in $H$); a consistent compression learner must reproduce all sample labels. For a fixed target concept $c\in H$, domain law $D$, iid labeled sample $Z_i=(X_i,c(X_i))$ with $X_i\sim D$, integer $m>d$, $\epsilon\in(0,1)$, and no extra side information, state $\Pr_{D^m}\{\text{some index subset }I\subseteq[m], |I|\le d,\text{ has }R_D(g(Z_I))>\epsilon\text{ while }g(Z_I)\text{ is consistent on the full sample}\}\le\sum_{i=0}^d\binom mi(1-\epsilon)^{m-i}$. Derive sample-size consequences from this exact union bound, not from OCR-corrupted displays. Separately let $(\Theta,\mathcal A)$ be measurable, $\pi$ a fixed prior independent of the iid sample, $\ell:\Theta\times\mathcal Z\to[0,C]$ measurable, $R(\theta)=E_D\ell(\theta,Z)$, and $\widehat R_S(\theta)=m^{-1}\sum_i\ell(\theta,Z_i)$. For each fixed $\lambda>0$ and $\delta\in(0,1)$, with probability at least $1-\delta$ simultaneously for every posterior probability $\rho\ll\pi$, $E_\rho R\le E_\rho\widehat R_S+\lambda C^2/(8m)+(\mathrm{KL}(\rho\|\pi)+\log(1/\delta))/\lambda$. The bounded loss makes both risks defined; infinite KL gives a vacuous bound. A finite data-independent $\lambda$-grid adds $\log|\Lambda|/\lambda$ for each candidate by a union bound. This controls posterior-average risk, not every posterior draw. Proposed interfaces: `def-sample-compression-scheme`, `thm-compression-generalization-bound`, `def-pac-bayes-prior-and-posterior`, `thm-pac-bayes-kl-bound`, `cor-pac-bayes-risk-bound`. **Proof route:** count each labeled compression subset once and union-bound its consistency event; PAC-Bayes integrates Hoeffding's exponential moment under $\pi$, applies Markov and the Donsker–Varadhan variational formula. The 1995 source does not prove size-$O(d)$ compression for every VC-$d$ class, and its question is not a current-status source. **Examples:** finite-support compressed classifier, posterior concentrated on one hypothesis, data-dependent prior violating independence. **Requires:** ML-01 iid/risk notation; MSF-18 — Concentration; measurable product integration. **Sources:** Floyd and Warmuth, “Sample Compression, Learnability, and the Vapnik–Chervonenkis Dimension,” Machine Learning 21 (1995), selected §§3–7 and Appendix proof of Theorem 5, physical PDF pp. 6–15, 17–30 and 34–35; its OCR damages some displays, so the stated formula is rederived from the proof. Alquier, *User-friendly Introduction to PAC-Bayes Bounds*, Chapter 2 §§2.1–2.4, PDF physical pp. 20–39, including complete Donsker–Varadhan and Catoni Theorem 2.1 proofs.

### ML-07 — Calibration, proper losses, and surrogate classification

**Pages:** calibration-proper-losses-and-surrogate-classification / calibration-proper-losses-and-surrogate-classification-examples. **Scope:** For finite labels $Y\in\{1,\ldots,K\}$, define full reliability by $P(Y\in\cdot\mid g(X))=g(X)$, classwise calibration by $P(Y=y\mid g_y(X))=g_y(X)$, and top-confidence calibration by $P(Y=\arg\max g(X)\mid\max g(X))=\max g(X)$; resolve argmax ties by the smallest class index. For $K\ge3$ these notions differ. Any measurable feature $h(X)$ induces the calibrated forecast $P(Y\in\cdot\mid h(X))$, including an uninformative constant forecast. Define strictly proper scoring rules on the finite probability simplex; log and Brier scores are strictly proper. For the binary surrogate theorem, take $Y\in\{-1,+1\}$, $\eta(x)=P(Y=1\mid X=x)$, convex nondecreasing margin loss $\Phi$, conditional risk $L_\Phi(x,u)=\eta(x)\Phi(-u)+(1-\eta(x))\Phi(u)$, and a measurable extended-real conditional minimizer $h_\Phi^*(x)\in\arg\min_{u\in[-\infty,+\infty]}L_\Phi(x,u)$. Define $R_{01}(h)=P(Yh(X)\le0)$ (zero score counts as error), $h^*(x)=\eta(x)-1/2$, and $L_\Phi^*=E[L_\Phi(X,h_\Phi^*(X))]$. If some $s\ge1,c>0$ satisfy $|h^*(x)|^s\le c[L_\Phi(x,0)-L_\Phi(x,h_\Phi^*(x))]$ for all $x$, then every measurable score $h$ with finite surrogate risk satisfies $R_{01}(h)-R_{01}^*\le2c[L_\Phi(h)-L_\Phi^*]^{1/s}$. For calibration estimation, state Vaicenavicius et al.'s exact conditions: fixed-bin underestimation uses a finite data-independent partition and continuous convex discrepancy; the refining-partition a.s. consistency theorem requires Lipschitz calibration function, discrepancy continuous and uniformly continuous in its first argument, and finite data-independent partitions whose mesh tends to zero, with $n\to\infty$ taken before mesh refinement. Fixed-bin ECE is not universally consistent. Proposed interfaces: `def-probability-calibration`, `def-proper-scoring-rule`, `thm-log-and-brier-strict-propriety`, `def-classification-calibration-of-a-surrogate`, `thm-surrogate-regret-controls-classification-regret`. **Proof route:** conditional expectations establish calibration; strict propriety follows by pointwise minimization of expected score; surrogate regret follows from the pointwise margin inequality and Jensen; calibration estimator claims use their partition limits in the stated order. **Examples:** distinct multiclass calibration tables, constant prior forecast, fixed-bin underestimation, a calibrated risk score with low informativeness. **Requires:** ML-01 risk/conditional-law hypotheses; MSF-03 risk and Bayes-rule interfaces; `thm-existence-of-regular-conditional-distributions-for-standard-borel-targets` (AC) if an RCD is used rather than finite-label conditional expectations. **Sources:** Vaicenavicius et al., “Evaluating Model Calibration in Classification,” PMLR 89 (2019), complete 9-page paper and 10-page supplement, Definitions 1–3, Propositions 1–2, Theorems 1–2 and Appendix A.2 proofs; Gneiting and Raftery, “Strictly Proper Scoring Rules, Prediction, and Estimation,” JASA 102(477) (2007), selected §§1, 2.1–2.2, 3.1–3.2 and Theorems 1–3, PDF pp. 1–7 of 20; Mohri et al., Chapter 4, Theorem 4.7 and proof, physical PDF pp. 93–94. Gneiting–Raftery pp. 8–20 are not relied on.

### ML-08 — Minimax learning lower bounds and optimal rates

**Pages:** minimax-learning-lower-bounds-and-optimal-rates / minimax-learning-lower-bounds-and-optimal-rates-examples. **Scope:** Define realizable and agnostic binary-class minimax excess risk separately; for the expectation version use $\inf_A\sup_D E_S[R_D(A(S))-R_D^*]$, and state high-probability lower bounds as such. For VC dimension $d\ge2$, the repaired realizable construction gives: for every learner $A$ and integer $m\ge\lceil(d-1)/4\rceil$, some distribution $D$ supported on a shattered $d$-point set and target $f\in H$ satisfy $P_{S\sim D^m}[R_D(A(S),f)>(d-1)/(32m)]\ge1/100$. The range ensures the source atom $1-8\epsilon$ is nonnegative for $\epsilon=(d-1)/(32m)$.

For the agnostic case, retain and complete the source’s exact conclusion only with $d\ge2$ and integer $m\ge\lceil64d/5\rceil$: for every learner there is a binary distribution $D$ with $P_{S\sim D^m}[R_D(A(S))-\inf_{h\in H}R_D(h)>\sqrt{d/(320m)}]\ge1/64$. Here $\sqrt{d/(320m)}\le1/64$. Close the source’s omitted Exercise D.3 locally by adding a two-point lemma: for $0<u<1$, let $P_\pm=\operatorname{Bern}(1/2\pm u/2)$; for any randomized test based on $k\ge1$ iid observations deciding between equally likely $P_-^k$ and $P_+^k$, its average error is at least $\frac12[1-\sqrt{1-(1-u^2)^k}]\ge2\Phi(2\lceil k/2\rceil,u)$, where $\Phi(t,u)=\frac14[1-\sqrt{1-\exp(-tu^2/(1-u^2))}]$. At $k=0$ the error is $1/2$. Prove the bound from the single-observation affinity $\sqrt{1-u^2}$, product affinity, Cauchy–Schwarz $\operatorname{TV}(P,Q)\le\sqrt{1-\operatorname{aff}(P,Q)^2}$, equal-prior optimal testing error $(1-\operatorname{TV})/2$, and $\log(1-u^2)\ge-u^2/(1-u^2)$. For $c=u^2/(1-u^2)$ and $q=e^{-ct}$, verify $\Phi'(t,u)=-cq/(8\sqrt{1-q})<0$ and $\Phi''(t,u)=c^2q(2-q)/(16(1-q)^{3/2})>0$ for $t>0$; continuity gives convexity at $t=0$. Thus source’s $\Phi(N_i+1,u)$ bounds are valid.

Complete the agnostic product-prior route, not merely a reference to the source: choose shattered points $x_1,\ldots,x_d$ and $σ\in\{-1,+1\}^d$ uniformly; define $D_\sigma(X=x_i)=1/d$ and $P(Y=1\mid X=x_i)=1/2+\sigma_i a/2$. Its Bayes labeling is in $H$, and the excess risk is $a/d$ times the number of coordinate mistakes. Conditional on the count $N_i$ of appearances of $x_i$ and all other coordinates, the learner’s prediction at $x_i$ is a randomized test for $\sigma_i$; the two-point lemma bounds its error by $\Phi(N_i+1,a)$; for $N_i=0$, use the no-data error $1/2\ge\Phi(1,a)$. Since $N_i\sim\operatorname{Bin}(m,1/d)$, Jensen and convexity give average normalized excess strictly above $v=\Phi(m/d+1,a)$. Select a sign vector with this expected excess. Add and prove the elementary bounded-variable lemma: if $Z\in[0,1]$ then $P(Z>t)\ge(EZ-t)/(1-t)$ for $0\le t<1$, by splitting the expectation on $Z\le t$ and $Z>t$. Set confidence $\delta=1/64$, $\gamma=1-8\delta=7/8$, and $a=8r/(1-8\delta)=64r/7$ for target excess $r=\sqrt{d/(320m)}$; the sample range gives $r\le1/64$ and $a\le1/7$. The source calculation $v\ge1/8$ follows from $m/d\le((1/a^2)-1)\log(4/3)-1$ and $\omega=(48\log(4/3)-1)/4096>1/320$ (verify the last strict inequality, e.g. by the alternating-series lower bound $\log(4/3)>23/80$). This yields the stated probability and target threshold with the strict event. Keep these as separate lemma/theorem items so no source exercise is treated as proof. The bounds imply the familiar $\Omega(d/r)$ realizable and $\Omega(d/r^2)$ agnostic sample lower rates in these ranges, not an efficient-estimator claim.

**Proposed interfaces:** `def-minimax-excess-risk`; `thm-realizable-vc-minimax-lower-bound`; `lem-bernoulli-two-point-testing-for-vc-lower-bound`; `lem-bounded-variable-lower-tail-from-mean`; `thm-agnostic-vc-minimax-lower-bound`; `cor-vc-minimax-rate-match`. **Proof route:** Use the published finite trace-counting supplier only after restricting to indexed shattered sample coordinates; prove the realizable unseen-atom/Chernoff argument under its repaired range. For the agnostic item author the local affinity/TV test lemma, coordinate-count conditioning, Jensen step, prior-realization choice, bounded-variable lemma, and exact $\delta,\gamma,a$ arithmetic above. MSF-21 testing interfaces may be reused only if their actual statements give these exact constants/ranges. **Examples:** Bernoulli two-point testing, finite shattered set, and fixed-confidence lower bounds. **Requires:** ML-01 iid binary risk setup; published finite-family shattering/Sauer only for the finite trace; MSF-21 (`thm-pinsker`, `thm-two-point-le-cam-risk-bound`, `thm-fano-packing`, `thm-assouad-hypercube`) only if the tested-hypothesis and divergence assumptions match. **Sources:** Mohri et al., *Foundations of Machine Learning*, 2e, Chapter 3 Theorem 3.20/proof, physical PDF pp. 60–62 (printed pp. 43–45); Lemma 3.21 and its explicit Exercise D.3 gap, physical p. 62 (printed p. 45); Lemma 3.22 and Theorem 3.23/proof, physical pp. 63–65 (printed pp. 46–48). The local test proof above fills Exercise D.3; the source proof text supplies the remaining product-prior route but must be reauthored and checked with these stated ranges/constants.

### ML-09 — Distribution shift and domain adaptation

**Pages:** distribution-shift-and-domain-adaptation / distribution-shift-and-domain-adaptation-examples. **Scope:** Define source/target joint laws and risks. For binary class $H$, define $d_{H\Delta H}(D_S,D_T)=2\sup_{h,h'\in H}|P_{D_S}(h\ne h')-P_{D_T}(h\ne h')|$; if source and target have labeling functions $f_S,f_T$, let $\lambda=\inf_{h'\in H}(\epsilon_S(h')+\epsilon_T(h'))$. Then every $h\in H$ obeys $\epsilon_T(h)\le\epsilon_S(h)+\tfrac12d_{H\Delta H}(D_S,D_T)+\lambda$; this joint-labeling term is essential. For arbitrary loss $L$, define $L_P(h^\prime,h)=E_{X\sim P_X}[L(h^\prime(X),h(X))]$ and $\mathrm{disc}_L(P,Q)=\sup_{h,h'\in H}|L_P(h',h)-L_Q(h',h)|$. If $L$ is symmetric and satisfies the triangle inequality, and the source/target minimizers $h_P^*,h_Q^*$ for the respective labeling functions exist, state the exact bound $L_P(h,f_P)\le L_P(h_P^*,f_P)+L_Q(h,h_Q^*)+\mathrm{disc}_L(P,Q)+\min\{L_Q(h_Q^*,h_P^*),L_P(h_Q^*,h_P^*)\}$. Under covariate shift require $P_T(Y\mid X)=P_S(Y\mid X)$ on target support and $P_T^X\ll P_S^X$; with $w=dP_T^X/dP_S^X$, $R_T(h)=E_S[w(X)\ell(h(X),Y)]$ whenever the weighted loss is integrable. Bounded weights or another moment condition are needed for finite-sample concentration. Proposed interfaces: `def-source-target-distributions-and-shift`, `def-h-delta-h-divergence`, `def-loss-discrepancy-between-domains`, `thm-covariate-shift-importance-weighting`, `thm-target-risk-bound-by-source-risk-divergence-and-joint-error`, `thm-discrepancy-domain-adaptation-bound`. **Proof route:** Radon–Nikodym change of measure proves the covariate-shift identity; insert a source/target near-minimizer, use the triangle inequality, and control disagreement/discrepancy terms for the transfer inequalities. Empirical discrepancy estimation is a separate theorem requiring bounded-loss/complexity assumptions; small unlabeled divergence alone gives no target guarantee if $\lambda$ is large. **Examples:** support mismatch, unbounded importance weights, label shift despite identical covariate laws. **Requires:** ML-01; ML-04 for finite-sample complexity; MSF-04 — Domination and Likelihood Ratios (`def-likelihood-density`, `thm-likelihood-ratio-change-of-measure`); published Radon–Nikodym theorem (AC and a common finite exhaustion; probability measures satisfy the exhaustion condition). **Sources:** Ben-David et al., “A Theory of Learning from Different Domains,” Machine Learning 79 (2010), complete 25-page article, Definitions 1–2, Theorems 2–3 and Appendix proofs; Mansour, Mohri, and Rostamizadeh, “Domain Adaptation: Learning Bounds and Algorithms,” arXiv:0902.3430v3 (2023), physical pp. 1–11, §§1–5, Theorem 2 proof, Theorems 8–13 and 14, Proposition 5 and Appendices A–C. Empirical figures on pp. 12–15 are not used.

### ML-10 — Adversarial robustness and robust risk

**Pages:** adversarial-robustness-and-robust-risk / adversarial-robustness-and-robust-risk-examples. **Scope:** Define a measurable threat correspondence $\Delta(x)$ and pointwise robust loss $\sup_{\delta\in\Delta(x)}\ell(f(x+\delta),y)$; require the supremum to be measurable (or state outer risk). In Schmidt et al.'s Gaussian model, $Y$ is uniform on $\{-1,+1\}$, $X|Y=y\sim N(y\theta^*,\sigma^2I_d)$ and $\|\theta^*\|_2=\sqrt d$. Corollary 22 assumes $d\ge2$ and $0<\sigma\le d^{1/4}/32$; with probability at least $1-2\exp[-d/(8(\sigma^2+1))]$, the class-weighted sample-mean classifier has $\ell_\infty^\epsilon$ robust error at most $1\%$ from one sample when $0\le\epsilon\le\tfrac14d^{-1/4}$, and when $\tfrac14d^{-1/4}\le\epsilon\le\tfrac14$ it suffices that $n\ge64\epsilon^2\sqrt d$. For d≥2 and 0<β<1, Corollary 19 separately gives one-sample natural error at most $\beta$ with probability at least $1-2\exp[-d/(8(\sigma^2+1))]$ when $\sigma\le d^{1/4}/(5\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)})$. For d≥2 and σ>0, the Gaussian lower bound says any learner from $n\ge0$ samples, with $\theta\sim N(0,I_d)$, has expected robust error at least $(1-1/d)/2$ for $\epsilon\ge0$ if $n\le\epsilon^2\sigma^2/(8\log d)$; taking $\sigma=c_1d^{1/4}$ gives the stated $\epsilon^2\sqrt d/\log d$ lower scale. In the separate Bernoulli model $\theta^*\in\{-1,+1\}^d$, coordinates are label-correlated with probability $1/2+\tau$. Theorem 31 assumes integer d≥1, uniform θ*∈{−1,+1}^d, 0<τ≤1/4, 0≤ε<3τ, 0<γ<1/2, and n≤ε²γ²/[5000τ⁴ log(4d/γ)]; every linear-classifier learner then has expected robust error at least $1/2-\gamma$. Coordinatewise thresholding $T(x)_i=\operatorname{sign}(x_i)$ is invariant to the source's $\ell_\infty$ perturbations for $\epsilon<1$; Theorem 10 gives its associated nonlinear classifier robust error at most $1\%$ from one sample when $c d^{-1/4}\le\tau\le1/2$. These are model- and architecture-specific, not distribution-free. Proposed interfaces: `def-adversarial-threat-set-and-robust-risk`; `alg-gaussian-mixture-robust-mean-classifier`; `thm-robust-sample-upper-bound-gaussian-mixture`; `thm-robust-sample-lower-bound-gaussian-mixture`; `alg-coordinate-threshold-preprocessing`; `thm-architecture-dependent-robustness-binary-feature-model`; `lem-gaussian-vector-norm-concentration`. **Proof route:** follow Gaussian likelihood/posterior and common-perturbation arguments; prove the needed Gaussian-vector maximum tail from the chi-square mgf and Chernoff optimization, while keeping the already published Gaussian concentration assumptions exact. For the Bernoulli model, verify sign invariance coordinatewise and reproduce Theorem 31's linear-classifier reduction. **Examples:** Gaussian robust-risk scaling with dimension and radius; Bernoulli perturbations preserving signs; natural versus robust risk on the same model. **Requires:** ML-01; ML-02 for sample-complexity notation; published `def-multivariate-normal-law` (singular covariance allowed; its construction assumes AC), expectation, and concentration interfaces; carry `def-axiom-of-choice` in new theorem dependencies that use this construction. The Gaussian norm tail must be supplied or proved locally. Do not import a generic VC/Rademacher robust-risk theorem. **Sources:** Schmidt et al., “Adversarially Robust Generalization Requires More Data,” arXiv:1804.11285v2 / NeurIPS 2018, full main text pp. 1–16 and Appendices A–B, pp. 22–38; exact results from Theorems 4–5, Corollaries 19, 22–23, and Theorems 8, 10, 31. The source's image experiments are not used.

### ML-11 — Fairness criteria, impossibility boundaries, and limits

**Pages:** fairness-criteria-and-impossibility-boundaries / fairness-criteria-and-impossibility-boundaries-examples. **Scope:** Define Kleinberg–Mullainathan–Raghavan's exact model with two groups, binary outcome, and possibly randomized assignment of each feature vector to a score bin in $[0,1]$: calibration within each group and equal mean assigned score across groups conditional separately on positive and negative outcomes. These balance means require $0<\mu_t<N_t$ for both groups. Under this nondegeneracy premise Theorem 1.1 says simultaneous satisfaction requires equal group base rates or perfect prediction (scores 0 or 1 only). Do not generalize to demographic parity, equalized odds, individual/causal fairness, or all policies. Proposed interfaces: def-group-risk-score-and-calibration; def-positive-and-negative-score-balance; thm-calibration-and-balance-incompatibility-for-risk-scores. **Proof route:** condition on group and outcome, express false-positive/true-positive rates through the score distributions, and derive the incompatibility algebra under unequal base rates and nonperfect prediction. **Examples:** equal base rates exception, perfect prediction exception, metric changing with threshold. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; ML-07 — Calibration, proper losses, and surrogate classification. No finite-sample inference or other fairness criterion is included. **Sources:** Kleinberg, Mullainathan, and Raghavan, “Inherent Trade-Offs in the Fair Determination of Risk Scores,” arXiv:1609.05807v2 (2016), complete 23-page article and proofs, especially Definitions 1–3 and Theorems 1.1–1.2. The exact incompatibility concerns the paper’s named calibration and positive/negative balance criteria: simultaneous satisfaction is possible only for equal group base rates or perfect scores. It is not a theorem about all fairness notions, policies, distributions, or causal criteria. Approximate Theorem 1.2 should be stated only with the source’s exact approximation definitions and formula.

### ML-12 — Linear prediction, shrinkage, and sparse regression

**Pages:** ml02-linear-sparse-models / ml02-linear-sparse-models-examples. **Scope:** Define a fixed real design matrix $X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}$, response $y$, empirical squared loss $\|y-X\beta\|_2^2/(2n)$, the full least-squares minimizer set and normal equations, ridge/Tikhonov with $\lambda>0$, and lasso with its exact penalty normalization. OLS coefficients are unique iff $X$ has full column rank; ridge is uniquely minimized at $(X^TX+\lambda I)^{-1}X^Ty$. For $X^TX=I_p$, lasso is coordinatewise soft-thresholding of $X^Ty$ at $n\lambda$ under this $1/(2n)$ convention. Define group/fused penalties as objectives only unless their own existence/algorithm guarantees are proved; explain feature scaling and keep any intercept unpenalized by separating its coordinate. Distinguish these deterministic optimization contracts from the following refuted random-design asymptotic source claim.

The following is Wainwright’s exact original source claim, retained for its two proved refutations below. For this claim, let $S=\{j:\beta_j^*\ne0\}$, $s=|S|$, $\rho_n=\min_{j\in S}|\beta_j^*|$, and use $\widehat\beta\in\arg\min_\beta\{(2n)^{-1}\|y-X\beta\|_2^2+\lambda_n\|\beta\|_1\}$. Define success as existence of an optimizer with $\operatorname{sgn}(\widehat\beta)=\operatorname{sgn}(\beta^*)$. Along a sequence with $s,n-s,p-s\to\infty$, assume $\Sigma$ has unit diagonal, $\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{SS})\ge C_{\min}>0$, $\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma)\le C_{\max}<\infty$, $\|(\Sigma_{SS})^{-1}\|_\infty\le D_{\max}<\infty$, and $\|\Sigma_{S^cS}(\Sigma_{SS})^{-1}\|_\infty\le1-\epsilon$ for fixed $0<\epsilon\le1$. Independently across observations, $x_i\sim N_p(0,\Sigma)$ and $w_i\sim N(0,\sigma^2)$, with design and noise independent, and $y_i=x_i^T\beta^*+w_i$. Set
$$\theta_\ell=\frac{\bigl(\sqrt{C_{\max}}-\sqrt{C_{\max}-1/C_{\max}}\bigr)^2}{C_{\max}(2-\epsilon)^2},\qquad \theta_u=\frac{C_{\max}}{\epsilon^2 C_{\min}}.$$
For every fixed $\nu>0$, if $n<2(\theta_\ell-\nu)s\log(p-s)+s+1$, the success probability tends to zero for every positive non-increasing $\lambda_n$. If $n>2(\theta_u+\nu)s\log(p-s)+s$ and $\lambda_n\to0$, $n\lambda_n^2/\log(p-s)\to\infty$, and $\rho_n^{-1}(\lambda_n+\sqrt{\log s/n})\to0$, it tends to one. For $\Sigma=I$, both thresholds equal one. The Wainwright model has no separate intercept. This is signed exact-support recovery, not a prediction-risk guarantee.

**Proof route:** Reuse the published normal-equation, SVD, and Tikhonov contracts. Prove orthonormal soft thresholding by minimizing each scalar absolute-value objective. Preserve the complete original Wainwright two-sided formulation above as a false claim: prove the upper active inverse-Gram fluctuation counterexample and the lower paired-covariance success counterexample locally, checking all original dimensional, penalty and beta-min conditions, and the exact existence-of-a-correct-signed-optimizer event. The lower witness uses delta=epsilon=1e-6, full Cmax=3, s=N^3, n=s+ceil(.001s log N), lambda_n=1, and beta_active=n; it succeeds below the claimed failure threshold. The upper proof keeps its conditional H,d event separate from active-Gram control. Do not replace the false source statement silently by the journal version’s distinct increment-variance threshold. ESL leaves Gauss–Markov as an exercise; include it only with a separate local covariance proof.

**Examples:** Correlated predictors, orthonormal design, and failure of exact recovery when beta-min is too small.

**Requires:** published `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation` (any rank; minimizers differ by the kernel), `def-tikhonov-regularised-least-squares`, `thm-tikhonov-regularised-least-squares-has-a-unique-minimiser` (finite real/complex matrix, $\lambda>0$), and `thm-singular-value-decomposition`. Any excess-risk or generalization claim consumes an exact Cat1 theorem and its assumptions. **Sources:** [ESL] Chapter 3 §§3.2–3.9, printed pp. 43–100, and §18.4, pp. 661–667; [WAIN] §§2.2–2.3, 3.1–3.4, printed pp. 5–15, especially Theorem 1 and its proof. The ESL Gauss–Markov argument is explicitly an exercise.

### ML-13 — Linear classifiers, discriminants, and logistic models

**Pages:** ml02-linear-classification / ml02-linear-classification-examples. **Scope:** Define binary and multiclass linear-score classifiers with an explicit deterministic label tie rule; indicator-response least-squares classification; LDA with class-conditional $N(\mu_k,\Sigma)$ densities, common positive-definite covariance, and positive class priors; QDA with positive-definite class-specific covariances; binary logistic $P(Y=1\mid x)=\{1+\exp[-(\beta_0+x^T\beta)]\}^{-1}$ and multiclass softmax linear-logit models; L1-penalized likelihood $-\ell(\beta)+\lambda\|\beta\|_1$ with an explicitly excluded intercept when desired; and the perceptron. Expanding Gaussian log densities gives affine LDA log-posterior scores only under the common covariance premise, while QDA scores are quadratic. Logistic regression is conditional and assumes no Gaussian feature law. With augmented design $X$ (including the intercept), state $\ell(\beta)=\sum_i[y_i x_i^T\beta-\log(1+e^{x_i^T\beta})]$, whose Hessian is $-X^TWX$ for $W_{ii}=p_i(1-p_i)$. When $X$ has full column rank and the current coefficients are finite, $W\succ0$; the exact Newton/IRLS step is $z=X\beta+W^{-1}(y-p)$ and $\beta_{new}=(X^TWX)^{-1}X^TWz$. This algebra does not promise iterative convergence. Under complete separation, the unpenalized likelihood supremum need not be attained at a finite coefficient. Define perceptron updates on augmented inputs $\tilde x_i=(x_i,1)$: in a fixed cyclic order, update $w\leftarrow w+y_i\tilde x_i$ whenever $y_iw^T\tilde x_i\le0$. If a finite sample has a unit separator with margin $\gamma>0$ and $\|\tilde x_i\|\le R$, prove termination after at most $R^2/\gamma^2$ mistakes; no convergence is asserted for nonseparable data, where cycles can occur.

**Proof route:** Derive the LDA/QDA scores by density expansion; derive logistic gradient and Hessian and solve the weighted least-squares Newton equation under full rank. For perceptron, include the complete progress/norm-squared mistake-bound proof, not the source’s exercise as evidence. State separation and nonattainment by an explicit diverging coefficient sequence. Avoid coefficient-equivalence claims between LDA and logistic outside separately stated model/estimation conditions.

**Examples:** Generative LDA and conditional logistic boundaries; a separated sample with diverging logistic coefficients; a nonseparable perceptron cycle.

**Requires:** published `thm-hessian-characterises-convexity`, `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation`, and `thm-singular-value-decomposition`, with their finite-dimensional hypotheses. Logistic MLE existence and asymptotic confidence remain separate results. **Sources:** [ESL] Chapter 4 §§4.1–4.5, printed pp. 101–135, especially perceptron update (4.44) and Exercise 4.6; §18.4, pp. 661–664. Exercise 4.6 is not treated as a completed proof.

### ML-14 — RKHS regularization, kernels, and support-vector machines

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** The published `thm-slater-strong-duality-and-dual-attainment` and `thm-convex-kkt-sufficiency-and-normal-cone-certificate` now replace the historical missing-duality assertion. Reduce first to the finite kernel-section span, establish primal attainment (coercive in the penalized finite-dimensional weight; treat all-one-class/unpenalized-bias boundary separately), independent equality rows in the dual formulation, and the stated relative-interior Slater premise. Strict feasibility alone does not establish primal attainment. RKHS construction/representer proofs remain local.

**Pages:** ml02-rkhs-svm / ml02-rkhs-svm-examples. **Scope:** Define a real symmetric positive-semidefinite kernel $k$ by nonnegative finite Gram quadratic forms and define its RKHS as the completion of the kernel-section span after quotienting zero-norm vectors; prove the reproducing identity. State the finite-sample representer result for $J(f)=L(f(x_1),\ldots,f(x_n))+\Omega(\|f\|_{\mathcal H})$ with nondecreasing $\Omega$: whenever a minimizer exists, projecting onto $\operatorname{span}\{k(\cdot,x_i)\}$ preserves evaluations and cannot increase the objective, so at least one minimizer has the finite kernel expansion. Do not claim existence without separate hypotheses.

For labels $y_i\in\{-1,+1\}$ and feature map $\phi$ into a real Hilbert space, define soft-margin SVM for $C>0$ by minimizing $\frac12\|w\|^2+C\sum_i\xi_i$ over $w,b,\xi$ subject to $y_i(\langle w,\phi(x_i)\rangle+b)\ge1-\xi_i$ and $\xi_i\ge0$. The dual is maximize $\sum_i\alpha_i-\frac12\sum_{ij}\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_jk(x_i,x_j)$ subject to $0\le\alpha_i\le C$ and $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i=0$. State KKT stationarity $w=\sum_i\alpha_i y_i\phi(x_i)$, complementarity for both inequality families, and the bias condition; the score is not a calibrated probability. A hard-margin version requires explicit sample separability. The bias is unpenalized throughout.

**Proof route:** Prove kernel/RKHS well-definedness by the finite Gram PSD construction and quotient completion. For representer, use $f=f_\parallel+f_\perp$, with $f_\perp(x_i)=0$ and orthogonal Pythagoras. Reduce the SVM to a finite-dimensional feature span, derive the Lagrangian dual and KKT equations, exhibit strict feasibility (choose $w=b=0$ and $\xi_i>1$), and invoke/prove finite-dimensional convex Slater duality. Convexity is global for the optimization problem; numerical solver convergence is separate.

**Examples:** Linear, polynomial and Gaussian radial kernels; separable and overlapping samples; margin score versus logistic probability.

**Requires:** published `thm-orthogonal-projection-is-the-unique-nearest-point` and `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` only for their exact finite-dimensional uses. The library still needs the local RKHS/general representer construction; convex-duality/KKT now use the published interfaces identified above, without making Cat3 depend on ML. Cat1 margin/generalization results require their own norm and distribution assumptions. **Sources:** [ESL] §5.8, printed pp. 167–173; Chapter 12 §§12.2–12.4, pp. 417–438; Chapter 18 §18.5, pp. 668–672; [GPML] Chapter 6 §§6.1–6.4, pp. 129–150. ESL Exercise 5.15 and GPML’s cited theorem do not replace the local representer proof.

### ML-15 — Gaussian-process regression and prediction

**Pages:** ml02-gaussian-process-regression / ml02-gaussian-process-regression-examples. **Scope:** Define a GP prior by mean $m$ and a symmetric PSD covariance kernel $k$, equivalently consistent finite-dimensional Gaussian laws. For a finite observation vector at $X$, $Y=f(X)+\varepsilon$ with independent $f\sim GP(m,k)$ and $\varepsilon\sim N(0,R)$, where $R\succeq0$, let $A=K_{XX}+R$ and require exactly $A\succ0$ for the standard inverse formulas. For a finite test vector $f_*=f(X_*)$, state its conditional Gaussian law given $Y=y$ as mean $m_*+K_{*X}A^{-1}(y-m_X)$ and covariance $K_{**}-K_{*X}A^{-1}K_{X*}$; this covariance may be singular. Distinguish prediction of the latent $f_*$ from noisy future observations, whose covariance also includes their noise covariance. No sample-path continuity follows from the GP definition or Kolmogorov extension. For $R=\sigma^2I$ and zero prior mean, the KRR minimizer of $\sum_i(y_i-g(x_i))^2+\sigma^2\|g\|_{\mathcal H_k}^2$ has the same evaluation vector as the posterior mean; for nonzero mean center the response and penalize $g-m$ when $m\in\mathcal H_k$. Define log marginal likelihood only when its Gaussian density is nonsingular; hyperparameter optimization is generally nonconvex. Dense Cholesky uses cubic setup and quadratic work per test vector in the arithmetic-operation model, with separate finite-precision/conditioning caveats. Logistic/probit GP classification is approximate, not an exact Gaussian posterior.

**Proof route:** Consume Category 3’s final finite GP posterior/predictive theorem for its stated finite Gaussian contract; locally show $A\succ0$ suffices and derive the KRR identity by the representer reduction/normal equations. The source finite conditioning formulas allow singular predictive covariance. Construct any general GP prior through its consistent finite-dimensional distributions and the published Kolmogorov extension, not the Brownian-only construction; both that construction and `def-multivariate-normal-law` carry AC, so state it for existence/construction uses. Prove explicit kernel PSD claims individually; GPML’s general Bochner/Mercer statements are not local proofs. Treat Cholesky cost as a finite dense linear-algebra operation count, not a numerical-stability guarantee.

**Examples:** Squared-exponential/Matérn kernels only after their PSD contracts are supplied; noisy finite interpolation; positive noise with singular prior Gram matrix; singular conditional test covariance.

**Requires:** consume Cat3 BCS-11 `thm-finite-gaussian-process-posterior-and-predictive-laws` (nonzero mean, PSD prior/noise/predictive covariances, finite evaluations, observed covariance positive definite); published `def-stochastic-process-and-finite-dimensional-distributions`, `def-multivariate-normal-law` (allows singular covariance, assumes AC), `def-gaussian-process`, `thm-kolmogorov-extension-for-standard-borel-coordinate-spaces` (assumes AC), `thm-non-negative-operator-characterisations`, and `thm-singular-value-decomposition`. Coordinate boundary remains Category 4 consumer-only. **Sources:** [GPML] Chapter 2 §§2.1–2.8, printed pp. 7–30; Chapter 4 §§4.1–4.4, pp. 79–102; Chapter 6 §§6.1–6.4, pp. 129–150; [ESL] §5.8, pp. 167–173. These books derive finite Gaussian conditioning but cite rather than prove general kernel construction/representer facts.

### ML-16 — CART trees and pruning

**Pages:** ml02-cart / ml02-cart-examples. **Scope:** On a fixed finite labeled sample in $\mathbb R^p$, define a finite axis-aligned binary tree by splits $x_j\le t$ versus $x_j>t$, retaining only candidate splits with nonempty children; make ties deterministic by a fixed coordinate/threshold order. For regression, a leaf region $R_m$ has count $N_m>0$, mean $\bar y_m$, and impurity $Q_m=N_m^{-1}\sum_{i:x_i\in R_m}(y_i-\bar y_m)^2$. For classification, define empirical class proportions, a deterministic majority-class tie rule, and optional misclassification, Gini, or entropy impurity, using $0\log0=0$. The greedy grower chooses the candidate with smallest child-weighted empirical loss at that node and repeats to a stated stopping rule; its result is not the globally optimal tree. For any subtree $T$ of a fixed finite maximal tree $T_0$, define $R(T)=\sum_{m\in\mathrm{leaves}(T)}N_mQ_m(T)$, terminal-node count $|T|$, and cost-complexity $C_\alpha(T)=R(T)+\alpha|T|$, $\alpha\ge0$. Define weakest-link pruning as repeatedly collapsing a nonterminal node $t$ minimizing $g(t)=[R(t)-R(T_t)]/(|T_t|-1)$, where $R(t)=N_tQ_t$ is the loss if $t$ is collapsed to a leaf and $R(T_t)$ is the loss of its current descendant leaves, with deterministic tie rule; state no claim that the resulting sequence solves every global/pruning/CV problem unless its separate theorem is proved. Choose $\alpha$ by validation/CV only as a data-selection procedure, not a test-risk guarantee. Proposed items: `def-empirical-decision-tree`; `alg-cart-greedy-split`; `def-cost-complexity-pruning`; `alg-weakest-link-pruning`; `thm-node-mean-minimizes-squared-error`.

**Proof route:** For every fixed nonempty leaf, expand $\sum_i(y_i-c)^2=\sum_i(y_i-\bar y)^2+N(c-\bar y)^2$ to prove the mean is the unique minimizing constant. Prove the greedy split is optimal only among the finite listed candidate splits. A finite tree loses at least one internal node at each pruning step and therefore terminates at the root; this proves termination, not global optimality. Specify how empty children and class ties are excluded/resolved.

**Examples:** A one-dimensional threshold tree and two distinct subtrees with the same fit but different leaf penalties.

**Requires:** published `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation` (finite-dimensional least-squares minimizers, any rank) for the squared-loss supplier; finite argmin/tie ordering can be proved locally. Cat1 generalization results require their exact assumptions. **Sources:** ESL Chapter 9 §9.2, printed pp. 305–316 (regression tree, class proportions/impurities, cost-complexity and weakest-link descriptions); Chapter 10 §§10.9–10.12, pp. 353–365, for trees as gradient-boosting base learners. ESL refers elsewhere for the weakest-link optimal-subtree result, which is not imported here.

### ML-17 — Bagging and random forests

**Pages:** ml02-random-forests / ml02-random-forests-examples. **Scope:** Conditional on a fixed training dataset, define randomized tree $h(x,\Theta)$ and construct $B$ trees with iid random seeds/bootstrap count vectors and, when specified, iid nodewise feature-subset randomization; finite-sample votes use a fixed class-label tie rule. Define an out-of-bag prediction only from trees whose bootstrap count for that training observation is zero; it is a diagnostic estimate, not an exact finite-forest identity. For independent test pair $(X,Y)$ and iid tree randomizations independent of it, let $G_B(x)$ be the smallest-index class maximizing the $B$ votes and define conditional finite-forest risk $PE_B(\Theta_{1:B})=P_{X,Y}(G_B(X)\ne Y\mid\Theta_{1:B},\text{training data})$; define limiting margin $m(X,Y)=P_\Theta(h(X,\Theta)=Y)-\max_{j\ne Y}P_\Theta(h(X,\Theta)=j)$. Under $P_{X,Y}(m(X,Y)=0)=0$, dominated convergence gives $PE_B\to P_{X,Y}(m(X,Y)<0)$; with positive mass on zero margin, Breiman’s strict-inequality limit need not equal finite-vote error limit.

State Breiman’s strength/correlation bound only for multiclass $J\ge2$ with $s=E_{X,Y}m(X,Y)>0$: choose $\hat j(X,Y)$ as the smallest-index maximizing wrong class-probability, define $rmg(\Theta,X,Y)=1\{h(X,\Theta)=Y\}-1\{h(X,\Theta)=\hat j(X,Y)\}$ and $sd(\Theta)=\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{X,Y}(rmg(\Theta,X,Y))}$. For independent iid $\Theta,\Theta'$, define $\rho=\frac{E_{\Theta,\Theta'}[\operatorname{Cov}_{X,Y}(rmg_\Theta,rmg_{\Theta'})]}{(E_\Theta sd(\Theta))^2}$ when the denominator is positive; this equals the source’s weighted mean correlation definition. With $PE^*=P_{X,Y}(m(X,Y)<0)$, one has $PE^*\le\rho(1-s^2)/s^2$. If the denominator vanishes, every raw margin is constant in $(X,Y)$ almost surely and positive strength gives $PE^*=0$. Keep regression separate: for square-integrable $Y,h(X,\Theta)$ and every $\Theta$, assume $E_{X,Y}Y=E_Xh(X,\Theta)$, so residuals $e_\Theta=Y-h(X,\Theta)$ are mean zero. Define $\rho=\frac{E_{\Theta,\Theta'}[\operatorname{Cov}_{X,Y}(e_\Theta,e_{\Theta'})]}{(E_\Theta sd(e_\Theta))^2}$ when the denominator is positive; this too equals the source’s weighted mean residual correlation. Define $PE^*(\mathrm{forest})=E_{X,Y}(Y-E_\Theta h(X,\Theta))^2$ and $PE^*(\mathrm{tree})=E_\Theta E_{X,Y}(Y-h(X,\Theta))^2$. Then $PE^*(\mathrm{forest})\le\rho PE^*(\mathrm{tree})$. When the denominator is zero, mean-zero residuals vanish almost surely and both risks are zero. These theorems are conditional on fixed training data and tree-randomization laws, not monotone finite-$B$ improvement or universal learning guarantees. Proposed items: `alg-bagged-random-forest`; `thm-infinite-forest-vote-limit`; `thm-random-forest-strength-correlation-bound`; `thm-regression-forest-risk-under-unbiased-randomized-trees`.

**Proof route:** For each fixed test pair apply the iid strong law to its finitely many class-vote indicators; Fubini then gives almost-sure vote convergence for $P_{X,Y}$-almost every pair. Away from zero margin the finite argmax decision eventually agrees with the limiting correct/incorrect status, and dominated convergence gives the risk limit. For the classifier bound, express $\operatorname{Var}(m)$ as the independent-pair raw-margin covariance, then use Chebyshev, $(E_\Theta sd)^2\le E_\Theta sd^2$, and $|rmg|\le1$ to get $\operatorname{Var}(m)\le\rho(1-s^2)$. For regression, expand forest squared error as the independent-pair residual covariance; the source-defined $\rho$ and $(E sd)^2\le E sd^2=PE^*(\mathrm{tree})$ yield the stated bound. Handle both zero-denominator cases directly.

**Examples:** Strength/correlation tradeoff; an equiprobable two-class vote at a positive-class point gives zero limiting margin and can fluctuate among finite odd-forest votes; OOB estimates with finite-tree variability.

**Requires:** ML-16 for the CART base learner; published `thm-kolmogorov-iid-l1-strong-law` (iid integrable indicators; no rate), `thm-independent-random-elements-have-product-joint-law` (independent randomization/test pair), `thm-fubini-theorem-for-l-one-on-sigma-finite-product-spaces` (integrable product-law functions), `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, and `def-moments-variance-and-covariance` (square-integrable inputs). **Sources:** Breiman, “Random Forests,” §§1–4, Theorems 1.2 and 2.3, printed pp. 1–9; §11, Theorems 11.1–11.2, pp. 24–26; Appendices I–II, pp. 29–33. Inspected the actual strict-margin limit, raw-margin definitions, weighted-correlation formulas, unbiased-residual condition and proofs. ESL Chapter 15 §§15.1–15.4.3, printed pp. 587–602, supplies algorithms/diagnostics, not these probability limits.

### ML-18 — AdaBoost and gradient boosting

**Pages:** ml02-boosting / ml02-boosting-examples. **Scope:** For a fixed binary sample $(x_i,y_i)$ with $y_i,h_m(x_i)\in\{-1,+1\}$, define AdaBoost using $w_i^{(1)}=1/N$ for $N$ training examples and normalized positive weights thereafter. At round $m$, weighted error $e_m=\sum_iw_i^{(m)}1\{h_m(x_i)\ne y_i\}$ must satisfy $0<e_m<1/2$; set $a_m=\frac12\log((1-e_m)/e_m)$, update $w_i\propto w_i^{(m)}e^{-a_my_ih_m(x_i)}$ and renormalize by $Z_m=2\sqrt{e_m(1-e_m)}$, and output $F_M=\sum_{m=1}^M a_mh_m$, predicted by a declared sign/tie rule. If $e_m=0$, stop and output the perfect round classifier with zero training error; do not use the undefined infinite coefficient. The product bound below applies to any finite sequence of rounds with $0<e_m<1/2$. State the empirical nonpositive-margin bound $N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N1\{y_iF_M(x_i)\le0\}\le\prod_{m=1}^MZ_m$ over completed rounds. This is a training-margin bound only, not test generalization. Proposed items: `alg-adaboost-m1`; `thm-adaboost-exponential-training-error-bound`; `alg-gradient-boosting`; `def-negative-gradient-pseudo-residual`.

Define gradient tree boosting on a finite training sample for differentiable per-observation loss $\ell(y,f(x))$: initialize $f_0$ as an attained minimizer of $\sum_i\ell(y_i,c)$ over constants $c$; at each round set pseudo-residuals $r_{im}=-\partial_2\ell(y_i,f_{m-1}(x_i))$, fit a finite regression tree to $(x_i,r_{im})$ by squared error, and let its nonempty terminal regions $R_{jm}$ partition the training observations. In each leaf choose an attained $\gamma_{jm}\in\arg\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb R}\sum_{i:x_i\in R_{jm}}\ell(y_i,f_{m-1}(x_i)+\gamma)$, then update $f_m=f_{m-1}+\sum_j\gamma_{jm}1_{R_{jm}}$. Because zero is an admissible leaf offset, each exact leaf minimization makes empirical loss nonincreasing. Shrinkage and subsampling are separate variants; they inherit no descent or convergence theorem without their own conditions. A regression tree fit to pseudo-residuals approximates the unrestricted negative-gradient vector, not an exact unconstrained gradient step.

**Proof route:** For AdaBoost, induction on unnormalized exponential loss gives $\sum_i e^{-y_iF_M(x_i)}=N\prod_{r\le M}Z_r$; combine it with $1\{yF\le0\}\le e^{-yF}$. Derive $a_m$ and $Z_m$ by minimizing $e_m e^a+(1-e_m)e^{-a}$ over $a\ge0$. For gradient tree boosting, use the chain rule to identify pseudo-residuals with negative empirical gradients, verify the tree partitions training observations into nonempty leaves, and use each leaf’s exact attained line minimizer with zero offset feasible to prove empirical loss nonincrease. Least-squares tree fitting itself is an approximation step; no convergence or generalization follows from this finite-step loss calculation.

**Examples:** Two weak classifiers and a normalized update; a perfect round with $e_m=0$; a tree fit whose pseudo-residual direction is not a descent direction.

**Requires:** ML-16 only when trees are the chosen base learner; finite exponential algebra and published `thm-chain-rule-for-total-derivatives` under its finite-dimensional differentiability premise for gradient statements. Cat1 margin/generalization theorems only under their exact complexity assumptions. **Sources:** ESL Chapter 10 §§10.1–10.6, printed pp. 337–350 (Algorithm 10.1 uses a full-log-weight convention equivalent up to the common positive factor to this half-log coefficient convention); §§10.9–10.12, pp. 353–365, for gradient/pseudo-residual tree fitting; Chapter 16 §§16.2–16.3, pp. 607–623. Empirical robustness is not theorem evidence.

### ML-19 — K-means, Gaussian mixtures, and soft assignments

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `alg-expectation-maximization`, `thm-em-observed-log-likelihood-is-nondecreasing`, `thm-generalized-em-is-nondecreasing-under-q-ascent`, and the published KL definition/nonnegativity. Do not mint a second generic dominated KL or EM-ascent theorem. The exact support, log-integrability, finite-positive observed normalizers, finite KL and AC clauses in the current supplier must be checked for each Gaussian-mixture step. The local affine-span/SPD covariance proof, collapse counterexample and Lloyd tie-sensitive termination remain ML-owned.

**Pages:** ml02-clustering-mixtures / ml02-clustering-mixtures-examples. **Scope:** For $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in\mathbb R^p$ and $1\le K\le n$, define labels $z_i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}$, centers $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_K$, and $J(z,\mu)=\sum_i\|x_i-\mu_{z_i}\|_2^2$. Initialize labels by nearest-center assignment with smallest-index ties. At each Lloyd iteration assign each point to a nearest center (retain its current label on a tie, otherwise choose the smallest index), replace every nonempty cluster center by its sample mean, and leave an empty-cluster center unchanged. This tie rule makes any changed assignment strictly reduce the fixed-center objective; combined with mean minimization and finitely many assignments, the procedure terminates at a coordinatewise fixed point. It may reach different local fixed points from different starts; global partition search is combinatorial. For fixed positive mixture weights $\pi_k$ and centers $\mu_k$, equal spherical Gaussian components $N(\mu_k,\sigma^2I)$ have responsibilities proportional to $\pi_k\exp[-\|x_i-\mu_k\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$; as $\sigma\downarrow0$, a unique nearest center receives responsibility tending to one, and tied nearest centers divide limiting responsibility in proportion to their $\pi_k$. The finite-data objective satisfies $-2\sigma^2\sum_i\log\sum_k\pi_k\exp[-\|x_i-\mu_k\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]\to\sum_i\min_k\|x_i-\mu_k\|^2$. This is a fixed-parameter low-variance limit, not a claim that EM iterates converge to a global K-means solution.

Define $\phi_p(x;\mu,\Sigma)=(2\pi)^{-p/2}(\det\Sigma)^{-1/2}\exp[-(x-\mu)^T\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)/2]$ for $\Sigma\succ0$, and locally prove it is a normalized density using the positive spectral square root and linear change of variables from independent scalar standard normals. Define a $K$-component Gaussian mixture with $\pi_k>0$, $\sum_k\pi_k=1$, means $\mu_k\in\mathbb R^p$, and SPD covariances $\Sigma_k$. For fixed observations $x_i$, define responsibilities $\gamma_{ik}=\frac{\pi_k\phi_p(x_i;\mu_k,\Sigma_k)}{\sum_j\pi_j\phi_p(x_i;\mu_j,\Sigma_j)}$ and exact EM updates $N_k=\sum_i\gamma_{ik}$, $\pi_k'=N_k/n$, $\mu_k'=N_k^{-1}\sum_i\gamma_{ik}x_i$, $\Sigma_k'=N_k^{-1}\sum_i\gamma_{ik}(x_i-\mu_k')(x_i-\mu_k')^T$. For the closed-form unrestricted M-step to stay inside the stated SPD domain, require the observed finite points affinely span $\mathbb R^p$; otherwise use an explicitly constrained covariance domain and its corresponding constrained M-step. For $K\ge2$, the unrestricted Gaussian-mixture likelihood can be unbounded as one component covariance collapses onto a data point while another component explains the remaining observations; no global maximum or parameter convergence follows.

For a general dominated latent model, define observed density $p_\theta(x)=\int p_\theta(x,z)\,d\nu(z)$ and log likelihood $\ell(\theta;x)=\log p_\theta(x)$. Require $0<p_{\theta_t}(x)<\infty$, posterior density $q_t(z)=p_{\theta_t}(x,z)/p_{\theta_t}(x)$, common posterior support $q_t\ll p_\theta(\cdot\mid x)$ for candidate updates, and finite expectations/KL in the identity. Define $D_{KL}(q\|p)=\int q(z)\log(q(z)/p(z))\,d\nu(z)$ when $q\ll p$ and the log integral is finite; prove nonnegativity by writing $q=rp$ and Jensen for convex $r\mapsto r\log r$ under probability density $p$. Then $\ell(\theta;x)-\ell(\theta_t;x)=Q(\theta,\theta_t)-Q(\theta_t,\theta_t)+D_{KL}(q_t\|p_\theta(\cdot\mid x))$, where $Q(\theta,\theta_t)=E_{q_t}\log p_\theta(x,Z)$. Any generalized M-step with $Q(\theta_{t+1},\theta_t)\ge Q(\theta_t,\theta_t)$ therefore has nondecreasing observed log likelihood.

**Proof route:** For K-means, expand square loss about each nonempty-cluster mean and use pointwise nearest assignment; prove strict decrease on changed assignments under the tie rule, so a finite assignment space gives termination. For the Gaussian responsibilities/M-step, derive Bayes’ formula and weighted normal maximum-likelihood updates; verify SPD using positive responsibilities and affine full span. The M-step proof expands the weighted complete log likelihood, maximizes the simplex term at $\pi_k=N_k/n$, the weighted quadratic at $\mu_k=\sum_i\gamma_{ik}x_i/N_k$, and the covariance term after writing $A_k=\Sigma_k^{-1}$ as $N_k\log\det A_k/2-\operatorname{tr}(A_kS_k)/2$. Prove strict concavity of $\log\det A$ on SPD matrices from $D^2\log\det(A)[H,H]=-\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}HA^{-1}H)<0$ for symmetric nonzero $H$; the unique covariance maximizer is $A_k=N_kS_k^{-1}$. Prove EM monotonicity by the displayed KL decomposition (or the Jensen/ELBO equivalent), with all support and integrability conditions; do not infer convergence of parameters from likelihood monotonicity. The low-variance limit follows by factoring the minimum squared distance from the finite log-sum-exp and taking $\sigma\to0$.

**Examples:** Initialization-dependent two-cluster data; fractional responsibilities for overlapping components; collapsed covariance and unbounded likelihood; tied nearest centers.

**Requires:** published `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation` (finite-dimensional least squares), `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, `def-moments-variance-and-covariance` (when variance/covariance is used), `thm-jensen-inequality-for-expectation` with its integrability/nonnegative-composition premise, `def-standard-normal-and-normal-laws` and `def-multivariate-normal-law` (both assume AC), `thm-independent-random-elements-have-product-joint-law`, `thm-tonelli-theorem-for-sigma-finite-product-spaces`, `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms`, and `thm-linear-change-of-variables-for-lebesgue-measure` (assumes Countable Choice, hence AC suffices), with their exact scalar/product, finite-dimensional, and measure-change clauses. These suppliers only support the local SPD Gaussian-density normalization proof; singular Gaussian laws are outside the mixture density formula. The generic EM proof is supplied by published Category 3; only the Gaussian-mixture/Lloyd specializations are local, and no Category 4 pair is an upstream dependency of Cat3. **Sources:** ESL §§13.2.1–13.2.3, printed pp. 460–463; Exercise 13.1, printed p. 481 (which poses, but does not prove, the low-variance limit); §§14.3.4–14.3.7, pp. 507–512; §8.5, pp. 272–281. ESL gives K-means descent and Gaussian mixture responsibilities, and its general EM monotonicity derivation (8.44)–(8.47); the present common-support/integrability and finite-stopping contracts are explicit local completions. Proposed items: `def-k-means-objective`; `alg-lloyd-k-means`; `thm-lloyd-objective-monotonicity-and-termination`; `def-finite-gaussian-mixture`; `alg-gaussian-mixture-em`; `def-dominated-kl-divergence-for-em`; `lem-log-determinant-strictly-concave-on-positive-definite-matrices`; `thm-em-observed-likelihood-monotonicity-under-common-support`; `thm-equal-spherical-mixture-low-variance-kmeans-limit`. The source does not provide a general parameter-convergence theorem.

### ML-20 — Spectral clustering and graph cuts

**Pages:** ml02-spectral-clustering / ml02-spectral-clustering-examples. **Scope:** For a finite vertex set $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ define a symmetric nonnegative weight matrix $W=(w_{ij})$ with zero diagonal, degrees $d_i=\sum_jw_{ij}$, $D=\operatorname{diag}(d_i)$, and $L=D-W$. Its support graph has edge $ij$ exactly when $w_{ij}>0$. Define $L_{\rm sym}=D^{-1/2}LD^{-1/2}$ only on positive-degree vertices. For normalized-cut optimization remove zero-degree vertices first and reinsert them as separately reported singleton outputs; they have zero volume and do not enter the Ncut objective. Prove $f^TLf=\frac12\sum_{i,j}w_{ij}(f_i-f_j)^2$, so $L\succeq0$ and $\ker L$ consists exactly of vectors constant on each connected component of the support graph.

For a partition into nonempty sets $A_1,\ldots,A_k$, define $\operatorname{cut}(A,A^c)=\sum_{i\in A,j\notin A}w_{ij}$ and $\operatorname{vol}(A)=\sum_{i\in A}d_i$. Define $\operatorname{RatioCut}=\sum_r\operatorname{cut}(A_r,A_r^c)/|A_r|$ and $\operatorname{Ncut}=\sum_r\operatorname{cut}(A_r,A_r^c)/\operatorname{vol}(A_r)$, requiring positive volume for every Ncut part. Show the normalized cluster-indicator matrices give $\operatorname{RatioCut}=\operatorname{tr}(H^TLH)$ with $H^TH=I$, and $\operatorname{Ncut}=\operatorname{tr}(H^TLH)$ with $H^TDH=I$. Relax indicator constraints to real matrices; the first relaxation is solved by the $k$ lowest eigenvectors of $L$, the second by the generalized eigenproblem $Lu=\lambda Du$ (equivalently $L_{\rm sym}$ after deleting isolates). Define unnormalized/normalized spectral clustering as graph construction, eigenvector embedding, optional row normalization in the normalized variant, and a deterministic K-means step. The relaxation gives a lower bound on the combinatorial optimum, not a rounding or approximation guarantee. Graph weights, bandwidth, kNN symmetrization, degree handling and cluster count are modeling choices. **Proof route:** derive the weighted quadratic identity and kernel characterization locally; derive the indicator trace formulas; prove the finite-dimensional trace-minimization eigenvector lemma by spectral decomposition. The published simple unweighted Laplacian results apply only to the equal-positive-weight special case; they do not prove the arbitrary weighted extension. Davis–Kahan perturbation bounds require an eigengap and a specified matrix norm and need a local proof or exact supplier. **Examples:** disconnected positive-weight components; an isolate; a kNN graph where changing symmetrization changes the cut; concentric-circle embeddings. **Requires:** published `def-laplacian-matrix-of-a-finite-simple-graph`, `thm-laplacian-is-positive-semidefinite-and-annihilates-ones`, and `thm-laplacian-kernel-dimension-counts-components` only for the unweighted special case; `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` for finite symmetric eigen-decompositions; `cor-rayleigh-quotient-extreme-eigenvalue-characterisation` for the one-vector case; ML-19 — K-means, Gaussian mixtures, and soft assignments for the final rounding algorithm. Prove the multi-vector trace-minimization lemma locally; the extreme-Rayleigh corollary alone is insufficient. **Sources:** von Luxburg, “A Tutorial on Spectral Clustering,” §§2–5, printed pp. 4–22, especially the RatioCut/Ncut indicator identities and relaxations in §§5.1–5.3; Theorem 7 (Davis–Kahan) in §7.1 is stated but referred to matrix perturbation sources for proof. ESL §14.5.3, printed pp. 544–547, gives the algorithmic comparison. Neither source proves graph/manifold consistency.

### ML-21 — PCA, kernel PCA, MDS, and manifold embeddings

**Pages:** ml02-dimension-reduction / ml02-dimension-reduction-examples. **Scope:** For a finite data matrix $X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}$ define the centering matrix $H_n=I-n^{-1}\mathbf1\mathbf1^T$ and $X_c=H_nX$. Define rank-$q$ PCA as a matrix $Q\in\mathbb R^{p\times q}$ with $Q^TQ=I$ minimizing $\|X_c-X_cQQ^T\|_F^2$; prove the minimizer spans the top $q$ right-singular vectors and the residual is $\sum_{j>q}\sigma_j(X_c)^2$. Define kernel PCA from a finite PSD kernel Gram matrix $K$, centering $K_c=H_nKH_n$ and retaining its largest nonnegative eigenvalues/eigenvectors; no kernel-positive-definiteness theorem is assumed for arbitrary formulas. Define classical MDS from a symmetric nonnegative zero-diagonal dissimilarity matrix $D$ by $B=-\frac12H_nD^{\circ2}H_n$; when $D$ contains Euclidean distances, $B$ is PSD and the positive-eigenvalue coordinates recover a Euclidean realization up to rigid motion when the embedding dimension is large enough. For non-Euclidean dissimilarities, positive-eigenvalue truncation is only the stated spectral procedure, not exact distance recovery.

Define ISOMAP by a fixed kNN or radius graph with deterministic distance ties and an explicit union/mutual symmetrization rule; require its graph be connected before all-pairs shortest-path distances are finite, then apply classical MDS. Define LLE by selecting $1\le k<n$ neighbors, choosing the minimum-Euclidean-norm minimizer of $\|x_i-\sum_{j\in N(i)}w_{ij}x_j\|^2$ subject to $\sum_jw_{ij}=1$, forming $M=(I-W_{\rm LLE})^T(I-W_{\rm LLE})$ with every row of $W_{\rm LLE}$ summing to one, and minimizing $\operatorname{tr}(Y^TMY)$ subject to $Y^T\mathbf1=0$ and $Y^TY=I_q$ with $1\le q<n$; require $\ker(I-W_{\rm LLE})=\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf1\}$ to isolate the constant zero eigenvector; graph connectivity alone need not imply this for LLE weights. These are finite-matrix algorithms, not guarantees of manifold recovery. Stress MDS minimizes a separate nonconvex distance-stress objective and has no classical spectral closed form. **Proof route:** prove the PCA/Frobenius result locally by SVD and trace expansion; the published Eckart–Young supplier is only operator-norm and cannot be stretched to Frobenius optimality. For Euclidean MDS, expand squared distances and double-center. Kernel PCA is a centered symmetric eigenproblem once the Gram PSD premise is supplied. LLE’s objective is exactly the displayed trace; its minimizers follow from the local trace/eigenvalue argument. Do not infer ISOMAP geodesic accuracy or LLE manifold consistency from these finite algorithms. **Examples:** exact rank-$q$ reconstruction; non-Euclidean dissimilarity with negative MDS eigenvalues; disconnected kNN graph; singular local LLE covariance; an unrolled curve where MDS, ISOMAP and LLE differ. **Requires:** published `thm-singular-value-decomposition`; `thm-spectral-and-frobenius-norms-are-unitarily-invariant-with-singular-value-formulas-and-rank-comparison` (Frobenius singular-value formula/unitary invariance); `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms`; `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation` and `thm-projection-onto-a-nonempty-closed-convex-set` with `def-countable-choice` for deterministic minimum-norm LLE weights; graph-Laplacian definitions only for graph construction. Supply the Ky Fan/trace minimization argument locally; `cor-rayleigh-quotient-extreme-eigenvalue-characterisation` alone covers only one vector. `thm-eckart-young-best-rank-k-approximation` may support a separately stated operator-norm claim only. **Sources:** ESL §14.5.1, printed pp. 534–541 (PCA); §§14.5.4–14.5.5, pp. 547–552 (kernel PCA/sparse PCA); §§14.8–14.9, pp. 570–576 (MDS, ISOMAP, LLE and local MDS); §18.5.2, pp. 670–672 (distance/Gram conversion); von Luxburg §§5.2–5.3, pp. 12–16 (spectral embedding comparison). ESL marks some PCA/MDS algebra as exercises and proves no manifold-recovery theorem.

### ML-22 — Latent factors, ICA, NMF, and representation ambiguity

**Pages:** ml02-latent-representations / ml02-latent-representations-examples. **Scope:** Define the covariance factor model with $X\in\mathbb R^p$, $S\in\mathbb R^q$, $A\in\mathbb R^{p\times q}$ and $q<p$, as $X=AS+\varepsilon$, $E[S]=E[\varepsilon]=0$, $\operatorname{Cov}(S)=I_q$, $\operatorname{Cov}(\varepsilon)=D$ diagonal positive, and $\operatorname{Cov}(S,\varepsilon)=0$, so $\operatorname{Cov}(X)=AA^T+D$. Prove covariance nonidentifiability under $A\mapsto AR^T$, $S\mapsto RS$ for every orthogonal $R$; this is a covariance-model statement (and an exact law symmetry when the standardized factors are jointly Gaussian), not identification of scientific causes.

Define square ICA by $X,S\in\mathbb R^p$ and $X=AS$ with $A\in GL_p(\mathbb R)$, mutually independent centered sources of unit variance, and finite second moments. State the classical identifiability condition “at most one source is Gaussian” and the resulting permutation/sign ambiguity after unit-variance standardization (or scale/permutation ambiguity without standardization). Whitening reduces the remaining mixing ambiguity to an orthogonal transform. ESL states this condition but does not prove the Darmois–Skitovich/characteristic-function theorem, so the identifiability result remains a local proof obligation unless a complete authoritative proof is supplied.

For Poisson NMF, take count data $x_{ij}\in\mathbb N_0$ with no all-zero row or column (otherwise remove it or specify a separate zero-factor rule), factors $W\in[0,\infty)^{n\times r}$ and $H\in[0,\infty)^{r\times p}$, rates $\lambda_{ij}=(WH)_{ij}$, and independent $X_{ij}\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda_{ij})$. Define the log-likelihood up to constants as $\ell(W,H)=\sum_{ij}[x_{ij}\log\lambda_{ij}-\lambda_{ij}]$, with $0\log0=0$ and $\ell=-\infty$ if $x_{ij}>0=\lambda_{ij}$. For positive initialization and strictly positive denominators, define the source’s multiplicative updates $W_{ik}\leftarrow W_{ik}\frac{\sum_jH_{kj}x_{ij}/(WH)_{ij}}{\sum_jH_{kj}}$ and $H_{kj}\leftarrow H_{kj}\frac{\sum_iW_{ik}x_{ij}/(WH)_{ij}}{\sum_iW_{ik}}$. Positive component rescalings $W_{\cdot k}\mapsto cW_{\cdot k}$, $H_{k\cdot}\mapsto c^{-1}H_{k\cdot}$ and component permutations leave $WH$ unchanged; additional nonuniqueness can occur. The factor objective is nonconvex, and no global optimum or convergence claim is made. **Proof route:** prove the factor covariance invariance by substitution. Prove ICA identifiability locally only if the independence theorem and its regularity conditions are supplied. Derive the Poisson likelihood directly.
