ESL assigns the multiplicative-update minorization proof to Exercise 14.23; author the full auxiliary-function/MM proof before claiming likelihood monotonicity, or present the update only as a finite procedure with no convergence claim. **Examples:** rotate two standardized Gaussian factors; non-Gaussian independent sources with permutation/sign ambiguity; nonunique nonnegative cones; positive NMF initialization and zero-count entries. **Requires:** published `thm-singular-value-decomposition` and `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` for whitening/eigen-algebra only. Cat3 latent likelihood/EM interfaces are optional for a separately specified probabilistic fit. The ICA and NMF guarantees are local proof obligations; do not turn source conditions or citations into proof evidence. **Sources:** ESL §§14.7.1–14.7.2, printed pp. 558–565 (factor analysis/ICA assumptions and discussion), and §14.6, pp. 553–554 (Poisson NMF objective/update; Exercise 14.23 leaves its MM proof open). ESL explains the ICA non-Gaussian condition but does not prove identifiability.

### ML-23 — Nearest neighbors and learned metrics

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `alg-projected-subgradient-method` and `thm-projected-subgradient-best-and-average-bound` when applying a finite-budget convex solver to the fixed-neighbor LMNN objective. A global convergence rate additionally needs an attained comparator and the theorem’s bounded subgradients on the iterate domain; convexity alone is insufficient. Eigenvalue clipping remains the exact finite PSD projection specialization.

**Pages:** ml02-metric-learning / ml02-metric-learning-examples. **Scope:** For a finite labeled sample $(x_i,y_i)_{i=1}^n$ in $\mathbb R^d\times\{1,\ldots,C\}$, define kNN using $1\le k\le n$ and a fixed sample-index tie rule for equal distances; break a class vote tie by the smallest class label. Define the squared Mahalanobis distance $q_M(x,x')=d_M^2(x,x')=(x-x')^TM(x-x')$ with $M\succeq0$. Its nonnegative square root $d_M(x,x')=\sqrt{q_M(x,x')}$ is the pseudometric: equivalently $M=L^TL$ and $d_M(x,x')=\|Lx-Lx'\|_2$; singular $M$ may identify distinct points. In LMNN, fix for each $i$ a set $N_i$ of $k$ target neighbors with $j\ne i$ and $y_j=y_i$ (assume at least $k$ same-class candidates, or define a smaller $k_i$ in advance). Define $\epsilon_{\rm pull}(M)=\sum_i\sum_{j\in N_i}d_M^2(x_i,x_j)$ and $\epsilon_{\rm push}(M)=\sum_i\sum_{j\in N_i}\sum_{l:y_l\ne y_i}[1+d_M^2(x_i,x_j)-d_M^2(x_i,x_l)]_+$. The LMNN SDP minimizes $(1-\mu)\epsilon_{\rm pull}(M)+\mu\epsilon_{\rm push}(M)$ over $M\succeq0$, for fixed $\mu\in[0,1]$ and fixed target neighbors. Each squared distance is linear in $M$, each hinge is convex, and the PSD cone is convex, so the objective is convex. This is a training surrogate, not test error or calibrated/generalization risk.

Define a finite gradient-projection procedure for a supplied iteration count $T$, initial $M_0\succeq0$, positive step sizes $\eta_t$, and the above objective $F$: at step $t$, select a subgradient $G_t\in\partial F(M_t)$ (choose hinge subgradient $0$ at an exact kink), write $M_t-\eta_tG_t=U_t\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{t,j})U_t^T$ and set $M_{t+1}=U_t\operatorname{diag}(\max\{\lambda_{t,j},0\})U_t^T$; return $M_T$. This is the Frobenius projection onto the PSD cone; no solver convergence or empirical-error improvement follows without step-size and objective-optimization analysis. **Proof route:** write $d_M^2=\operatorname{tr}(M(x-x')(x-x')^T)$, so every pull term is linear and every push hinge is convex in $M$; intersections with $M\succeq0$ form a convex SDP. Derive the finite subgradient and projection updates from the stated formulas; eigenvalue clipping is proved by the SVD/Frobenius projection argument if included as a theorem. **Examples:** rank-deficient $M$ gives a pseudometric; fixed targets can conflict; a projection step can reduce objective without establishing convergence. **Requires:** published `thm-non-negative-operator-characterisations` for the finite PSD matrix clause; `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` and `thm-spectral-and-frobenius-norms-are-unitarily-invariant-with-singular-value-formulas-and-rank-comparison` for eigenvalue clipping; `thm-projection-onto-a-nonempty-closed-convex-set` and `def-countable-choice` for existence/uniqueness of each Frobenius projection; the projection theorem uses Countable Choice, which must be carried into any theorem analyzing this procedure. The hinge objective’s convexity is proved directly from affine distances and the convex positive-part function; no C² Hessian criterion is applied. ML-14 — RKHS regularization, kernels, and support-vector machines is needed only for an optional separately defined kernelized extension. **Sources:** Weinberger and Saul, “Distance Metric Learning for Large Margin Nearest Neighbor Classification,” §2.1 and §§3.1–3.5, printed pp. 209–221 (Mahalanobis pseudometrics, target neighbors, impostors, pull/push hinge and convex SDP); §§5.3, 6.1–6.3, pp. 230–240; Appendix A.2–A.3 (custom solver). The paper’s convex formulation does not prove convergence of the custom projection solver or empirical kNN error gains.

### ML-24 — Undirected graphical models and sparse precision estimation

**Pages:** ml02-graphical-models / ml02-graphical-models-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite undirected graph on a finite family of random variables, its pairwise Markov condition (nonadjacent variables are conditionally independent given all remaining vertices), and its global Markov condition (vertex separation implies conditional independence). Separately define a finite-state clique-factorized model by a strictly positive mass function $p(x)=Z^{-1}\prod_{C\in\mathcal C}\psi_C(x_C)$, with positive clique potentials and finite partition function; do not claim equivalence with either Markov condition unless the finite-state Hammersley–Clifford proof is supplied. For $X\sim N_p(\mu,\Sigma)$ with $\Sigma\succ0$ and $\Theta=\Sigma^{-1}$, define the Gaussian conditional-independence graph by $i-j\iff\Theta_{ij}\ne0$ for $i\ne j$, and prove $\Theta_{ij}=0\iff X_i\perp X_j\mid X_{-\{i,j\}}$ using the conditional two-coordinate precision block. Define centered sample covariance $S=N^{-1}\sum_r(x_r-\bar x)(x_r-\bar x)^T$ and profiled Gaussian log likelihood $\log\det\Theta-\operatorname{tr}(S\Theta)$, up to constants. Use the source’s primary graphical-lasso convention $\max_{\Theta\succ0}\{\log\det\Theta-\operatorname{tr}(S\Theta)-\lambda\|\Theta\|_1\}$, $\lambda>0$, where $\|\Theta\|_1$ sums every matrix entry including the diagonal; identify the off-diagonal-only edge-penalty variant as a distinct convention. State the optimizer KKT condition with $\Gamma_{ij}=\operatorname{sign}(\Theta_{ij})$ off zero and $\Gamma_{ij}\in[-1,1]$ at zero, so $\Theta^{-1}-S-\lambda\Gamma=0$. State the block/cyclic coordinate algorithm as a procedure; do not claim its implementation converges. Define a binary RBM on a finite bipartite visible/hidden graph by $E(v,h)=-a^Tv-b^Th-v^TWh$, $p(v,h)\propto e^{-E(v,h)}$, and derive conditional independence within each layer and the sigmoid conditional probabilities. Distinguish the exact likelihood gradient from contrastive-divergence’s finite-chain approximation. Proposed items: def-undirected-graphical-model-and-markov-properties; thm-gaussian-precision-zero-conditional-independence; def-graphical-lasso-objective; alg-graphical-lasso-coordinate-descent; thm-graphical-lasso-convexity-and-kkt; def-restricted-boltzmann-machine. **Proof route:** obtain the Gaussian conditional law from a block precision factorization and show that its conditional two-variable density has cross term $-\Theta_{ij}x_ix_j$; positive conditional Gaussian covariance gives independence exactly when that coefficient vanishes. On the open convex cone of symmetric positive-definite matrices, differentiate $-\log\det\Theta$ twice to get $\operatorname{tr}(\Theta^{-1}H\Theta^{-1}H)\ge0$; derive the entrywise $\ell_1$ subgradient and KKT equation, and use strict concavity of $\log\det\Theta$ plus coercivity for $\lambda>0$ to justify a unique maximizer. The published Hessian criterion applies only to $C^2$ functions on open convex domains; the derivative and trace identities remain local algebra. The source’s block updates reduce each row/column subproblem to a lasso and use cyclic coordinate descent, but no global convergence proof is supplied. Hammersley–Clifford needs a separate finite-state, strictly-positive proof if an equivalence theorem is asserted. Hidden-variable marginalization can induce higher-order visible interactions. **Examples:** 3-node Gaussian chain; how $\lambda$ changes off-diagonal sparsity; RBM visible units conditionally independent given hidden units. **Requires:** def-multivariate-normal-law (published; AC; permits singular covariance generally, but this Gaussian-graphical claim adds $\Sigma\succ0$); def-conditional-independence-given-a-sigma-algebra (published; AC); def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable (published); thm-non-negative-operator-characterisations and cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms (published, finite-dimensional spectral/PSD clauses); thm-hessian-characterises-convexity and def-convex-and-strictly-convex-functions-on-euclidean-sets (published, with its open-convex $C^2$ scope). Supply the Gaussian conditional block proof, log-determinant derivative, trace positivity and Hammersley–Clifford result locally if claimed. **Sources:** ESL, corrected 12th printing, Ch. 17 §§17.2–17.4.4, printed pp. 627–645 (pairwise/global Markov properties and Hammersley–Clifford statement; Gaussian conditional law; Exercise 17.3 leaves the precision-zero implication as an exercise; Gaussian likelihood and graphical-lasso Algorithm 17.2; RBM equations and contrastive divergence); Friedman, Hastie, and Tibshirani, “Sparse Inverse Covariance Estimation with the Lasso,” §§1–5, printed pp. 1–9, especially objective (1), KKT (5), and covariance-lasso algorithm. Both sources are fully available in the audit source dossier. ESL does not prove Hammersley–Clifford or the full Gaussian precision-zero equivalence; Friedman et al. give no convergence proof for the cyclic implementation.

### ML-25 — PC algorithm and Markov-equivalence structure learning

**Pages:** ml02-causal-pc / ml02-causal-pc-examples. **Scope:** Consume the finite recursive SCM/DAG, d-separation, global-Markov, and faithfulness interfaces from SME-20; do not redefine them here. Define the PC discovery regime locally: a finite set of causally sufficient observed variables, one DAG $G$, a joint law Markov and faithful to $G$, and a perfect conditional-independence oracle for all queried disjoint sets. For distinct vertices $i,k,j$, define the triple $i-k-j$ to be unshielded when $i,j$ are nonadjacent, and a v-structure when its arrows are $i\to k\leftarrow j$; a skeleton forgets all edge directions. Define DAG Markov equivalence by equality of the induced d-separation relations, PDAG as a mixed graph with no directed cycle among its already-directed edges, and a consistent DAG extension as an acyclic orientation of all undirected edges that creates no unshielded collider absent from the PDAG. A CPDAG is the unique completed PDAG representing one equivalence class: every directed edge is present with that orientation in every represented DAG, and each undirected edge occurs in both orientations among its consistent extensions. Population PC starts from the complete undirected graph and, at levels $\ell=0,1,\ldots$, tests every $\ell$-element subset of the current neighbors of either endpoint of each adjacent pair in a fixed vertex order; delete $i-j$ on the first oracle result $X_i\perp X_j\mid X_S$ and record that $S$. Stop when no adjacent pair has enough neighbors for the next level. Orient an unshielded $i-k-j$ as $i\to k\leftarrow j$ when $k\notin\operatorname{Sep}(i,j)$, then repeatedly apply the background-free Meek rules R1–R3 to closure: R1, $i\to j-k$ with $i,k$ nonadjacent implies $j\to k$; R2, $i-j$ with a directed chain $i\to k\to j$ implies $i\to j$; R3, if $i-j$, $i-k$, and $i-\ell$ are undirected, $k\to j$ and $\ell\to j$, and $k,\ell$ are nonadjacent, then $i\to j$. The no-background completion does not use Meek’s separate R4 rule for required/forbidden background orientations. State and prove locally: (i) an edge is in the true skeleton iff no conditioning subset d-separates its endpoints under Markov plus faithfulness; (ii) DAGs are Markov equivalent iff they have the same skeleton and unshielded colliders; (iii) the separating-set rule recovers precisely the unshielded colliders; (iv) the orientation rules are sound and complete and their closure is the CPDAG; hence oracle PC is correct. Do not attribute (i)–(iv) to SME-20 or to the PC source’s citations. The sample variant is for iid draws from a nondegenerate $p_n$-variate Gaussian law; test CI by zero partial correlation and use Fisher’s $z=\frac12\log\frac{1+\hat\rho}{1-\hat\rho}$ with the source test threshold $\sqrt{n-|S|-3}|z|>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha_n/2)$. State Kalisch–Bühlmann’s triangular-array assumptions exactly: Gaussianity and faithfulness for every $n$; $p_n=O(n^a)$ for some finite $a\ge0$; maximum skeleton degree $q_n=O(n^{1-b})$, $0<b\le1$; every nonzero partial correlation has magnitude at least $c_n$, with $c_n^{-1}=O(n^d)$ and $0<d<b/2$; and all partial correlations over all pairs/conditioning sets have magnitude at most a fixed $M<1$. With the theoretical choice $\alpha_n=2\{1-\Phi(n^{1/2}c_n/2)\}$, both the skeleton and CPDAG error probabilities are $O(\exp(-C n^{1-2d}))$ for a source constant $C>0$. The choice depends on the unknown $c_n$, so this theorem is not an implementable tuning rule. A CPDAG leaves non-compelled edges unoriented; latent confounding violates the causal-sufficiency regime. Proposed items: def-causal-discovery-oracle-regime; def-cpdag-markov-equivalence-class; alg-pc-causal-discovery; thm-pc-oracle-correctness; thm-pc-high-dimensional-consistency. **Proof route:** show true adjacent endpoints cannot be removed (their length-one path remains active) and nonadjacent endpoints have a separating set eventually tested from the shrinking neighborhoods; use Markov and faithfulness only to exchange graph separation and CI. Prove the same-skeleton/unshielded-collider equivalence characterization separately. Prove collider orientation from the recorded separator, then prove each Meek rule preserves acyclicity and the represented class and prove rule closure or an equivalent extension argument is complete for compelled orientations. For the Gaussian sample variant, prove locally that zero partial correlation is equivalent to conditional independence, from the nondegenerate Gaussian conditional law, and state the covariance-invertibility and $n-|S|-3$ conditions. For the consistency theorem, reproduce the source’s uniform Fisher-transform concentration, count the tested pairs/conditioning sets using $p_n,q_n$, and union-bound false deletions/retentions; carry the lower/upper partial-correlation and Gaussian nondegeneracy assumptions through. **Examples:** chain versus collider with separation sets; two DAGs with identical skeleton/v-structures; latent confounding. **Requires:** SME-20 — SCM, DAGs, adjustment, exact proposed interfaces def-recursive-structural-causal-model-and-dag, def-d-separation-in-a-dag, thm-dag-factorization-implies-d-separation-conditional-independence, and def-faithfulness-of-a-dag-model (proposed upstream; use only the finite-DAG measurable recursive SCM/full-joint topological-kernel factorization under mutually independent exogenous variables, d-separation, factorization-implies-global-Markov direction, and separately defined faithfulness converse). SME-20 explicitly does not supply Markov-equivalence, skeleton/unshielded-collider characterization, CPDAG, Meek-rule proofs, or PC correctness; these statements and their proofs remain local to this pair. Also require published def-conditional-independence-given-a-sigma-algebra (AC) for the probability statement, def-independent-random-elements for the independent exogenous variables, and def-multivariate-normal-law (AC, PSD laws allowed but this result assumes nondegenerate covariance) for the Gaussian variant. **Sources:** Kalisch and Bühlmann, “Estimating High-Dimensional Directed Acyclic Graphs with the PC-Algorithm,” §§2.1–2.3, printed pp. 614–619 (definitions, PCpop skeleton, Fisher test, recorded separators, and the displayed orientation rules); §3 and Theorems 1–2, pp. 619–620 (exact triangular-array assumptions and both consistency claims); Appendix A, pp. 628–636 (skeleton/CPDAG error arguments). Its printed R4 condition is internally inconsistent (it requires $k\to\ell$ and also says $k,\ell$ are nonadjacent), so this no-background scaffold instead uses the complete R1–R3 closure. Meek, “Causal Inference and Causal Explanation with Background Knowledge,” UAI 1995, Theorem 1 and §2.1, printed pp. 404–405, Theorems 2–3, pp. 406, and Appendix proofs, pp. 408–410: full article consulted; Theorem 1 proves the skeleton/unshielded-collider equivalence characterization, and Theorem 3 proves R1–R3 completeness for the background-free pattern. The PC paper cites the Verma–Pearl and Meek results rather than proving them; reproduce their hypotheses and proof argument locally.

### ML-26 — Additive-noise causal discovery and identifiability

**Pages:** ml02-causal-anm / ml02-causal-anm-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite recursive DAG structural equation model $X_j=f_j(X_{\mathrm{pa}(j)})+N_j$ with mutually independent exogenous noises, absolutely continuous variables and strictly positive densities. Carry the source’s continuity and $C^3$ smoothness conditions wherever Condition 19 is used. In the bivariate direction theorem, assume causal minimality (the cause-to-effect function is nonconstant) and exclude exactly the source’s differential-equation exception: the log-density/function triple does not solve equation (7) at all points where $\nu''(x_j-f(x_i))f'(x_i)\ne0$. This gives identifiability from the observational joint law; linear-Gaussian models generally remain directionally reversible. For the multivariate restricted-ANM theorem, require for every node $j$, parent $i\in PA_j$, and conditioning set $S$ with $PA_j\setminus\{i\}\subseteq S\subseteq ND_j\setminus\{i,j\}$, some $x_S$ with $p_S(x_S)>0$ such that the induced bivariate triple satisfies Condition 19; retain the nonvanishing noise-density, continuous-function and $C^3$ hypotheses. Under this restriction and causal minimality (each parent coordinate of every nonroot structural function is essential; roots have no parent-coordinate condition), the population DAG is identifiable. Define RESIT’s two phases: repeatedly regress each remaining node on the other remaining variables, use a perfect residual-independence oracle to select a sink and remove it, then prune superfluous incoming edges by residual-independence tests. Theorem 34 is population-only and assumes restricted ANM, causal minimality, a consistent nonparametric regression method and perfect independence oracle. Do not claim a finite-sample/high-dimensional theorem for the practical HSIC/regression implementation. Proposed items: def-additive-noise-model; thm-bivariate-additive-noise-identifiability; def-restricted-additive-noise-model; alg-resit; thm-restricted-anm-population-identification. **Proof route:** derive the reverse-model density factorization and its differential equation, then use the Condition 19 exclusion; prove multivariate identifiability by conditioning over the source’s intermediate sets and sink-removal induction. For RESIT, prove that true sinks have residual independent of all remaining variables and that a wrong sink would contradict bivariate identifiability, then use causal minimality for the pruning phase. Separate those population arguments from finite-sample regression/HSIC behavior. **Examples:** identifiable nonlinear ANM; linear-Gaussian reversible pair; latent confounding. **Requires:** SME-20 — SCM, DAGs, adjustment, exact proposed interface def-recursive-structural-causal-model-and-dag for finite recursive-DAG structural notation and the topological structural-equation convention (ANM noise independence and identifiability remain local; no faithfulness or Markov-equivalence theorem is used); published def-law-or-distribution-of-a-random-element, def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces, and def-independent-random-elements for the probability, measurability, and exogenous-noise contracts. The ANM density positivity, conditional-density versions, regression consistency and independence-oracle premises are stated locally and proved or assumed only where named. No Category 4 pair is a Category 1–3 prerequisite. **Sources:** Peters, Mooij, Janzing, and Schölkopf, “Causal Discovery with Continuous Additive Noise Models,” §2 and Definitions 18/27, Theorem 20, Condition 19, §3.2–3.3, Theorems 28/34, printed pp. 2014–2029; Appendix A.5, A.11–A.15, pp. 2040–2049. The article states and proves the population identifiability/RESIT claims but gives only finite-sample experiments for the implemented method.

### ML-27 — Differential privacy mechanisms and composition

**Pages:** ml02-differential-privacy / ml02-differential-privacy-examples. **Scope:** Define neighboring databases by one record addition or removal, and a randomized mechanism as a probability kernel from the database space to a measurable output space. For every neighboring $D,D'$ and measurable output event $A$, define $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-DP by $\Pr[M(D)\in A]\le e^\varepsilon\Pr[M(D')\in A]+\delta$, with $\varepsilon\ge0$, $0\le\delta\le1$. Define global $\ell_1$-sensitivity $\Delta_1f=\max_{D\sim D'}\|f(D)-f(D')\|_1$; for $\varepsilon>0$, the Laplace mechanism releases $f(D)+Z$ with independent coordinates $Z_j\sim\operatorname{Lap}(\Delta_1f/\varepsilon)$ and is $(\varepsilon,0)$-DP. Define exponential-mechanism utility sensitivity $\Delta_u=\max_{D\sim D',r}|u(D,r)-u(D',r)|$, require a database-independent finite range $\mathcal R$ and $\Delta_u>0$, and sample $r$ with probability proportional to $\exp(\varepsilon u(D,r)/(2\Delta_u))$. It is $(\varepsilon,0)$-DP; writing $\mathcal R_{\rm OPT}=\{r:u(D,r)=\max_{r'}u(D,r')\}$, for each $t>0$,
$$
\Pr\!\left[u(D,M(D))\le \operatorname{OPT}_u(D)-\frac{2\Delta_u}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{|\mathcal R|}{|\mathcal R_{\rm OPT}|}+t\right)\right]\le e^{-t}.
$$
State postprocessing for any data-independent randomized map. For adaptive composition, if round $i$, conditional on every prior transcript, is $(\varepsilon_i,\delta_i)$-DP, basic composition is $(\sum_i\varepsilon_i,\sum_i\delta_i)$-DP. Assuming AC, for $k$ adaptive $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-DP rounds and $0<\delta'<1$, advanced composition is $(\varepsilon',k\delta+\delta')$-DP, where $\varepsilon'=\sqrt{2k\log(1/\delta')}\,\varepsilon+k\varepsilon(e^\varepsilon-1)$; state $k\delta+\delta'\le1$ when a nontrivial guarantee is intended. Distinguish record-level central DP from local/client-level DP and secure aggregation; keeping records on devices alone gives no DP guarantee, and DP does not prevent inference about attributes shared by many records. Proposed items: def-differential-privacy-and-adjacency; def-global-l1-sensitivity; alg-laplace-mechanism; thm-laplace-mechanism-privacy; alg-exponential-mechanism; thm-exponential-mechanism-privacy-and-utility; thm-dp-postprocessing; thm-basic-and-advanced-composition. **Proof route:** compare Laplace output densities by the $\ell_1$ triangle inequality; for the exponential mechanism bound both the score ratio and partition-function ratio by $e^{\varepsilon/2}$, then sum utility mass outside the threshold; prove postprocessing by event preimages; prove basic composition on arbitrary measurable outputs by the bounded-test-function event extension. For advanced composition retain arbitrary measurable outputs: prove lem-dp-joint-measure-purification using ordinary finite-measure RN derivatives of joint measures with a fixed history marginal, then thm-advanced-adaptive-composition-arbitrary-measurable-outputs using consistent prefix laws, conditional loss drift and predictable-interval Azuma. No regular conditional distribution or jointly measurable kernel RN derivative is assumed. The standard-Borel specialization remains available. The advanced clause carries AC; the basic clause is choice-free. The general advanced supplier permits any symmetric adjacency relation on a power-set input set, including the ML-28 loss-vector replacement relation. Separate the ideal real-valued mechanism from floating-point implementations, whose rounding can destroy support and DP. **Examples:** add/remove noisy count; finite hypothesis selection; postprocessing a private output; record-level versus client-level adjacency. **Requires:** published def-measurable-space, def-probability-measure, and def-measure-kernel-and-probability-kernel for output events and randomized mechanisms; published def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable for privacy-loss expectations. The mechanism definitions and all adjacency/output-space hypotheses are stated locally. **Sources:** Dwork and Roth, The Algorithmic Foundations of Differential Privacy, Ch. 2 §§2.1–2.4, printed pp. 11–27 (adjacency, DP, postprocessing, group privacy and limitations); Ch. 3 §§3.2–3.5.1, pp. 29–50 (sensitivity, Laplace, exponential mechanism, simple/advanced composition and complete proofs); Ch. 3 §§3.9–3.10, pp. 58–65 (additional composition/privacy discussion). The complete relevant source chapters and proof units were read; ideal continuous-noise implementation caveats remain material.


### ML-28 — Private ERM and private online learning

**Pages:** ml02-private-learning / ml02-private-learning-examples. **Scope:** For a finite binary hypothesis class $\mathcal C$, define empirical 0–1 error $\widehat L_D(h)=|D|^{-1}\sum_{(x,y)\in D}1\{h(x)\ne y\}$. Under add/remove adjacency and datasets of size at least $n$, this score has sensitivity at most $1/n$. Select $h\in\mathcal C$ by the exponential mechanism with utility $-\widehat L_D(h)$ and privacy parameter $\varepsilon>0$. For iid samples from an arbitrary distribution, $0<\alpha,\beta<1$, and $0<\varepsilon$, utility plus finite-class uniform convergence gives agnostic excess-risk at most $\alpha$ with probability at least $1-\beta$ for
$$
n=O\!\left(\max\!\left\{\frac{\log|\mathcal C|+\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon\alpha},\frac{\log|\mathcal C|+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^2}\right\}\right).
$$
This is information-theoretic and may require enumerating an impractically large $\mathcal C$. For online learning, fix integers $k,T\ge1$, loss vectors $\ell_t\in[0,1]^k$, and average regret $R_T=T^{-1}\sum_t\ell_t(a_t)-\min_jT^{-1}\sum_t\ell_t(j)$. Initialize $w_{1,j}=1$, choose $a_t=j$ with probability $p_{t,j}=w_{t,j}/\sum_rw_{t,r}$, observe the entire loss vector, then update $w_{t+1,j}=w_{t,j}e^{-\eta\ell_t(j)}$. Require losses to be nonanticipating: the round-$t$ loss vector is fixed before the fresh action draw, though it may depend on the previous transcript. For every such adversary and every $\eta>0$, $\mathbb E R_T\le\eta+\ln k/(\eta T)$; with probability at least $1-\beta$,
$$R_T\le\eta+\frac{\ln k}{\eta T}+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(1/\beta)}{2T}}.$$
The expected-regret bound gives $2\sqrt{\ln k/T}$ at $\eta=\sqrt{\ln k/T}$ for $k\ge2$; at $k=1$ the regret is identically zero. No computational-efficiency obstruction arises in the finite $k$ update itself.

For privacy, view a fixed loss sequence as the private input and declare two sequences adjacent when one entire time-step vector is arbitrarily replaced. The released output is the full action transcript. Each action kernel is $(2\eta,0)$-DP under that replacement relation, for every previous action transcript. Under AC, for every $\varepsilon>0$ and $0<\delta<1$, put $L=\ln(1/\delta)$ and choose
$$\eta=\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{32TL}},\frac12,\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{16T}}\right\}>0.$$
Advanced composition with round error zero and auxiliary error $\delta'=\delta$ gives $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-DP for the full action transcript. The displayed exact regret bounds apply with this choice for all $\varepsilon>0$ and $0<\delta,\beta<1$. In particular the high-probability bound can be written without an implicit simplification as
$$R_T\le\eta+\frac{\ln k}{T}\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{32TL}}{\varepsilon},2,4\sqrt{\frac{T}{\varepsilon}}\right\}+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(1/\beta)}{2T}}.$$
The source choice $\eta_0=\varepsilon/\sqrt{32TL}$ is retained whenever $\varepsilon\le\sqrt{8TL}$ and $\varepsilon\le2L$; in this region the bound specializes to $\eta_0+\sqrt{32L}\ln k/(\varepsilon\sqrt T)+\sqrt{\ln(1/\beta)/(2T)}$. Do not replace these bounds by the originally planned unrestricted $O(\sqrt{L\ln(k/\beta)}/(\varepsilon\sqrt T))$: the stated update and proof do not support that simplification for arbitrary $k,\varepsilon,\delta$. The privacy claim for a fixed sequence and the regret claim for a nonanticipating adaptive sequence are distinct quantified claims. For an interactive privacy formulation with loss-generating strategies, require at every common transcript the per-round ordered kernel inequalities under the chosen adjacent-strategy relation, then apply the same general-adjacency composition theorem. Arbitrary response to the current fresh action is not permitted by the regret claim.

Proposed items: alg-private-finite-class-erm; thm-private-erm-excess-risk; alg-private-randomized-weighted-majority; thm-private-online-learning-regret-under-composition. **Proof route:** derive the utility gap for finite-class exponential selection, then apply a two-sided uniform convergence bound and compare to the population ERM; for online regret use the log-partition potential, prove each round is a sensitivity-one exponential-mechanism selection from prior cumulative losses, apply the general-adjacency advanced-composition supplier under AC with the three explicit eta caps above, and prove the nonanticipating loss martingale tail with predictable intervals of length one. Retain the exact regret expression; the original unrestricted eta inference and big-O summary are corrected by the explicit proof in machine-learning-all-101-2026-10-19-owner-privacy-composition-repair/ml28-planning-correction.md. Keep empirical error, population error, privacy level, and computational cost separate. **Examples:** finite threshold class; a finite class too large to enumerate; one changing loss vector as the protected record. **Requires:** ML-27 — Differential privacy mechanisms and composition; ML-02 — Finite-class PAC learning and ERM guarantees for the Cat1 uniform-convergence result. No general computational-efficiency theorem is asserted. **Sources:** Dwork and Roth, The Algorithmic Foundations of Differential Privacy, Ch. 11 §11.1, printed pp. 217–221 (PAC/agnostic definitions, sensitivity-$1/n$ empirical loss, exponential-mechanism utility and sample-size calculation); §11.2, pp. 222–226 (Theorems 11.1–11.4, per-time-step adjacency, advanced composition, expected and high-probability regret); complete relevant theorem proofs read.


### ML-29 — Federated averaging, local SGD, and SCAFFOLD

**Pages:** ml02-federated-optimization / ml02-federated-optimization-examples. **Scope:** Define client objectives $F_i$ and the weighted deployment objective $f_{\rm wt}(w)=\sum_i(n_i/n)F_i(w)$; distinguish it from the equal-client objective $f(w)=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^N f_i(w)$ used by the convergence theorems below. Define FedAvg as uniform client sampling, multiple local updates, and server averaging; McMahan et al.’s article is algorithm/setup plus experiments, not a general convergence proof or privacy guarantee. State local SGD separately for a finite sum $f(x)=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^n f_j(x)$: $K$ workers independently sample $i_t^k\sim{\rm Unif}\{1,\ldots,n\}$, take stochastic gradient steps, and synchronize at a declared index set $I_T$, with maximum gap $H=\operatorname{gap}(I_T)$. Under $L$-smooth, $\mu$-strongly convex $f$, uniform-sampling gradient variance $\mathbb E_i\|\nabla f_i(x)-\nabla f(x)\|^2\le\sigma^2$, second moment $\mathbb E_i\|\nabla f_i(x)\|^2\le G^2$, steps $\eta_t=4/(\mu(a+t))$, and $a>\max\{16L/\mu,H\}$, let $S_T=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(a+t)^2$ and $\hat x_T=(KS_T)^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^K\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(a+t)^2x_t^k$. Then
$$
\mathbb E[f(\hat x_T)-f^\star]\le\frac{\mu a^3\|x_0-x^\star\|^2}{2S_T}+\frac{4T(T+2a)\sigma^2}{\mu K S_T}+\frac{256TG^2H^2L}{\mu^2S_T}.
$$
For mini-batch size $b$, replace $\sigma^2$ by $\sigma^2/b$; the source’s variance-dominated linear-computation-speedup discussion gives $H=O(\sqrt{T/(Kb)})$ after suppressing constants depending on $L,\mu,\sigma^2,G^2$. This guarantee is for the displayed weighted average, not the last iterate or one-shot averaging. For the equal-client heterogeneous setting, define $(G,B)$-bounded gradient dissimilarity by $N^{-1}\sum_i\|\nabla f_i(x)\|^2\le G^2+B^2\|\nabla f(x)\|^2$, $B\ge1$; when each $f_i$ is convex, the source also permits $N^{-1}\sum_i\|\nabla f_i(x)\|^2\le G^2+2\beta B^2(f(x)-f^\star)$. Define $f_i$ bounded below collectively, $\beta$-smooth, and stochastic gradients $g_i(x)=\nabla f_i(x;\zeta_i)$ unbiased with within-client variance $\mathbb E\|g_i(x)-\nabla f_i(x)\|^2\le\sigma^2$; do not replace this with a variance bound across clients. In each FedAvg round sample uniformly a fixed-size subset of $S$ among $N$ clients, perform $K$ local steps of size $\eta_l$, and average updates using global step $\eta_g$. Under the source’s $(G,B)$-dissimilarity, $\beta$-smoothness, stochastic-gradient assumptions and $ \eta_g\ge\sqrt S,\ \eta_l\le[(1+B^2)6\beta K\eta_g]^{-1}$, state its strongly-convex communication rate
$$
\widetilde O\!\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{\mu KS\epsilon}+\frac{(1-S/N)G^2}{\mu S\epsilon}+\frac{\sqrt\beta G}{\mu\sqrt\epsilon}+\frac{B^2\beta}{\mu}\right),
$$
with the local functions $\mu$-strongly convex. If general-convex or nonconvex FedAvg rates are included, use their separate source A3 conditions and outputs rather than extending this line. Define SCAFFOLD with stateful client controls $c_i$, server control $c$, corrected local update $y_i\leftarrow y_i-\eta_l(g_i(y_i)-c_i+c)$, aggregate updates $\Delta x=S^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal S}(y_i-x)$ and $\Delta c=S^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal S}(c_i^+-c_i)$, then server updates $x\leftarrow x+\eta_g\Delta x$, $c\leftarrow c+(S/N)\Delta c$; its control update has the source’s Option I $c_i^+=g_i(x)$ or Option II $c_i^+=c_i-c+(K\eta_l)^{-1}(x-y_i)$. For Theorem III use the source’s equal-client objective, uniform fixed-size client sampling, $f$ bounded below, $\beta$-smooth client functions and within-client unbiased variance bound $\sigma^2$, with no gradient-dissimilarity assumption; convex regimes additionally require source A3 ($\mu$-convex $f_i$, $\mu>0$ for strong convexity and $\mu=0$ for general convexity). With $\eta_g=\sqrt S$, the source’s local-step limits and round rates are: strongly convex,
$$ \eta_l\le\min\!\left\{\frac1{81\beta K\eta_g},\frac{S}{15\mu NK\eta_g}\right\},\quad R=\widetilde O\!\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{\mu KS\epsilon}+\frac{\beta}{\mu}+\frac NS\right); $$
general convex, $\eta_l\le(81\beta K\eta_g)^{-1}$ and
$$
R=\widetilde O\!\left(\frac{\sigma^2D^2}{KS\epsilon^2}+\frac{\beta D^2}{\epsilon}+\frac{NF}{S}\right);
$$
nonconvex, $\eta_l\le(24K\eta_g\beta)^{-1}(S/N)^{2/3}$ and
$$
R=O\!\left(\frac{\beta\sigma^2F}{KS\epsilon^2}+\left(\frac NS\right)^{2/3}\frac{\beta F}{\epsilon}\right).
$$
Here $D=\|x_0-x^\star\|_2$, $F=f(x_0)-f^\star$; convex branches target expected objective suboptimality, and the nonconvex branch targets expected squared-gradient stationarity of the source output, not objective error. The local stepsize/global-scale and output convention must be kept with each branch. SCAFFOLD Theorem IV’s extra benefit from bounded Hessian dissimilarity is only proved for quadratic client functions; do not generalize it to arbitrary smooth losses. Client data staying local is not a DP theorem, and weighted-count objectives do not inherit these equal-client rates without a separate weighted proof. Proposed items: def-federated-objective-and-client-sampling; alg-fedavg; alg-local-sgd; thm-local-sgd-convex-round-complexity; alg-scaffold; thm-fedavg-under-gradient-dissimilarity; thm-scaffold-convex-and-nonconvex-rates. **Proof route:** for local SGD, introduce the virtual synchronized sequence, bound minibatch variance and local drift, and telescope the weighted iterate inequality; carry the exact $a,H$, second-moment, variance and average-output conditions. For FedAvg/SCAFFOLD, reproduce the one-round progress and drift/variance bounds with uniform client sampling, then telescope with the source step sizes; the nonconvex output is a uniform random server iterate when applying the squared-gradient descent bound. Preserve equal-client versus weighted objectives and do not infer optimizer convergence from empirical FedAvg performance. **Examples:** two clients with shifted local optima; SCAFFOLD’s $c_i,c$ correction; one local step versus periodic synchronization. **Requires:** ML-12 — Linear prediction, shrinkage, and sparse regression only for an example objective; ML-27 — Differential privacy mechanisms and composition for interpreting any privacy statement; published def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable. State convexity, strong convexity, smoothness, stochastic-gradient unbiasedness/variance and client-sampling hypotheses locally. No Category 3 result may depend on this pair. **Sources:** McMahan et al., “Communication-Efficient Learning of Deep Networks from Decentralized Data,” §§1–4, Algorithm 1, printed pp. 1–12 (empirical FedAvg setup and weighted client aggregation); Stich, “Local SGD Converges Fast and Communicates Little,” §§2–3, Definition 2.1, Algorithm 1, Theorem 2.2, Corollary 2.3 and complete Appendix A, printed pp. 4–8 plus proof appendix (nonconvex and one-shot guarantees are explicitly excluded); Karimireddy et al., “SCAFFOLD: Stochastic Controlled Averaging for Federated Learning,” §§2–5, Algorithm 1, Theorems I–III, Appendix B assumptions A1–A5, D.2, E.1–E.2. The quadratic-only Theorem IV and its restriction were read; no generalization is claimed.

### ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality

**Pages:** feedforward-networks-and-universal-approximation / feedforward-networks-and-universal-approximation-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite directed acyclic computation graph and layered MLP specialization, then define the single-hidden-layer realization x ↦ c+Σⱼaⱼσ(wⱼ·x+bⱼ). State Leshno et al.’s exact activation class: σ is locally essentially bounded and the closure of its discontinuity set has Lebesgue measure zero. For this broad class M, the affine-ridge span approximates continuous functions on every compact set in the Lebesgue essential-supremum seminorm iff σ is not an algebraic polynomial almost everywhere, as in the source’s Definition 5. Separately retain pointwise compact-uniform density for the subclass M_pt of actual representatives bounded at every point on every compact interval, with the same iff across all compact sets. The original broader pointwise-M reading is false, as proved by cex-pointwise-uniformity-fails-for-an-essentially-bounded-activation; retain its explicit H-plus-reciprocal-spikes witness. State the Lᵖ(μ) iff for nonzero compactly supported finite regular Borel μ≪dx and 1<p<∞. Use Borel representatives of raw networks equal Lebesgue-almost everywhere, preserving constant-input units exactly; prove existence and uniqueness of the resulting Lᵖ(μ) classes. State μ=0 separately: Lᵖ(0)={0} and every activation gives density, including polynomial activations, as cex-zero-measure-does-not-force-a-nonpolynomial-activation proves. Positivity of an arbitrary atomic measure does not replace absolute continuity. These are density/existence claims without a quantitative width bound; they imply neither trainability nor statistical generalization. Proposed items: def-feedforward-neural-network; def-single-hidden-layer-network; def-activation-class-locally-essentially-bounded-ae-continuous; thm-single-hidden-layer-universal-approximation. **Proof route:** polynomial activations produce bounded-degree polynomials and fail density. For smooth nonpolynomial activations use finite differences of translates to generate monomials and Weierstrass. For rough activations approximate convolutions by Riemann sums of translates; if all convolutions were polynomial, Baire category yields a common degree bound and a distributional derivative identity forcing σ polynomial a.e. Leshno cite, but do not prove, ridge-function density; discharge it locally by Stone–Weierstrass applied to the polynomial algebra on each compact set and polarization of homogeneous polynomials into powers of linear forms. Retain the convolution, common-degree, and distributional-derivative argument for the merely locally essentially bounded activation class: first prove the Riemann-sum approximation for M_pt, then replace σ by zero on its closed null discontinuity set to transfer approximants for M in essential supremum and Lᵖ. For zero input weights absorb the exact constant values into the output bias; nonzero affine pullbacks of the exceptional null set are null. Prove the polynomial obstruction both on a segment in pointwise norm and on a full cube in essential supremum. The positive AC Lᵖ obstruction uses the proper closed finite-dimensional bounded-degree polynomial subspace. **Examples:** nonpolynomial ReLU/sigmoid; polynomial activation with bounded-degree obstruction; offset-free sine units remain odd and cannot approximate even functions. Distinguish pointwise compact-uniform and Lebesgue essential-supremum topologies; neither means uniform approximation on all ℝᵈ; offsets are essential in general. **Requires:** thm-real-stone-weierstrass-general for compact polynomial approximation; the polarization/ridge-density argument is local to this pair. No Category 3 dependency. **Sources:** Leshno, Lin, Pinkus, and Schocken, NYU Working Paper IS-92-13 (1992), complete source text read: Definitions 3–5, §4 Theorem 1, §5 offset example, and complete §6 proof Steps 1–7. This is the author working-paper text of the result later published in Neural Networks 6 (1993); Goodfellow, Deep Learning, §6.4.1 is an overview only, not the proof supplier.

### ML-31 — ReLU approximation rates, depth separation, and linear regions

**Pages:** relu-approximation-rates-and-depth-expressivity / relu-approximation-rates-and-depth-expressivity-examples. **Scope:** Define ReLU networks and connected activation-pattern regions. State separate results, not a single universal “depth helps” claim. Yarotsky’s target-independent rate: for the unit ball Wⁿ,∞([0,1]ᵈ), sup error ε is achieved with depth at most c(log(1/ε)+1) and at most c ε⁻ᵈ⁄ⁿ(log(1/ε)+1) weights and computation units, c=c(d,n). His target-dependent unit-Lipschitz one-dimensional construction instead uses depth 6 and at most c/[ε log(1/ε)] weights/connections/units. Keep the parameter-selection conditions on the approximation lower bounds: Ω(ε⁻ᵈ⁄ⁿ) under continuous architecture-parameter selection; arbitrary selection gives Ω(ε⁻ᵈ⁄²ⁿ), strengthened to Ω(ε⁻ᵈ⁄ⁿ log⁻²ᵖ⁻¹(1/ε)) at depth O(logᵖ(1/ε)); fixed depth L≥3 has curvature lower bound Ω(ε⁻¹⁄²⁽ᴸ⁻²⁾) for each nonlinear C² target. Telgarsky’s fixed gate-class separation uses a target with 2k³+8 layers, 3k³+12 nodes and 4+d distinct parameters whose L¹([0,1]ᵈ) distance from any at-most-k-layer semi-algebraic network with at most 2^{k/(tαβ)} nodes, or a sum of at most t decision trees with at most 2^{k³/t} nodes each, is ≥1/64; retain the gate class parameters (t,α,β). Serra et al.’s region upper bound for widths n₁,…,n_L and input dimension n₀ is Σ_(j∈J) Π_ℓ binom(nℓ,jℓ), with J={0≤jℓ≤min(n₀,n₁−j₁,…,nℓ₋₁−jℓ₋₁,nℓ)}; it is tight for one hidden layer. Lower bounds include the exact one-dimensional product Πℓ(nℓ+1) when all widths nℓ≥3 and, when every nℓ≥3n₀, the product/arrangement lower bound $\prod_{\ell=1}^{L-1}(\lfloor n_\ell/n_0\rfloor+1)^{n_0}\sum_{j=0}^{n_0}\binom{n_L}{j}$. Region count is an expressivity proxy, not accuracy or generalization. Proposed items: def-relu-network; def-relu-linear-region; thm-yarotsky-fixed-architecture-sobolev-approximation-rate; thm-yarotsky-adaptive-one-dimensional-lipschitz-approximation; thm-yarotsky-approximation-lower-bounds; thm-telgarsky-depth-separation-l1; thm-relu-linear-region-upper-bound; thm-relu-linear-region-lower-bounds. **Proof route:** Yarotsky partitions the cube, approximates local Taylor polynomials, and implements products with repeated-tent-map approximate squaring; grid-cell count gives ε⁻ᵈ⁄ⁿ and precision yields logarithmic overhead. The adaptive construction caches quantized local residual shapes. Lower bounds use continuous parameter selection, VC/shattering, or restriction to a line and fixed curvature. Telgarsky composes sawtooth maps and counts crossings by shallow semi-algebraic gates; PDF page 2 was visually checked for the exponent. Serra recursively counts hyperplane cuts in each activation region; lower bounds compose one-dimensional zigzags and a general-position final arrangement. **Examples:** tent-map composition; same target with target-dependent vs target-independent architecture; region-rich networks without a learning guarantee. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for network notation; finite-dimensional approximation and hyperplane-arrangement counting proved or cited locally. No learning-04 optimization or training theorem is implied. **Sources:** Yarotsky, “Error bounds for approximations with deep ReLU networks,” Theorems 1–6 and proofs; Telgarsky, “Benefits of depth in neural networks,” Theorem 1.1 and §3.4; Serra, Tjandraatmadja, and Ramalingam, “Bounding and Counting Linear Regions of Deep Neural Networks,” Theorems 1, 7, 8, Corollary 3 and appendices. DeVore–Hanin–Petrova was read only in a partial survey range and is context/cross-check, not proof evidence.

### ML-32 — Convolution and finite-group equivariance

**Pages:** convolution-and-group-equivariance / convolution-and-group-equivariance-examples. **Scope:** Define discrete convolutional layers with local receptive fields, channel mixing, tied weights, stride and padding; note that common software calls cross-correlation “convolution.” Define group actions $T_g,T'_g$ and equivariance $Φ(T_gx)=T'_gΦ(x)$. For a discrete group $G$, define $[f\star ψ](g)=∑_{h∈G}f(h)ψ(g^{-1}h)$ when the sum is finite or absolutely convergent, with left action $(L_uf)(g)=f(u^{-1}g)$; the first layer takes $f$ on $ℤ^2$ and outputs on $G$, and later layers take both feature maps and filters on $G$. Prove correlation equivariance by the left-invariant counting-measure reindexing $h=uk$, not by assuming that the generally infinite p4/p4m group is finite. Cover translation equivariance of standard discrete convolution and Cohen–Welling’s p4 (integer translations and quarter-turns) and p4m (plus reflections) constructions with orientation-indexed features and group convolution across layers. Pointwise nonlinearities commute with the feature-map action; pooling without subsampling preserves equivariance, while subgroup/coset pooling can reduce to subgroup equivariance or invariance. Exact finite-grid equivariance assumes a compatible lattice and boundary convention; cropping, padding, or stride may break symmetry. No CNN universal-approximation or empirical-superiority theorem is claimed. Proposed items: def-discrete-convolutional-layer; def-group-action-on-feature-maps; def-equivariant-neural-layer; def-group-convolution; thm-group-convolution-equivariance. **Proof route:** state left/right action convention; reindex a finite or absolutely convergent group sum by left translation; verify the first image-to-group layer and group-to-group layers; state how the finite-grid implementation inherits the action. **Examples:** translation-equivariant layer with zero padding breaking boundary symmetry; p4 vs p4m orientation channels; pooling that preserves or reduces the symmetry group. **Requires:** published def-group-action and def-equivariant-map-of-group-actions for the action and intertwining notation. These support algebraic equivariance, not universal approximation or benchmark claims.

### ML-35 — Attention, transformer universality, and sequence limits

**Pages:** attention-and-transformer-expressivity / attention-and-transformer-expressivity-examples. **Scope:** Define scaled dot-product attention softmax(QKᵀ/√dₖ)V, multi-head attention, transformer encoder/decoder layers, residual/feed-forward blocks, positional encoding, causal masks, and forward-pass algorithm. Without positions, unmasked self-attention and tokenwise feed-forward maps are permutation equivariant. State Yun et al.’s fixed-length theorem: for fixed sequence length n and embedding dimension d, continuous compactly supported permutation-equivariant maps ℝᵈˣⁿ→ℝᵈˣⁿ are approximable in integrated Lebesgue d_p error, any 1≤p<∞, by compositions with two heads, head size one, and tokenwise ReLU FFN width four; trainable positional embeddings extend the theorem to continuous maps on compact domains without permutation restriction. It is an existence result with potentially exponential construction size and no layer normalization in the theorem’s block. Separately state Hahn’s hard/soft attention limits. For softmax, fix the parameter matrices and layer count independently of sequence length, require uniformly bounded token and positional embeddings and Lipschitz ReLU-MLP positionwise/prediction maps; changing one nonfinal input symbol then changes the last-layer output by O(1/n), with constants depending on the matrices and layer count. The source’s parity next-token distribution is generated by the two-state automaton with fixed p∈(0,1), which terminates with probability p when the number of 1s is even and otherwise emits 0 or 1 with equal probability; its cross-entropy tends to unigram chance level. Its 2DYCK distribution is the PCFG with fixed p∈(0,1), S→(S)S and S→[S]S each of probability p/2 and S→ε of probability 1−p; its next-token cross-entropy stays a positive constant above optimum. This is not an exact-classification impossibility theorem. These asymptotic limits do not contradict fixed-length universality with growing resources.
