### ML-38 — Nonconvex gradient descent and stationarity

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** The historical missing-descent claim is stale: reuse published `alg-gradient-descent` and `lem-l-smoothness-descent-inequality`; retain the local nonconvex summation/stationarity corollary. AD is an optional implementation supplier, not a logical prerequisite to the mathematical GD theorem. The fixed positive step bound is essential to deduce vanishing gradients from a finite sum.

**Pages:** nonconvex-gradient-descent-and-stationarity / nonconvex-gradient-descent-and-stationarity-examples. **Scope:** Define gradient descent and prove a stationarity result for an L-smooth objective bounded below along its iterates, with fixed step $0<\alpha\le1/L$: the descent lemma gives $F(w_{t+1})\le F(w_t)-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\nabla F(w_t)\|^2$, so telescoping yields a finite sum of squared gradient norms and hence $\nabla F(w_t)\to0$. This is not convergence to a global or local minimum. Proposed items: alg-gradient-descent; thm-smooth-bounded-below-gd-stationarity. **Proof route:** use the L-smooth descent inequality at each update, telescope objective decrease, and invoke the lower bound. State the step threshold and whether the theorem concludes vanishing gradients or a finite-horizon minimum-gradient bound. **Examples:** saddle point with zero gradient; nonconvex objective with multiple basins; objective unbounded below outside the iterate path. **Requires:** ML-37 — Automatic differentiation and backpropagation for gradient evaluation; published `thm-chain-rule-for-total-derivatives`, `def-jacobian-matrix-and-gradient`, `thm-gradient-represents-directional-derivatives-and-steepest-ascent`, and `thm-cauchy-schwarz-and-the-euclidean-norm` supply only the derivative, gradient, and inner-product clauses. Use the now-published `lem-l-smoothness-descent-inequality` under the stated L-Lipschitz-gradient premise. No Category 3 optimization theorem is required. **Sources:** Bottou, Curtis, and Nocedal, “Optimization Methods for Large-Scale Machine Learning,” Algorithm 4.1 and smoothness discussion, pp. 21–24. Global optimality is not supported.

### ML-39 — Stochastic gradient descent and random-iterate guarantees

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `def-stochastic-gradient-oracle-and-unconstrained-update`, `thm-stochastic-gradient-nonconvex-randomized-iterate-bound`, `thm-stochastic-gradient-biased-noise-floor`, and the convex/projected stochastic-gradient results for their exact oracle regimes. The published constant-step theorem has a global finite lower bound, square-integrable conditional oracle, AC and an independent uniform output index; it gives E||grad f(x_R)||^2 <= 2 Delta/(alpha N)+L alpha sigma^2 for 0<alpha<=1/L. It does not supply the more general alignment/noise contract or Ghadimi–Lan nonuniform output weighting retained above: those broader promised results still need separate local proofs.

**Pages:** stochastic-gradient-descent-for-learning / stochastic-gradient-descent-for-learning-examples. **Scope:** Define SGD/minibatch SGD and state stochastic-direction assumptions separately from unbiased SGD. Bottou–Curtis–Nocedal assume continuously differentiable F with L-Lipschitz gradient, lower bounded along iterates, expected direction positively aligned with ∇F and norm-comparable, conditional variance ≤M+M_V||∇F||². For $0<\alpha\le\mu/(LM_G)$, the fixed-step theorem gives $E[T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^T\|\nabla F(w_t)\|^2]\le\alpha LM/\mu+2(F(w_1)-F_{\inf})/(T\mu\alpha)$; for positive steps with $\sum_t\alpha_t=\infty$ and $\sum_t\alpha_t^2<\infty$, the source’s weighted expected average squared gradient tends to zero. Unbiased SGD is the μ=μ_G=1 case. Ghadimi–Lan separately assume $f\in C_L^{1,1}$, finite $f^*$, and a conditionally unbiased first-order oracle with conditional variance at most $\sigma^2$. Their RSG output obeys $P(R=k)=(2\gamma_k-L\gamma_k^2)/\sum_{j=1}^N(2\gamma_j-L\gamma_j^2)$ and $L^{-1}E\|\nabla f(x_R)\|^2\le[D_f^2+\sigma^2\sum_k\gamma_k^2]/\sum_k(2\gamma_k-L\gamma_k^2)$ for $0<\gamma_k<2/L$, where $D_f^2=2(f(x_1)-f^*)/L$. Its constant-step choice gives an $O(N^{-1}+N^{-1/2})$ bound. None guarantees a global minimum. Proposed items: alg-stochastic-gradient-descent; alg-minibatch-stochastic-gradient-descent; thm-stochastic-direction-expected-average-stationarity; thm-nonconvex-sgd-random-iterate-stationarity. **Proof route:** condition on the filtration, apply the smoothness descent inequality, use alignment and variance bounds, sum the recursion, and choose or weight the output iterate as in the theorem. Keep empirical-risk sampling distinct from population-risk sampling. **Examples:** unbiased bounded-variance gradient; noise floor at constant step; biased direction violating alignment. **Requires:** ML-38 — Nonconvex gradient descent and stationarity; ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization for sample-risk notation; published `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, `def-filtration-and-filtered-probability-space`, `def-adapted-and-integrable-stochastic-process`, `def-conditional-expectation-as-an-ae-class`, `thm-tower-property-of-conditional-expectation`, and `def-martingale-difference-sequence` for the conditional oracle/filtration conventions (carry the AC premise on conditional-expectation interfaces). The alignment and conditional-moment assumptions remain local and do not follow from unbiasedness unless separately stated. **Sources:** Bottou et al., Assumptions 4.1/4.3, Lemmas 4.2/4.4, Theorems 4.8–4.10, pp. 21–34; Ghadimi and Lan, Assumption A1, RSG algorithm, Theorem 2.1 and proof, §§1–2.1. No global-minimum result is claimed.

### ML-40 — Momentum and quadratic acceleration

**Pages:** momentum-and-accelerated-training / momentum-and-accelerated-training-examples. **Scope:** Define heavy-ball by $w_{t+1}=w_t-\alpha\nabla F(w_t)+\beta(w_t-w_{t-1})$ with $w_0=w_1$, and Nesterov acceleration separately by $\tilde w_t=w_t+\beta_t(w_t-w_{t-1})$, $w_{t+1}=\tilde w_t-\alpha_t\nabla F(\tilde w_t)$. Prove only the tuned heavy-ball result for $F(w)=\tfrac12(w-w^*)^TA(w-w^*)$ with symmetric positive-definite $A$ whose spectrum lies in $[c,L]$. The source parameters are $\alpha=4/(\sqrt L+\sqrt c)^2$ and $\beta=((\sqrt L-\sqrt c)/(\sqrt L+\sqrt c))^2$; with $\kappa=L/c$ the worst-case asymptotic spectral factor is $(\sqrt{\kappa}-1)/(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)$, versus $(\kappa-1)/(\kappa+1)$ for steepest descent with step $2/(L+c)$. Treat these as linear convergence factors from the scalar recurrences, not a claimed one-step Euclidean contraction; at endpoint eigenvalues heavy ball can have a repeated characteristic root. This spectral statement does not imply a general stochastic momentum rate; the cited review says such guarantees for general learning objectives remain elusive. Proposed items: alg-heavy-ball-momentum; alg-nesterov-accelerated-gradient; thm-heavy-ball-rate-on-strongly-convex-quadratics. **Proof route:** diagonalize the quadratic Hessian and analyze the scalar second-order recurrence for each eigenvalue; choose parameters and maximize the worst-mode contraction. Do not transfer the result to nonquadratic nonconvex training. **Examples:** quadratic spectrum with optimized parameters; unstable parameter choice; stochastic momentum experiment as empirical evidence only. **Requires:** ML-38 — Nonconvex gradient descent and stationarity for update notation; published `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` for the finite-dimensional symmetric Hessian eigenbasis and `prop-gradient-of-a-quadratic-matrix-form` for the quadratic gradient/Hessian (factor a positive-semidefinite Hessian using its spectral decomposition, or prove the quadratic derivative directly). The scalar recurrence and parameter optimization are local. No generic Nesterov rate is proposed from this source. **Sources:** Bottou et al., §§6.5 and 7.1, pp. 64–70; checked local spectral and matrix-differentiation suppliers. General stochastic-momentum guarantees are explicitly excluded.

### ML-41 — Adaptive gradient algorithms and regret limits

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `alg-adam-update`, `alg-amsgrad-update`, `alg-diagonal-adagrad-online-regret`, `thm-diagonal-adagrad-regret-bound`, and `cex-adam-periodic-linear-losses-force-divergence`. The published diagonal AdaGrad theorem uses a nonempty closed convex domain, finite coordinate diameters D_j, convex losses/subgradients, eta>0, epsilon>0 and s_Tj=sqrt(epsilon^2+sum_t g_tj^2); regret <= sum_j (D_j^2/(2 eta)+eta)s_Tj. RMSProp and the source-matched decreasing-beta AMSGrad theorem remain local additions. Do not present one particular periodic counterexample as proof of every beta/epsilon branch.

**Pages:** adaptive-gradient-optimizers / adaptive-gradient-optimizers-examples. **Scope:** Give separate algorithm definitions for AdaGrad, RMSProp, Adam, and AMSGrad. Reddi–Kale–Kumar’s counterexample is a one-dimensional online convex sequence on [−1,1] where Adam has nonzero average regret; the failure extends to any fixed $\beta_1,\beta_2\in[0,1)$ with $\beta_1<\sqrt{\beta_2}$, and for each fixed $\epsilon>0$ the Adam update with that denominator modification still admits a nonzero-average-regret example. Their AMSGrad replaces the second-moment denominator by its coordinatewise historical maximum. State its regret theorem under bounded domain diameter, bounded coordinate gradients, α_t=α/√t and $\beta_{1t}\le\beta_1$, $\gamma=\beta_1/\sqrt{\beta_2}<1$; the $O(\sqrt T)$ corollary needs a schedule such as $\beta_{1t}=\beta_1\lambda^{t-1}$ for $0\le\lambda<1$ or $\beta_{1t}=\beta_1/t$. Constant β₁ used in practice is not that corollary. These are online convex-regret results, not nonconvex neural loss convergence. Proposed items: alg-adagrad; alg-rmsprop; alg-adam; alg-amsgrad; thm-adam-online-regret-counterexample; thm-amsgrad-online-convex-regret. **Proof route:** display each moment/denominator recursion; use the source’s explicit one-dimensional loss sequence to evaluate Adam’s regret; for AMSGrad, bound the adaptive potential with monotone denominator and the specified β schedule. Keep algorithm pseudocode apart from guarantees. **Examples:** Adam counterexample; AMSGrad historical maximum; constant β₁ outside the proved schedule. **Requires:** ML-39 — Stochastic gradient descent and random-iterate guarantees for update/noise notation; online-convex potential and bounded-gradient inequalities proved locally. Do not depend on the later online-decision pair. **Sources:** Reddi, Kale, and Kumar, “On the Convergence of Adam and Beyond,” Algorithms 1–2, Theorems 1–4 and proof appendices A, D; Theorem 6 and Appendix F for the fixed-$\epsilon$ Adam counterexample; Bottou et al., adaptive/momentum discussion. The AMSGrad theorem’s decreasing-$\beta_{1t}$ condition is explicit; constant-$\beta_1$ experiments are not its $O(\sqrt T)$ corollary.

### ML-42 — Initialization and signal propagation

**Pages:** initialization-and-signal-propagation / initialization-and-signal-propagation-examples. **Scope:** Define rectifier variance-preserving initialization and derive the ReLU moment recurrence under layerwise independent identically distributed weights whose law is symmetric about zero, independent identically distributed layer inputs independent of the weights, symmetric preactivations with no atom at zero, and zero biases. For the backward recurrence additionally assume each layer weight is independent of its incoming gradient and the ReLU gate is independent of the downstream gradient; zero mean alone does not imply the required symmetry. At fan-in n_l, Var(y_l)=½n_l Var(w_l)Var(y_{l−1}); choosing Var(w_l)=2/n_l prevents this simplified forward second moment from gaining an exponential layer factor. The backward recurrence uses fan-out; the two choices need not both hold in arbitrary architectures. For PReLU slope $a$, the forward factor is $2/((1+a^2)n_l)$ and the corresponding backward factor uses fan-out $\hat n_l$, namely $2/((1+a^2)\hat n_l)$. This is a moment calculation, not a theorem that training succeeds or generalizes. Proposed items: alg-rectifier-variance-preserving-initialization; thm-relu-forward-variance-recurrence; thm-relu-backward-variance-recurrence; cor-prelu-variance-preserving-scale. **Proof route:** condition on the previous layer, use symmetry and independence to compute second moments and the ReLU half-moment; for backpropagation separately use independence of weights from incoming gradients and of gates from downstream gradients. Propagate the forward/backward recurrences and state finite-moment and independence assumptions at every layer. **Examples:** fan-in versus fan-out choice; nonzero bias or correlated weights violating recurrence; PReLU slope. ImageNet depth and accuracy comparisons remain experiments. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for layer notation; published `def-independent-random-elements`, `thm-independent-random-elements-have-product-joint-law`, `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, and `def-moments-variance-and-covariance` for the exact independence and second-moment clauses. The layerwise ReLU/PReLU moment calculations are local; the moment identities do not justify independence or symmetry by themselves. **Sources:** He, Zhang, Ren, and Sun, “Delving Deep into Rectifiers,” §2.2 and forward/backward variance recurrences, pp. 3–5. Report its empirical depth/accuracy results only as experiments.

### ML-43 — Implicit bias of gradient-based learning

**Pages:** implicit-bias-of-gradient-training / implicit-bias-of-gradient-training-examples. **Scope:** Separate three regimes. For linear least squares, zero-initialized GD with 0<η≤1/λ_max(XᵀX) converges to the minimum-Euclidean-norm least-squares solution, including nonunique cases. For separable linear classification with logistic or exponential loss, full-batch GD with sufficiently small fixed step has loss→0, ||w_t||→∞ and normalized direction converging to the L₂ hard-margin SVM direction; the all-data theorem needs the source’s smooth decreasing loss with tight exponential tail, with local-smoothness step-size caveat for exponential loss. The sharper bounded-residual expansion is for almost every data set; degenerate cases can require iterated-log corrections. For an $L$-homogeneous classifier with exponential loss, Lyu–Li’s gradient-flow theorem assumes each output map is locally Lipschitz and admits a chain rule, and that the loss is below $1$ at some time $t_0$. Define the flow as a global absolutely continuous trajectory satisfying the Clarke differential inclusion $\dot\theta\in-\partial^\circ\mathcal L(\theta)$; its existence/continuation must be supplied or assumed explicitly. Do not choose a pointwise derivative at a ReLU kink and treat it as a classical vector field. The source’s smoothed normalized margin is nondecreasing after $t_0$, and every parameter-direction limit point is proportional to a KKT point of $\min \frac12\|\theta\|^2$ subject to all training margins $q_n(\theta)\ge1$. KKT is necessary, not sufficient for global maximum margin under these nonconvex constraints. The source treats discrete gradient descent separately and requires output smoothness on $\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}$ plus its small-step condition; ordinary ReLU does not satisfy that discrete theorem’s smoothness assumption. Proposed items: thm-zero-initialized-gd-selects-minimum-norm-least-squares; thm-separable-logistic-gd-selects-hard-margin-direction; thm-homogeneous-gradient-flow-limit-satisfies-margin-kkt. **Proof route:** linear least squares uses row-space invariance and spectral iteration; separable linear loss uses margin dynamics and the source’s tail conditions; for the homogeneous gradient-flow result, define the Clarke subdifferential and differential inclusion, explicitly assume or prove a global absolutely continuous solution exists, carry the locally Lipschitz/chain-rule premise, and reproduce the normalized-margin and KKT proof with the source constraint qualification. The discrete smooth-gradient-descent result is separate; do not substitute a pointwise ReLU derivative for the Clarke inclusion. The full Lyu–Li auxiliary margin proof and discrete-GD derivation were not fully audited; record a local proof-review obligation before authoring it. **Examples:** underdetermined linear system; degenerate separable data; homogeneous network whose KKT point is not global. **Requires:** Published `thm-a-plus-b-is-the-unique-minimum-norm-least-squares-solution` and `thm-least-squares-solutions-and-normal-equation` for the finite-dimensional least-squares selector; ML-37 — Automatic differentiation and backpropagation; ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for homogeneous model notation; published `def-subgradient-and-subdifferential` only for its convex subgradient clause. No published Clarke-subdifferential/differential-inclusion or global solution-existence interface was found; define the Clarke object locally or add an exact upstream supplier, and explicitly assume/prove global trajectory existence while carrying A1’s local-Lipschitz/chain-rule premise. The library’s `thm-lagrange-multipliers-for-regular-level-set-constraints` applies only to regular equality constraints and is not a complete supplier for the source’s nonconvex inequality KKT conclusion; derive the active-margin, multiplier-sign/complementarity and constraint-qualification argument locally, retaining the source’s MFCQ and model assumptions. **Sources:** Hastie et al., Proposition 1; Soudry et al., Assumptions 1–3, Theorem 3, Appendices A–C; Lyu and Li, Assumptions A1–A4/S1/S5, Theorem 4.4 and Appendix A.8/C.6 route. Keep each optimizer/loss regime separate; no test-risk claim follows.

### ML-44 — Overparameterized networks and training to interpolation

**Pages:** overparameterized-training-and-interpolation / overparameterized-training-and-interpolation-examples. **Scope:** State Du et al.’s finite-width, random-initialization result for the empirical quadratic loss $L(\theta)=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^n(f(\theta,x_i)-y_i)^2$. For the fully connected architecture, define $x^{(0)}=x$, $x^{(h)}=\sqrt{c_\sigma/m}\,\sigma(W^{(h)}x^{(h-1)})$, and $f(x,\theta)=a^Tx^{(H)}$; for the residual architecture define $x^{(1)}=\sqrt{c_\sigma/m}\,\sigma(W^{(1)}x)$, $x^{(h)}=x^{(h-1)}+\frac{c_{\rm res}}{H\sqrt m}\sigma(W^{(h)}x^{(h-1)})$ for $2\le h\le H$, and $f_{\rm res}(x,\theta)=a^Tx^{(H)}$, with $0<c_{\rm res}<1$ and $c_\sigma=(E_{Z\sim N(0,1)}[\sigma(Z)^2])^{-1}$. The random initialization has independent standard Gaussian entries in each weight matrix and output vector. Fully connected case: $\|x_i\|_2=1$, $|y_i|=O(1)$, activation satisfying the source’s Condition 3.1 ($|\sigma(0)|$ bounded, $\sigma$ Lipschitz, and $\sigma\prime$ Lipschitz) and Condition 3.2 (analytic and nonpolynomial), Gaussian initialization, and the source Gram matrix $K^{(H)}$ with $\lambda_0=\lambda_{\min}(K^{(H)})>0$. Theorem 5.1 requires $m=\Omega\!\left(2^{O(H)}\max\left\{n^4/\lambda_0^4,\ n/\delta,\ n^2\log(Hn/\delta)/\lambda_0^2\right\}\right)$ and $\eta=O(\lambda_0/(n^2 2^{O(H)}))$. The residual theorem uses a skip at each layer with $c_{\rm res}/(H\sqrt m)$ scaling and requires $m=\Omega\!\left(\max\left\{n^4H^6/\lambda_0^4,\ n^2/(\lambda_0^2H^2),\ n/\delta,\ n^2\log(Hn/\delta)/\lambda_0^2\right\}\right)$ and $\eta=O(\lambda_0H^2/n^2)$. Both give $L(\theta^{(k)})\le(1-\eta\lambda_0/2)^kL(\theta^{(0)})$ with probability at least $1-\delta$ over initialization. The residual architecture is not the standard every-two-layer ResNet block. Zero training loss is not test accuracy; ReLU is outside the smooth-activation theorem. Proposed items: thm-wide-network-gradient-descent-reaches-zero-training-loss; thm-residual-network-polynomial-width-training-convergence. **Proof route:** use concentration at Gaussian initialization to show the empirical neural tangent/Gram matrix remains positive definite in a neighborhood; bound each GD update to keep iterates in that neighborhood; obtain geometric loss contraction. Du et al.’s exact theorem and proof architecture were read, but low-level initialization/concentration estimates were not independently rederived; retain them as local proof obligations or import explicit lemmas. **Examples:** fully connected exponential depth dependence; one-skip-per-layer residual block; interpolation with no test guarantee. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for architecture notation; ML-37 — Automatic differentiation and backpropagation; ML-38 — Nonconvex gradient descent and stationarity; published `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms` and `thm-operator-norm-is-the-largest-singular-value` for finite Gram-matrix eigenvalue notation only. Published `thm-standard-gaussian-lipschitz-concentration` now supplies the one-sided bound P(f(Z)>=Ef(Z)+r)<=exp(−r^2/(2L^2)) for standard Gaussian Z and L-Lipschitz f, carrying AC, DC and Countable Choice. It does not itself supply Gram-eigenvalue persistence; reproduce Du et al.’s initialization concentration, perturbation and iterate-stability lemmas locally or import exact audited lemmas. **Sources:** Du et al., fully connected Theorem 5.1 and residual Theorem 6.1, §§3–6 and Appendix C proof lemmas. Do not silently extend the theorem to ReLU, standard ResNets, or population risk.

### ML-45 — Minimum-norm interpolation and double descent

**Pages:** minimum-norm-interpolation-and-double-descent / minimum-norm-interpolation-and-double-descent-examples. **Scope:** Define ridgeless minimum-norm least squares. Hastie et al.’s rigorous double-descent model has iid pairs y=xᵀβ+ε, independent centered noise variance σ², iid isotropic feature entries of mean zero/unit variance/finite 4+η moment, ||β||²=r² and p/n→γ. The almost-sure design-conditional risk is σ²γ/(1−γ) for γ<1 and r²(1−1/γ)+σ²/(γ−1) for γ>1. It diverges at γ=1, can decline on the overparameterized side as variance falls, and tends to null risk r² as γ→∞; it is not monotonically decreasing immediately for all SNR, and in the well-specified isotropic case the global minimum remains underparameterized. Anisotropy, misspecification, latent directions and ridge change the curve. Belkin et al.’s plots for RFF, two-layer networks, trees and ensembles and their small-norm mechanism are empirical. Their rigorous approximation theorem is noiseless: iid uniform points on compact cube, target in Gaussian RKHS, and any interpolant in that same RKHS; sup error is bounded by a fill-distance rate times ||h*||+||h||. It is not noisy deep-network generalization. Proposed items: def-ridgeless-minimum-norm-regression; thm-isotropic-ridgeless-risk-across-interpolation-threshold; thm-noiseless-rkhs-interpolation-fill-distance-bound; ex-double-descent-in-neural-and-tree-models. **Proof route:** use the high-dimensional covariance resolvent and Marchenko–Pastur limit for the two exact risk formulas; supply Marchenko–Pastur convergence, Bai–Yin extremal singular-value bounds and resolvent deterministic equivalents upstream. For the RKHS result, control interpolation error by fill distance and RKHS norms. Do not claim the random-matrix proof without those suppliers. **Examples:** risk blow-up at γ=1; overparameterized-side variance decline; empirical double-descent plots separated from theorem. **Requires:** thm-a-plus-b-is-the-unique-minimum-norm-least-squares-solution; RKHS interface from ML-14 — RKHS regularization, kernels, and support-vector machines; independent P-RMT-01 — Marchenko–Pastur law and sample-covariance edge bounds; P-RMT-02 — Sample-covariance resolvent deterministic equivalents. Place P-RMT-01 and P-RMT-02 in Category 1 or 2 before this pair. **Sources:** Hastie et al., minimum-norm definition, Proposition 1, Theorem 1 and Appendix C.2 proof; Belkin et al., §2 and Appendix A Theorem 1. The empirical mechanism is not a theorem.

### ML-46 — Neural tangent kernels and lazy training

**Pages:** neural-tangent-kernels-and-lazy-training / neural-tangent-kernels-and-lazy-training-examples. **Scope:** Define NTK as the sum over parameters of parameter-Jacobian outer products. For fully connected networks with the source’s 1/√width normalization, iid Gaussian initialization, and sequential hidden-width limits, the initialization NTK converges in probability to a deterministic recursive kernel. Under Lipschitz C² activation with bounded second derivative and a training direction whose integrated function-space norm remains bounded on each fixed time interval, the NTK stays uniformly close to that limit and function evolution is kernel gradient flow. On a finite data set, for least-squares loss (1/(2N)) sum_i ||f_i−y_i||², the kernel-flow generator is K/N and each mode decays as exp(−t lambda_i(K)/N); a positive-definite Gram matrix gives convergence to the target evaluation vector. More generally, for a differentiable convex finite-dimensional cost with locally Lipschitz gradient and an attained minimum, a fixed positive-definite kernel gives a global gradient flow converging to a minimizer; retain this claim only with a complete local proof of global existence, boundedness and convergence in the kernel-induced metric. Retain `prop-ntk-positive-definite-on-the-sphere` for every input dimension n0>=1, depth L>=2, source bias scale beta>0 and nonpolynomial Lipschitz activation: the limiting scalar NTK is strictly positive definite on every finite set of distinct unit-sphere points, including antipodal pairs and the two-point sphere. Duplicate observation indices do not give an invertible unquotiented Gram matrix. This is an infinite-width finite-time lazy regime, not a finite-width fixed-kernel theorem. Proposed items: def-neural-tangent-kernel; thm-sequential-infinite-width-ntk-limit; prop-ntk-positive-definite-on-the-sphere; thm-least-squares-ntk-mode-decay. **Proof route:** recursively take width limits of covariance and Jacobian-kernel sums; use the integrated direction bound to control kernel drift on fixed time intervals; solve finite-data kernel gradient flow by eigendecomposition. For sphere positivity, prove the four local intermediate suppliers in the normative [ML-46 NTK prerequisite module](machine-learning-all-101-2026-10-19-ntk-prerequisite-module.md): Gaussian-L2 Hermite completeness, the Hermite Gaussian covariance expansion, the biased all-positive power-series kernel criterion with its finite tensor/polynomial separation proof, and Gaussian activation strictness/PSD kernel recursion. Place them on this A page before the retained proposition; no unproved Schoenberg import is needed. **Examples:** mode-wise exponential decay; wide network in a fixed-time lazy regime; feature learning beyond assumptions. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality; ML-37 — Automatic differentiation and backpropagation; ML-14 — RKHS regularization, kernels, and support-vector machines for kernel notation; published `def-monic-probabilists-hermite-polynomials` and `lem-hermite-orthogonality-and-monomial-expansion` supply the Gaussian Hermite polynomial convention/orthogonality only. The missing Gaussian-L2 completeness and strict-sphere argument are now explicit owner-authorized local proof obligations on `neural-tangent-kernels-and-lazy-training` under that normative module. Existing A-page Fourier uniqueness/L2 and compact-interval absolute-continuity/FTC suppliers are already in the transitive page closure; the published B-page Hermite-function basis example is reading context only. No proof acceptance or coverage is asserted by this planning repair. **Sources:** Jacot, Gabriel, and Hongler, network normalization, Theorems 1–2, Appendix A.1–A.4 and least-squares section; the normative module records this repair’s actual Appendix A.4, MIT Lecture 24 and Gneiting Theorem 1 reading, source limits and exact alternate proof route. Sequential width and fixed-time conditions are essential.

### ML-47 — Mean-field training and feature learning

**Pages:** mean-field-training-and-feature-learning / mean-field-training-and-feature-learning-examples. **Scope:** Define a one-hidden-layer particle-measure model and Wasserstein gradient flow. Under Chizat–Bach’s Hilbert-space, smoothness, semiconvexity and growth assumptions, empirical particle gradient flows converge as particle count grows to a unique Wasserstein gradient flow. Their global-optimum result adds convex loss, positive homogeneity, Sard-type regularity, separating support initialization, and convergence of the flow or specified projected measure. This is continuous-time qualitative moving-feature behavior, not a finite-particle rate or global-convergence result for arbitrary finite-width SGD. Classical ReLU parameterization lacks the differentiability required for their flow theorem; their application uses signed-square reparameterization. Proposed items: def-particle-measure-model; def-wasserstein-gradient-flow; thm-many-particle-training-limit; thm-homogeneous-mean-field-global-minimizer-under-separation. **Proof route:** obtain compactness/tightness of particle measures and identify the limiting continuity equation; use uniqueness/stability of the Wasserstein gradient flow for the mean-field limit. For global minimization, use convexity in the measure, positive homogeneity, Sard-type regular values and the separating support premise, then carry the assumed flow convergence. **Examples:** moving particles/features vs fixed NTK; signed-square ReLU parameterization; failure of separation. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for the parameterized model; ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; published `def-hilbert-space`, `def-bochner-integrable-function`, `thm-bochner-integrability-criterion`, `thm-bounded-linear-maps-commute-with-bochner-integration`, and `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable` for the exact vector-integral and empirical-expectation interfaces; independent P-WGF-01 — Wasserstein metric and continuity-equation foundations; P-WGF-02 — Existence, uniqueness, and stability of Wasserstein gradient flows. The cited Bochner results do not provide Chizat–Bach’s feature-map regularity or growth assumptions. Put this foundation before Category 3 or explicitly add it to the Category 3 mathematical supplier plan; do not rely on this Category 4 pair as a prerequisite. **Sources:** Chizat and Bach, Assumptions 2.1/3.2/3.4, Theorems 2.6/3.3/3.5, Appendix B.3 and C.1–C.4. Experiments do not prove the limit or global optimum.

### ML-48 — Norm and margin generalization for neural networks

**Pages:** norm-and-margin-generalization-for-neural-networks / norm-and-margin-generalization-for-neural-networks-examples. **Scope:** Define spectral network complexity RA=(Πᵢρᵢ||Aᵢ||₂)(Σᵢ(||Aᵢᵀ−Mᵢᵀ||_(2,1)/||Aᵢ||₂)^(2/3))^(3/2). State Bartlett–Foster–Telgarsky’s simultaneous multiclass margin bound for iid examples, fixed Lipschitz nonlinearities with $\sigma_i(0)=0$, reference matrices $M_i$, empirical margin $\gamma>0$, sample input matrix $X$, nonzero layer spectral norms (zero layers give a constant network and can be treated separately), and width $W$ maximum over input/hidden/output. Bound is empirical margin loss plus Õ(||X||₂ RA log(W)/(γn)+√(log(1/δ)/n)). It is uniform over the stated class; it does not imply a particular optimizer obtains small norm or good margin. Proposed items: def-spectral-network-complexity; thm-spectral-margin-bound-for-multiclass-networks. **Proof route:** margin ramp-loss covering-number bound from the layerwise spectral/reference-matrix complexity, then uniformization and concentration; reproduce the exact covering estimate, margin ramp and width/log terms, not just the asymptotic shorthand. **Examples:** small empirical margin error but large norm complexity; effect of reference matrices; margin bound versus realized test error. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for network notation; ML-04 — Rademacher complexity and uniform generalization only for reused notation, not as a supplier of Bartlett–Foster–Telgarsky’s bound; published `cor-real-spectral-theorem-for-self-adjoint-endomorphisms`, `def-operator-norm-on-a-finite-dimensional-inner-product-space`, `thm-operator-norm-is-the-largest-singular-value`, and `thm-spectral-and-frobenius-norms-are-unitarily-invariant-with-singular-value-formulas-and-rank-comparison` for finite matrix norm/eigenvalue clauses. Prove the source-specific mixed $(2,1)$-norm and covering inequalities locally. **Sources:** Bartlett, Foster, and Telgarsky, complexity definition (1.2), Theorem 1.1, Lemma 3.1, Theorem 3.3 and Appendix A.7 proof. The proof is lengthy and some extracted appendix equations are imperfect; reproduce its assumptions and margin-ramp argument locally.

### ML-49 — Compression generalization for trained networks

**Pages:** compression-generalization-for-trained-networks / compression-generalization-for-trained-networks-examples. **Scope:** Define margin-preserving compression of a sampled classifier to q discrete parameters, each with r possible values. Let $\epsilon$ be the source’s coordinatewise score-distortion bound: a helper string is fixed before the sample, and the compressor has at most $q$ discrete parameters with at most $r$ values each and satisfies $\max_y|f(x)[y]-g_A(x)[y]|\le\epsilon$ on every training input. The source’s Theorem 2.1 claims $\widehat L_\epsilon(f)$, but its Appendix A.1 implication is missing a factor of two: coordinatewise score error $\epsilon$ only guarantees label preservation when the original margin exceeds $2\epsilon$. Repair the local item to state that, with probability at least $1-\delta$, the compressed classifier has population zero-one error at most $\widehat L_{2\epsilon}(f)+\sqrt{(q\log r+\log(1/\delta))/(2m)}$. Equivalently, a margin parameter $\gamma$ with bound $\widehat L_\gamma(f)$ requires coordinatewise distortion at most $\gamma/2$. It bounds the compressed classifier. It bounds the original network only if a separate all-domain loss-preservation argument is proved. Their fully connected/convolutional constructions require layer-stability/cushion hypotheses and are not needed for the general theorem. Proposed items: def-margin-preserving-compression; thm-finite-description-compression-bound; ex-compressed-classifier-vs-original-predictor. **Proof route:** count at most $r^q$ descriptions and use Hoeffding plus a union bound for their zero-one risks. For the sample compression step, show explicitly that a score perturbation at most $\epsilon$ in every coordinate preserves the label only on examples where the original margin exceeds $2\epsilon$; retain the helper-string independence and sample-compression premises. Keep $q$, $r$, confidence, and the corrected $2\epsilon$ margin threshold explicit. **Examples:** compressed classifier with changed predictions off the training set; layer cushion assumption; finite codebook. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality only for architecture examples; ML-02 — Finite-class PAC learning and ERM guarantees for the finite-class union bound. **Sources:** Arora, Ge, Neyshabur, and Zhang, Definitions 1–2 and Theorem 2.1, PDF p. 4; Appendix A.1 proof, PDF p. 19. The statement uses a coordinatewise $\gamma$ score error and margin loss $\widehat L_\gamma$, but the proof’s label-preservation implication is false at that factor; the local $2\epsilon$ repair above is required. The specialized neural construction was not fully proof-audited and is not claimed here.

### ML-50 — Algorithmic stability of stochastic training

**Pages:** algorithmic-stability-of-stochastic-training / algorithmic-stability-of-stochastic-training-examples. **Scope:** Define uniform stability and state Hardt–Recht–Singer’s expected generalization gap. For convex β-smooth L-Lipschitz per-example loss and SGM steps α_t≤2/β, ε≤(2L²/n)Σ_t α_t. For nonconvex training with sample size $n\ge2$, each loss lies in [0,1], is L-Lipschitz and β-smooth, with nonincreasing α_t≤c/t; their expected gap is at most (1+1/(βc))/(n−1)·(2cL²)^(1/(βc+1))·T^(βc/(βc+1)). It concerns randomized sample-selection updates and expected generalization; it does not say all SGD training generalizes. Proposed items: def-uniform-stability; thm-uniform-stability-implies-expected-generalization; thm-sgd-stability-convex-loss; thm-sgd-stability-nonconvex-loss. **Proof route:** couple neighboring data sets with the same update-index sequence; use smoothness to recurse on parameter separation and Lipschitz loss to bound output loss; average the replacement difference. Convex and nonconvex step restrictions and recursion are distinct. **Examples:** stability growth with number of steps; convex versus nonconvex schedule; a non-Lipschitz loss outside assumptions. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; ML-39 — Stochastic gradient descent and random-iterate guarantees; ML-37 — Automatic differentiation and backpropagation for update notation. **Sources:** Hardt, Recht, and Singer, Definitions 2.1/3.1–3.6, Theorems 2.2/3.8/3.12 and proofs. Bound is an expected generalization statement under the stated step and loss conditions.

### ML-51 — Self-supervised and contrastive representations
**Pages:** self-supervised-and-contrastive-representation-learning / self-supervised-and-contrastive-representation-learning-examples. **Scope:** Let $C$ be latent classes, $\rho$ a class law and $D_c$ a data law for each class. Define positive pairs by $D_{\rm sim}(dx,dx^+)=\mathbb E_{c\sim\rho}[D_c(dx)D_c(dx^+)]$ and negative samples by the marginal $D_{\rm neg}=\mathbb E_{c\sim\rho}D_c$. For the Arora et al. main theorems use one independent marginal negative per positive pair, $M$ iid triplets, a representation class $\mathcal F$ satisfying $\sup_x\|f(x)\|_2\le R$, the source hinge or logistic contrastive loss, and an attained empirical minimizer $\hat f\in\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal F}\widehat L_{\rm un}(f)$; if a minimum is not attained, state an approximate selector and derive its additive optimization tolerance separately. Define $\tau=\Pr_{c,c'\sim\rho}(c=c')<1$. The main paper’s average downstream binary task samples two classes iid from $\rho$ conditioned to be distinct and uses the source’s uniform class-label law; the guarantee is for average binary linear-classification loss and the class-mean classifier, not general multiclass top-1 accuracy. With the source’s vector Rademacher complexity $\mathcal R_S(\mathcal F)$, Theorem 4.1 states with probability at least $1-\delta$, simultaneously for all comparators $f\in\mathcal F$, $L^\mu_{\rm sup}(\hat f)\le [L_{\rm un}(f)-\tau]/(1-\tau)+G_M/(1-\tau)$, where $G_M=O(R\mathcal R_S(\mathcal F)/M+R^2\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/M})$ under the paper’s bounded/Lipschitz loss contract. For Theorem 4.5 define $L^\ne_{\rm un}(f)$ by conditioning the positive and negative classes to differ, $\nu(c)\propto\rho(c)^2$, $\Sigma_f(c)=\operatorname{Cov}_{x\sim D_c}(f(x))$, and $s(f)=\mathbb E_{c\sim\nu}[\sqrt{\|\Sigma_f(c)\|_2}\,\mathbb E_{x\sim D_c}\|f(x)\|_2]$. Then $L_{\rm sup}(\hat f)\le L^\mu_{\rm sup}(\hat f)\le L^\ne_{\rm un}(f)+c_0\tau s(f)/(1-\tau)+G_M/(1-\tau)$. The optional Corollary 5.1.1 additionally requires each class-conditional $f(X)$ to be $\sigma^2$-sub-Gaussian in every direction and the mean classifier to have the stated margin loss. For $0<\epsilon\le1$, its Lemma 5.1 uses $\gamma(f)=1+2R\sigma\sqrt{2\log R+\log(3/\epsilon)}$ when $R\ge1$, and $\gamma(f)=1+2R\sigma\sqrt{\log(3/\epsilon)}$ when $R<1$; preserve the source constant and margin-loss definition. SimCLR’s projection-head, augmentation, and benchmark results are empirical and do not prove universal invariance or multiclass transfer. **Proof route:** use Jensen to compare pairwise contrastive loss with the class-mean classifier, the supplement’s vector-contraction/Rademacher bound to pass empirical to population contrastive loss, then the class-collision decomposition $L_{\rm un}=\tau L^=_{\rm un}+(1-\tau)L^\ne_{\rm un}$ and the within-class covariance bound. The margin corollary needs the separate sub-Gaussian concentration lemma. Keep collision and within-class terms visible. **Examples:** positive-pair class collision; a high within-class covariance representation; an augmentation that erases a label-relevant feature; SimCLR projection-head results as reported evidence. **Requires:** ML-01 — Prediction problems, risks, and empirical minimization; ML-04 — Rademacher complexity and uniform generalization; existing probability law, expectation, iid product-law and bounded-variable interfaces. Carry the source’s loss boundedness/Lipschitz and measurable-selector conditions; no global minimum or multiclass extension is implied. **Sources:** Arora et al., main paper §§2–6, Theorems 4.1/4.5 and Corollary 5.1.1, PDF pp. 2–8; supplementary Appendix A.1–A.4, PDF pp. 1–10, complete supplement read. The latent-class result is conditional and does not prove the empirical ImageNet/multiclass claims. Chen et al. (SimCLR), §§2–4, PDF pp. 2–9 and appendix pp. 19–20; pp. 10–18 were not read.

### ML-52 — Reconstruction and masked autoencoders

**Pages:** reconstruction-and-masked-autoencoders / reconstruction-and-masked-autoencoders-examples. **Scope:** Define encoder/decoder reconstruction risk, denoising corruption, and the source MAE mask law: for a fixed patch grid, uniformly sample visible patches without replacement, encode only those patches without mask tokens, then append learned mask tokens and decode all patch positions. The target is original pixel values (with a separately tested normalized-pixel variant), and the per-example MSE is computed only on masked patches. Write the objective as its expectation over this mask law; because the encoder input depends on the mask, do not call it an unbiased estimate of a mask-independent full-image reconstruction risk. Reconstruction of a chosen target does not imply semantic sufficiency, identifiability, or useful downstream features. Proposed items: def-reconstruction-autoencoder; alg-masked-autoencoder-pretraining; ex-reconstruction-objective-shortcut. **Proof route:** expand the sampled masked-patch MSE and take its expectation under the specified corruption/mask law. No semantic-representation theorem is supported; MAE transfer and ablation claims are empirical. **Examples:** a constant or shortcut reconstruction under a poorly chosen target; the paper’s 224-by-224 ImageNet-1K setup with a 75% default mask and asymmetric encoder/decoder. **Requires:** ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for encoder/decoder notation; probability expectation. The reported MAE optimizer/training run is empirical and does not consume an SGD convergence theorem. **Sources:** He et al., §§1–4, PDF pp. 1–8 (main paper read; references pp. 9–10 not read). Report the stated image domain, masking ratio, architecture, training, and evaluation conditions with benchmark results.

### ML-53 — Variational autoencoders and pathwise training

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `thm-pathwise-simulation-gradient-is-unbiased-under-envelope` for derivative/integral interchange when its differentiable simulator, parameter neighborhood and common integrable derivative-envelope hypotheses hold; check these for the encoder reparameterization. A Gaussian base law alone does not justify interchange. Retain the VAE-specific decoder/encoder, ELBO substitution, and estimator-variance discussion.
**Pages:** variational-autoencoders-and-the-elbo / variational-autoencoders-and-the-elbo-examples. **Scope:** Define a latent-variable model with nonnegative joint-density slice $\gamma_\theta(z;x)=p_\theta(x,z)$ and variational conditional density $q_\phi(z\mid x)$. For each fixed $x$, require the evidence normalizer $Z_\theta(x)=\int\gamma_\theta(z;x)dz=p_\theta(x)$ to satisfy $0<Z_\theta(x)<\infty$, so $\pi_\theta(dz\mid x)=\gamma_\theta(z;x)dz/Z_\theta(x)$ is a proper posterior; require $q_\phi(\cdot\mid x)\ll\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)$ and $\mathbb E_q[|\log\gamma_\theta(Z;x)|+|\log q_\phi(Z\mid x)|]<\infty$. Then the exact identity is $\log p_\theta(x)=\operatorname{ELBO}(q_\phi;x)+\operatorname{KL}(q_\phi(\cdot\mid x)\|\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x))$. Consume BCS-22’s generic theorem only under these hypotheses. State pathwise differentiation separately: if $Z=g_\phi(\epsilon,x)$, the law of $\epsilon$ is parameter-independent, the pointwise integrand is $C^1$ in $\phi$, and on every compact parameter neighborhood its derivative is dominated by an integrable envelope, then dominated differentiation gives $\nabla_\phi\mathbb E_\epsilon[f(\phi,\epsilon,x)]=\mathbb E_\epsilon[\nabla_\phi f(\phi,\epsilon,x)]$. Neither maximizing this lower bound nor pathwise gradient validity implies posterior recovery, latent identifiability, or global optimization. Proposed items: def-variational-autoencoder; alg-reparameterized-variational-training; thm-reparameterization-gradient-under-dominated-differentiation; ex-vae-inference-gap. **Proof route:** expand the finite KL against the proper posterior for the ELBO identity; apply the chain rule pointwise and dominated convergence to difference quotients for the pathwise derivative. Explicitly carry positive finite normalizer, absolute continuity, finite absolute log-integrability, and parameter-local derivative envelope. **Examples:** Gaussian latent model with analytic posterior and restricted $q$; positive inference gap; posterior collapse as an example, not a universal theorem. **Requires:** BCS-22 — Variational inference and the ELBO: `def-variational-family-and-elbo` and `thm-elbo-kl-decomposition` under its exact normalizer, $q\ll\pi$, and log-integrability conditions; probability conditional-density and expectation interfaces; `thm-dominated-convergence`. This is a one-way Category 3 → Category 4 edge. **Sources:** Kingma and Welling, §§2–4, PDF pp. 2–7; complete 9-page PDF read. Their general “mild” pathwise conditions are not a complete theorem, so the scaffold states and proves its own dominated-differentiation version. No finite-network training guarantee is supplied.

### ML-54 — Energy models, score matching, and contrastive divergence

**Pages:** energy-models-score-matching-and-contrastive-divergence / energy-models-score-matching-and-contrastive-divergence-examples. **Scope:** With respect to a declared reference measure (Lebesgue measure for Hyvärinen’s continuous score identity), define $p_\theta(x)=\exp(-E_\theta(x))/Z_\theta$ only when $0<Z_\theta<\infty$; distinguish normalized likelihood gradients from score objectives. State Hyvärinen’s identity for a differentiable data density and locally C1 (or weakly differentiable) model score $\psi_\theta$, with $E_p\|\psi_\theta(X)\|^2$, $E_p\|\nabla\log p(X)\|^2$, and $E_p|\nabla\cdot\psi_\theta(X)|$ finite. The source’s pointwise boundary statement $p(x)\psi_\theta(x)\to0$ is recorded but is not used alone to justify a multivariate boundary limit; instead require compact-support integration by parts and use cutoffs $\chi_R=1$ on $B_R$, supported in $B_{2R}$, with $\|\nabla\chi_R\|_\infty=O(R^{-1})$. Define CD-k and identify the omitted derivative through the finite reconstruction chain. Do not state a general consistency theorem from the paper’s pointwise LLN sketch. Proposed items: def-energy-based-density-and-partition-function; thm-score-matching-integration-by-parts-identity; alg-contrastive-divergence-k; ex-short-run-contrastive-divergence-bias. **Proof route:** expand squared score error and integrate the cross term by parts with $\chi_R$. Since $E_p\|\psi_\theta\|^2<\infty$, $p\psi_\theta\in L^1$; hence the cutoff boundary term is bounded by $O(R^{-1})\int_{R\le\|x\|\le2R}p\|\psi_\theta\|dx\to0$. The remaining terms converge using the stated $p$-integrability and Cauchy–Schwarz for $p\nabla\log p\cdot\psi_\theta$. The resulting identity is $J(\theta)=E_p[\tfrac12\|\psi_\theta\|^2+\nabla\cdot\psi_\theta]+C$; the unknown normalizer vanishes after differentiation in x. For CD, compare the exact likelihood gradient with the finite-chain update and retain the chain-distribution derivative. Any consistency extension needs separate uniform convergence and argmin regularity. **Examples:** finite-state model with tractable partition function; short Gibbs chain that does not reach stationarity; nonintegrable energy. **Requires:** probability density, expectation, Markov-kernel, conditional-expectation, and measure-integration interfaces. MCMC may be taught from Category 3, but no Bayesian computation result is needed to establish the score identity or the finite-chain bias. **Sources:** Hyvärinen, §§2–4 and consistency discussion, PDF pp. 3–12, plus limitations pp. 13–15 (complete paper read); Hinton, PDF pp. 1–5 only for the likelihood-gradient and CD derivation (pp. 6–19 not read; old two-column extraction is noisy). Restrict Hinton claims to the read pages.

### ML-55 — GAN objectives and the ideal discriminator

**Pages:** generative-adversarial-networks / generative-adversarial-networks-examples. **Scope:** Define the generator/discriminator minimax objective. For densities with respect to a common dominating measure and an unrestricted discriminator, maximize pointwise to obtain D*G(x)=p_data(x)/(p_data(x)+p_G(x)) almost everywhere, then substitute to get C(G)=−log 4+2 JS(p_data∥p_G). Equality of the two laws is the ideal global distribution optimum. This does not establish convergence of finite neural alternating optimization. Proposed items: def-gan-minimax-objective; thm-optimal-discriminator-and-js-divergence; ex-neural-game-nonconvergence. **Proof route:** differentiate the binary cross-entropy integrand with respect to D(x), verify its maximizing value including zero-density boundary cases, and rewrite the optimized functional as the two KL terms defining Jensen–Shannon divergence. The paper’s distribution-space convergence proposition assumes unlimited capacity, an optimal discriminator at each generator step, and sufficiently small distribution-space updates; do not transfer those assumptions to neural gradient descent. **Examples:** separated supports with a saturated discriminator; a finite-parameter game whose updates cycle. **Requires:** probability densities and integrals; KL and convexity definitions if supplied by Category 3. Any training algorithm discussion consumes ML-39 — Stochastic gradient descent and random-iterate guarantees or ML-41 — Adaptive gradient algorithms and regret limits, but the ideal theorem itself does not. **Sources:** Goodfellow et al., §§2–4 and convergence discussion, PDF pp. 2–6; complete 9-page paper read. No practical GAN convergence or mode-collapse theorem is claimed.

### ML-58 — Neural ODEs and continuous normalizing flows

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Reuse published `thm-continuous-flow-log-density-equation`, `thm-global-error-from-one-step-defect-and-stability`, `thm-divergence-integral-error-with-separate-quadrature`, and `cor-global-log-density-error-from-state-and-quadrature`. Discrete adjoints use published `thm-discrete-adjoint-gradient-recursion` and finite-iteration sensitivity bounds; continuous/discrete gradient consistency is a distinct claim.

**Pages:** neural-odes-and-continuous-normalizing-flows / neural-odes-and-continuous-normalizing-flows-examples. **Scope:** Define $dot z=v_\theta(t,z)$. On a finite time interval, assume $v_\theta$ is jointly $C^\infty$ in time, state, and parameters and is uniformly globally Lipschitz in state, with a uniform bound at the origin on compact parameter neighborhoods. These hypotheses give a unique global forward and backward flow, a $C^1$ diffeomorphism in the initial state, and the parameter smoothness required by the named ODE supplier. If $p_t$ is the push-forward of a Lebesgue density by this flow, derive $\frac{d}{dt}\log p_t(z_t)=-\operatorname{tr}(D_zv_\theta(t,z_t))$. Define the adjoint sensitivity equation under the same smooth-flow and differentiable-loss hypotheses. Distinguish these exact continuous equations from numerical solver outputs and from gradients of a finite numerical computation; these sources give no general solver error bound. Proposed items: def-neural-ode; thm-instantaneous-change-of-variables-for-ode-flow; alg-adjoint-sensitivity-method; ex-numerical-adjoint-error. **Proof route:** use the published global ODE and smooth parameter-dependence results; the flow Jacobian $J_t$ solves $\dot J_t=D_zv_\theta(t,z_t)J_t$, $J_0=I$. It stays invertible, and Jacobi’s formula gives $\frac{d}{dt}\log|\det J_t|=\operatorname{tr}(D_zv_\theta)$. Apply the C¹ change-of-variables theorem with Countable Choice to $p_t(z_t)=p_0(z_0)/|\det J_t|$. Chen et al.’s appendix states the instantaneous formula but its abbreviated proof does not establish these differentiability and flow-Jacobian steps from Lipschitz continuity alone; author the stated local proof under these stronger hypotheses. If numerical accuracy is part of the course, use independent P-NUM-ODE-01 and P-NUM-ODE-02; exact ODE existence does not prove solver convergence. **Examples:** one-dimensional linear field with exact density evolution; stiff field where solver tolerance changes likelihood or adjoint gradient. **Requires:** thm-existence-and-uniqueness-of-a-maximal-ode-solution; cor-globally-lipschitz-odes-have-global-solutions; thm-smooth-dependence-of-ode-solutions-on-parameters; thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions; thm-determinant-differential-and-jacobis-formula. Carry the change-of-variables Countable Choice assumption. P-NUM-ODE-01 and P-NUM-ODE-02 are needed only for numerical-error guarantees and must be independent of Categories 3–4. **Sources:** Chen et al., §§2–3 and Appendix A/B, PDF pp. 2–9 and 13–16; Papamakarios et al., continuous-flow section, pp. 31–33. Neither source proves general integrator convergence.

### ML-59 — Discrete diffusion chains and DDPM objectives

**Pages:** discrete-diffusion-and-variational-objectives / discrete-diffusion-and-variational-objectives-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite forward Gaussian noising chain with $0<\beta_t<1$, $\alpha_t=1-\beta_t$, and $\bar\alpha_t=\prod_{s\le t}\alpha_s$, and a learned reverse Gaussian chain with every variance used in a density/log-likelihood term finite and strictly positive. For $t\ge2$, state the exact posterior variance $\tilde\beta_t=\beta_t(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})/(1-\bar\alpha_t)>0$; treat the $t=1$ reconstruction term as its separate learned reverse likelihood with positive variance, not as a Lebesgue density for the degenerate $q(x_0\mid x_1,x_0)$. Derive $q(x_t\mid x_0)$, $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t,x_0)$, and the variational lower-bound decomposition into endpoint and per-step KL terms. For each observed $x_0$, require the generative marginal $p_\theta(x_0)=\int p_\theta(x_{0:T})\,dx_{1:T}\in(0,\infty)$, define its proper posterior $\pi_\theta(x_{1:T}\mid x_0)$, and require $q(x_{1:T}\mid x_0)\ll\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x_0)$ with $\mathbb E_q[|\log q(x_{1:T}\mid x_0)|+|\log p_\theta(x_{0:T})|]<\infty$; these are the BCS-22 hypotheses for applying its ELBO/KL identity. State that the noise-prediction objective is a weighted ELBO term; the common $L_{\rm simple}$ drops weights and is not exact maximum-likelihood training. Separate Gaussian identities from learned score accuracy and empirical sample quality. Proposed items: def-forward-gaussian-diffusion-chain; thm-gaussian-forward-and-posterior-kernels; thm-ddpm-elbo-decomposition; alg-epsilon-prediction-diffusion-training. **Proof route:** repeated Gaussian marginalization gives the forward marginal; use the multivariate-normal conditional-law result for the posterior kernel; verify posterior propriety, $q\ll\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x_0)$, and finite absolute log-integrability before applying the exact BCS-22 decomposition $\log p_\theta(x_0)=\mathrm{ELBO}(q)+\mathrm{KL}(q\|\pi_\theta)$; simplify the finite-chain Gaussian terms algebraically to the weighted noise-prediction terms. Carry positive schedule variances through every Gaussian density formula. **Examples:** scalar Gaussian chain with analytic posterior; compare weighted ELBO objective with the unweighted simple loss. **Requires:** BCS-03 — Conjugate families and posterior prediction: thm-multivariate-normal-conditional-law (retain PSD joint covariance and positive-definite observed covariance conditions); BCS-22 — Variational inference and the ELBO: def-variational-family-and-elbo and thm-elbo-kl-decomposition, under its exact conditions $Z\in(0,\infty)$, $q\ll\pi$, and finite $q$-integrability of $|\log\gamma|+|\log q|$; published probability-law and expectation definitions. These are one-way Category 3 → Category 4 uses. **Sources:** Ho et al., §§2–4, PDF pp. 2–9, and appendix posterior/objective derivations, pp. 13–16. PDF pp. 10–12 and 17–25 were not read; no claim relies on them. Exact objective algebra is not a fidelity or sampler-convergence guarantee.

### ML-60 — Score SDEs and reverse-time generation

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** P-NUM-SDE-01/02 now have published carriers. Reuse their precise strong/weak rates, including the newer square-integrable-initial-data strong theorem under global state Lipschitz, linear growth, half-Hölder time regularity and an exact path-sup moment bound. The Higham–Mao–Stuart nonlinear theorem remains a separate all-moments regime; its hypotheses are not weakened. Reverse-time density/score regularity remains unmet under P-SDE-02 and the separate density addendum to P-SDE-01; neither global strong existence nor a forward Euler theorem supplies reversal. Separate endpoint truncation, score-estimation bias, time discretization and Monte Carlo error.
**Pages:** score-sdes-and-reverse-time-generation / score-sdes-and-reverse-time-generation-examples. **Scope:** Reuse P-SDE-01 for the forward Itô equation and define the marginal score $\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$ wherever $p_t$ is positive and differentiable. Reuse P-SDE-02 for both the bounded-start Anderson reversal and its separate unbounded-start density-kernel reversal; do not duplicate either theorem here. Anderson’s contract requires an a.s.-bounded initial state independent of Brownian motion, a unique strong forward solution, a positive unique smooth forward density, and unique smooth joint/conditional joint KFP densities. The broader P-SDE-02 interface covers unbounded $X_\epsilon$ only under its square uniformly nonsingular diffusion, positive smooth transition/marginal densities, Chapman–Kolmogorov and semigroup regularity, integrability/decay, and well-posed nonexplosive reverse martingale-problem hypotheses; its reversed kernels are $K_{u,v}(y,dx)=p_{T-v}(x)q(T-v,x;T-u,y)dx/p_{T-u}(y)$ and its generator on $\phi\in C_c^\infty$ is $p_{T-u}^{-1}\{L_{T-u}^*(p_{T-u}\phi)-\phi L_{T-u}^*p_{T-u}\}$. If the data law at $t=0$ is singular, apply the latter interface on $[\epsilon,T]$ only where its positive-density hypotheses hold; convergence to the data endpoint requires a separate weak-limit proof. A stationary Gaussian Ornstein–Uhlenbeck example has an exact Gaussian terminal law at every finite time by separate explicit Gaussian calculation. State the density-divergence correction for state-dependent diffusion, $-b+\nabla\!\cdot a+a\nabla\log p$, with $[\nabla\!\cdot a]_i=\sum_j\partial_j a_{ij}$ and the reversed-time convention. The familiar $f-g(t)^2\nabla\log p_t$ drift is only the state-independent scalar-diffusion specialization. Song’s probability-flow ODE has the same one-time marginals under exact score and regular density evolution. Start reverse generation from the actual forward terminal law $P_T$; $N(0,I)$ is an additional approximation unless terminal matching is proved. For finite integrated noise in a VP/OU process, $\bar\alpha_T>0$ in general; $\bar\alpha_T=0$ is a limiting/infinite-noise endpoint, not the usual finite-interval theorem. Separate terminal-prior mismatch, learned-score error, reverse discretization, numerical integration, and divergence estimation. Proposed items: def-marginal-score-function; thm-probability-flow-ode-marginals; alg-predictor-corrector-score-sampling. **Proof route:** consume the exact P-SDE-01/02 interfaces and keep their independent proof obligations upstream of Category 3/4. Match Fokker–Planck and continuity equations for the probability-flow ODE. Existing Brownian and Itô calculus do not supply SDE existence, positive density, or time reversal. **Examples:** stationary Gaussian Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process with known score and exact finite-time terminal law (separate explicit Gaussian calculation); a singular data law requiring truncation at $\epsilon$ and an endpoint weak limit; Gaussian-prior initialization with nonzero terminal mismatch. **Requires:** P-SDE-01 — Forward Itô SDE well-posedness and density regularity; P-SDE-02 — the exact Anderson theorem and the explicitly separate unbounded-start positive-transition-density/martingale-problem contract; existing `def-brownian-motion`, `def-ito-integral-for-square-integrable-predictable-processes`, and `thm-multidimensional-ito-formula-for-brownian-driven-processes`. P-NUM-SDE-02 is needed only for numerical sampler-error claims and must remain independent of Categories 3–4. **Sources:** Anderson, §§1–4, PDF pp. 1–9 (linear stationary Gaussian case, theorem, and proof read); Song et al., §§2–4, pp. 2–5 and appendices pp. 13–15, 17–19, 31–32. Neither consulted source proves the broader independent P-SDE-02 route; that exact upstream proof obligation remains open.

### ML-61 — Conditional flow matching and transport paths

**Pages:** conditional-flow-matching-and-transport-paths / conditional-flow-matching-and-transport-paths-examples. **Scope:** Define a probability path and velocity field. State the conditional-to-marginal continuity-equation identity only when differentiation under the $x_1$ integral is justified by the required Leibniz regularity. The conditional-flow-matching objective differs from the marginal objective by a parameter-independent constant, hence has the same parameter gradient, under $p_t(x)>0$, Fubini/integrability and decay conditions, bounded $u_t,v_t,\nabla_\theta v_t$, and parameter differentiability of $v_t$. For the Gaussian conditional path $p_t(x\mid x_1)=N(\mu_t(x_1),\sigma_t(x_1)^2I)$ require differentiable $\mu_t,\sigma_t$ and $\sigma_t>0$; its affine flow $\psi_t(x)=\sigma_t(x_1)x+\mu_t(x_1)$ has conditional field $u_t(x\mid x_1)=\frac{\sigma'_t(x_1)}{\sigma_t(x_1)}(x-\mu_t(x_1))+\mu'_t(x_1)$. The paper’s conditional OT path uses $\mu_t(x_1)=tx_1$ and $\sigma_t(x_1)=1-(1-\sigma_{\min})t$ with $\sigma_{\min}>0$, so the affine conditional map runs from $N(0,I)$ to $N(x_1,\sigma_{\min}^2I)$. Conditional OT paths do not imply globally optimal transport for the induced marginal vector field. Training and sampling comparisons remain empirical. Proposed items: def-probability-path-and-velocity-field; thm-conditional-flow-matching-gradient-identity; thm-gaussian-conditional-optimal-transport-map; alg-flow-matching-training-and-sampling. **Proof route:** integrate the conditional continuity equation to obtain the marginal equation, explicitly checking Leibniz hypotheses; expand both squared objectives and condition on the endpoint variables to identify the finite parameter-independent variance term, checking Fubini and differentiability assumptions. Derive the Gaussian field by differentiating the invertible affine flow. For $X\sim N(0,I_d)$ and $Y\sim N(x_1,\sigma_{\min}^2I_d)$, any coupling satisfies $E\|Y-X\|^2=d(1+\sigma_{\min}^2)+\|x_1\|^2-2E[X\cdot(Y-x_1)]\ge d(1-\sigma_{\min})^2+\|x_1\|^2$ by Cauchy–Schwarz, with equality for $Y=x_1+\sigma_{\min}X$; this verifies the affine conditional map is quadratic-cost optimal. Do not infer a general OT theorem or marginal OT optimality. Solve the field through ML-58’s exact ODE flow; numerical error requires P-NUM-ODE-01. **Examples:** Gaussian prior-to-data conditional interpolation; a mixture of straight conditional paths whose marginal vector field is curved. **Requires:** ML-58 — Neural ODEs and continuous normalizing flows; P-NUM-ODE-01 and P-NUM-ODE-02 only for solver-error guarantees. P-KR-02 is required only if adding a $W_1$ claim. All independent suppliers precede Categories 3–4. **Sources:** Lipman et al., §§2–4, Theorems 1–3, PDF pp. 2–7, plus appendix regularity and derivations pp. 14–16. Remaining experiment/reference pages were not read; no empirical comparison is presented as a theorem.

### ML-62 — Multimodal contrastive alignment and transfer

**Pages:** multimodal-contrastive-alignment-and-transfer / multimodal-contrastive-alignment-and-transfer-examples. **Scope:** For a batch of $N$ matched image/text pairs, define normalized image and text embeddings $u_i,v_i$, logits $\ell_{ij}=s\,u_i^\top v_j$ with positive learned scale $s=e^\tau$, and the symmetric objective as the average of image-to-text and text-to-image cross-entropies with the matched diagonal as labels. Define the encoders, zero-shot prompt classifier and prompt-template averaging separately. Describe the reported large paired-web-data training and transfer results only as empirical outcomes under the source’s measured data, architecture, training, and prompt choices. Include prompt dependence, the paper’s main-text overlap analysis, and limitations; do not claim universal OOD, reasoning, fairness, or semantic alignment. Proposed items: def-symmetric-image-text-contrastive-loss; alg-zero-shot-prompt-classification; ex-prompt-and-distribution-sensitive-transfer. **Proof route:** verify the loss is the stated batch cross-entropy and normalized dot products give its logits. No theorem derives broad transfer from paired web data. The latent-class theorem in ML-51 does not automatically apply because captions, sampling, class labels, and task assumptions differ. **Examples:** prompt wording changes class prototypes; retrieval versus zero-shot classification; semantically mismatched captions. **Requires:** ML-51 — Self-supervised and contrastive representations for the loss convention; ML-30 — Feedforward networks and single-hidden-layer universality for encoder notation; ML-09 — Distribution shift and domain adaptation for shift vocabulary. CLIP optimizer results are empirical; no SGD/Adam convergence theorem is imported. **Sources:** Radford et al., §§2–5, PDF pp. 2–9 (main paper read, including overlap analysis p. 8); appendices pp. 10–16 were not read and are not used.

### ML-63 — Foundation models, transfer, and scaling evidence

**Pages:** foundation-models-transfer-and-scaling-evidence / foundation-models-transfer-and-scaling-evidence-examples. **Scope:** Present the report’s definition—broad-data pretraining, generally self-supervised at scale, followed by adaptation to varied downstream tasks—along with its multimodal/ecosystem framing, risks, and theory gaps. Present Kaplan et al.’s empirical fits for decoder-only Transformers trained on WebText2: $L(N)=(N_c/N)^{\alpha_N}$ with $\alpha_N\approx0.076$, $L(D)=(D_c/D)^{\alpha_D}$ with $\alpha_D\approx0.095$, and compute-efficient $L(C_{\min})=(C_c/C_{\min})^{\alpha_{C_{\min}}}$ with $\alpha_{C_{\min}}\approx0.050$, over the paper’s tested non-embedding parameter, token, and compute ranges. Its fitted compute allocation is approximately $N\propto C_{\min}^{0.73}$, $B\propto C_{\min}^{0.24}$, and $S\propto C_{\min}^{0.03}$ under its empirical frontier model. These are measured/fitted trends, not general theorems about emergent capability, universal transfer, optimal compute allocation, future modalities, or OOD reliability; extrapolation beyond the observed regimes is a hypothesis. Proposed items: def-foundation-model-as-broad-pretraining-and-adaptation; ex-empirical-language-model-scaling-law; ex-pretraining-adaptation-interface-limitations. **Proof route:** No proof is appropriate for a taxonomy or empirical regression. Define the fitted quantities and tested range; distinguish the paper’s fitted compute-allocation model from a theorem. The report identifies prompt/in-context learning as outside a simple ERM adaptation framing and treats theory of the pretraining-adaptation interface as open. **Examples:** reproduce an observed-range power-law fit; contrast interpolation of measured cross-entropy with extrapolation to an unmeasured capability. **Requires:** ML-35 — Attention, transformer universality, and sequence limits for architecture notation; ML-09 — Distribution shift and domain adaptation for shift vocabulary. Scaling and training evidence is empirical and imports no SGD/Adam convergence result. No Category 3 result is supplied by foundation-model evidence. **Sources:** Bommasani et al., intro/definition/ecosystem pp. 3–12, training/adaptation pp. 81–90, theory pp. 118–122 (selected sections only; remaining report not read); Kaplan et al., method/results pp. 1–6 and summary/caveats pp. 20–22 (selected pages only). Neither source proves a general scaling or transfer law.

### ML-64 — Predictive calibration and uncertainty under shift

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** Consume the published `alg-split-conformal-prediction-set` and `thm-split-conformal-marginal-coverage`. Its conditional statement is given the training sigma-field under a regular conditional exchangeable calibration/test tuple, not coverage conditional on the test covariate or on observed calibration data. ML-90 adds nonbinary monotone-risk calibration; it does not change this rank theorem.
**Pages:** predictive-calibration-and-uncertainty-under-shift / predictive-calibration-and-uncertainty-under-shift-examples. **Scope:** Define top-label calibration $\Pr(Y=\hat Y\mid C=p)=p$ and binned ECE/MCE; distinguish calibration from accuracy and proper scores (NLL/Brier) from bin-dependent estimators. Define positive-temperature scaling of logits and show it preserves argmax. Validation NLL selection is an empirical calibration method, not a finite-sample guarantee. Add split conformal prediction using a nonconformity score fixed by training data: for $m$ calibration examples and $0<\alpha<1$, take $k=\lceil(m+1)(1-\alpha)\rceil$, set $q=R_{(k)}$ if $k\le m$ and $q=+\infty$ if $k=m+1$, and return $C(x)=\{y:s_{\hat f}(x,y)\le q\}$. If conditional on the training sigma-field calibration and test examples are exchangeable, MSF-37 gives $\Pr(Y_{m+1}\in C(X_{m+1})\mid\text{training})\ge1-\alpha$; its optional shared-uniform randomized rank gives exact marginal coverage. This is marginal set coverage, not calibrated class probabilities or conditional-on-covariates coverage. Exchangeability failure under shift voids that guarantee. Summarize Ovadia’s benchmark only for its reported shifts/methods; no universal ensemble ranking or calibration guarantee under shift follows. Unknown-label OOD cases cannot be assessed by ordinary supervised calibration metrics. Proposed items: def-top-label-calibration; def-binned-expected-calibration-error; alg-validation-temperature-scaling; alg-split-conformal-set-for-classification; ex-calibration-degradation-under-shift. **Proof route:** positive temperature preserves logit order; the calibration definition alone gives no sampling guarantee; ECE depends on bins/sample size. For split conformal, consume MSF-37’s fixed training score, order statistic (including the $+\infty$ sentinel), and exchangeable-rank proof; do not reprove or strengthen it. Report temperature-scaling and shift results as experiments; iid validation behavior need not hold under distribution shift. **Examples:** perfectly accurate but overconfident classifier; identical argmax with different temperature and ECE; split conformal set with a rare subgroup’s poor conditional coverage despite marginal validity; corrupted-image shift where exchangeability fails. **Requires:** ML-07 — Calibration, proper losses, and surrogate classification; ML-09 — Distribution shift and domain adaptation; MSF-37 — Conformal Prediction and Exchangeable Ranks, specifically `def-conformal-nonconformity-score-and-rank-pvalue`, `alg-split-conformal-prediction-set`, and `thm-split-conformal-marginal-coverage`; probability conditional-law interface. MSF-37 supplies only its stated marginal exchangeable coverage; it gives no guarantee under shift or for conditional coverage. **Sources:** Guo et al., §§2–4 and discussion, PDF pp. 2–8 (main paper read; pp. 9–10 not read); Ovadia et al., §§1–4 and discussion, pp. 1–12 (complete paper read); Lei et al. (2018), split conformal theorem and proof contract as audited in MSF-37. These are method/benchmark evidence and a marginal conformal theorem, not distribution-free probability calibration theorems.

### ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks

**Pages:** online-decision-protocols / online-decision-protocols-examples. **Scope:** Define the protocol as the learner choosing $x_t$ from past history and then receiving feedback about the round-$t$ loss: full information reveals $f_t$ (or the full expert-loss vector), while bandit feedback reveals only the played loss. Distinguish an oblivious adversary that fixes the loss sequence before learner randomization, a non-anticipating adaptive adversary that chooses $f_t$ from past history before $x_t$, a reactive adversary that sees $x_t$, and a stochastic environment with a specified fixed reward law; state guarantee claims against each separately. Define realized regret against fixed $u$ by $\sum_{t=1}^T f_t(x_t)-\sum_{t=1}^T f_t(u)$ and static regret by minimizing the comparator term over $u\in K$; expected regret is the expectation over learner/environment randomness. In a stochastic finite-arm model with means $\mu_i$, define pseudo-regret $\bar R_T=\sum_{t=1}^T(\mu^*-\mu_{A_t})$ and distinguish it from the realized reward regret; their expectations agree under the conditional-mean model. Define dynamic regret as $\sum_{t=1}^T f_t(x_t)-\sum_{t=1}^T f_t(u_t)$ and Hazan’s path length $P(u_{1:T})=1+\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\|u_t-u_{t+1}\|$. Define interval regret on $I=[r,s]$ as $\sum_{t=r}^s f_t(x_t)-\min_{u\in K}\sum_{t=r}^s f_t(u)$ and adaptive regret as its maximum over all contiguous intervals; call an algorithm strongly adaptive only when, for a stated base algorithm $A_0$ and constants $C,p$, every interval $I$ has regret at most $C(\log(eT))^p$ times the stated regret bound of $A_0$ at horizon $|I|$. These comparator/feedback/adversary conventions are separate; interval adaptation is not anytime statistical validity. Proposed interfaces: `def-online-decision-protocol`, `def-static-dynamic-and-strongly-adaptive-regret`, `def-adversary-and-feedback-model`. **Proof route:** verify the regret decompositions directly from the protocol; no theorem is needed for the definitions. **Examples:** full-information finite experts, stochastic arm means, changing comparator. **Requires:** `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`; finite sums. **Sources:** Hazan, *Introduction to Online Convex Optimization*, PDF pp. 23–38 (printed pp. 1–16) and pp. 191–206 (printed pp. 169–184); Lattimore–Szepesvári, *Bandit Algorithms*, PDF pp. 65–82 (printed pp. 56–73). The adaptive-regret chapter defers parts of the FLH/FLH2 proof to exercises; import no omitted proof.

### ML-66 — Hedge and online gradient descent

**Pages:** hedge-and-online-gradient-descent / hedge-and-online-gradient-descent-examples. **Scope:** Define Hedge for full-information expert losses $\ell_t(i)\in[0,1]$: $x_t(i)=W_t(i)/\sum_jW_t(j)$, sample expert $I_t\sim x_t$, and update $W_{t+1}(i)=W_t(i)e^{-\epsilon\ell_t(i)}$. State Hazan’s nonnegative-loss bound $\sum_t x_t^\top\ell_t\le\sum_t\ell_t(i^*)+\epsilon\sum_t x_t^\top\ell_t^{\,2}+\log(N)/\epsilon$; for $[0,1]$ losses, tuning $\epsilon=\sqrt{\log(N)/T}$ gives expected static regret at most $2\sqrt{T\log N}$ (with the $N=1$ case trivial). Define projected OGD on a nonempty closed convex $K\subset\mathbb R^d$ of finite Euclidean diameter $D$, with $x_{t+1}=\Pi_K(x_t-\eta_tg_t)$ and $\eta_t=D/(G\sqrt t)$. For differentiable convex losses on a neighborhood of $K$, require $g_t=\nabla f_t(x_t)$ and $\|g_t\|_2\le G$; the local nonsmooth extension instead chooses $g_t\in\partial f_t(x_t)$ and explicitly assumes the subgradient inequality against every $u\in K$. State Hazan’s bound $\mathrm{Regret}_T\le\frac32DG\sqrt T$; handle $D=0$ or $G=0$ separately as zero-regret cases. Proposed interfaces: `alg-hedge`, `alg-online-gradient-descent`, `thm-hedge-static-regret`, `thm-online-gradient-descent-regret`. **Proof route:** Hedge uses $e^{-x}\le1-x+x^2$, $1+x\le e^x$ and telescoping total weight; OGD uses the metric-projection variational inequality, the gradient/subgradient inequality, and telescoping squared distances. Do not claim a matching minimax lower bound from Hazan's incomplete exercise proof. **Examples:** two experts, Euclidean projection on an interval, infeasible comparator. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; `thm-differentiable-convex-functions-and-gradient-inequality`, `def-subgradient-and-subdifferential`, `lem-projection-onto-a-closed-convex-set-has-the-variational-inequality`, `thm-projection-onto-a-nonempty-closed-convex-set`, and `lem-metric-projection-onto-a-nonempty-closed-convex-set-is-nonexpansive`. The projection existence supplier assumes Countable Choice; carry it into the theorem. **Sources:** Hazan PDF pp. 23–38 (printed pp. 1–16), especially Theorem 1.5 and its proof, pp. 34–35; and pp. 63–76 (printed pp. 41–54), especially Theorem 3.1 and proof, pp. 65–66. Hazan's displayed OGD proof covers differentiable convex losses with gradient norm at most $G$; the stated subgradient version is a local extension using the same projection calculation and the subgradient inequality. The lower-bound derivation and a projection-existence step are exercises.

### ML-67 — Mirror descent and adaptive regularization

**Pages:** mirror-descent-and-adaptive-regularization / mirror-descent-and-adaptive-regularization-examples. **Scope:** On a nonempty bounded closed convex decision set $K$, define a twice-differentiable strongly convex regularizer $R$ with positive-definite Hessian on the relevant interior, $B_R(x\|y)=R(x)-R(y)-\nabla R(y)^\top(x-y)$, and, at interior $z$, $\|v\|_z^2=v^\top\nabla^2R(z)v$ and $\|h\|_{*,z}^2=h^\top(\nabla^2R(z))^{-1}h$. Take $\|g_t\|_{*,t}$ at the intermediate Hessian point on the segment from $x_t$ to $y_{t+1}$. Require every dual-gradient update and Bregman projection to be well-defined. For agile OMD state $\nabla R(y_{t+1})=\nabla R(x_t)-\eta g_t$ and $x_{t+1}\in\arg\min_{x\in K}B_R(x\|y_{t+1})$, with $g_t=\nabla f_t(x_t)$. For convex losses state Hazan's bound $\mathrm{Regret}_T(u)\le\frac{\eta}{4}\sum_{t=1}^T\|g_t\|_{*,t}^2+\frac{R(u)-R(x_1)}{2\eta}$; if $D_R^2=\max_{x,y\in K}(R(x)-R(y))$ and $\|g_t\|_{*,t}\le G_R$, the choice $\eta=D_R/(G_R\sqrt T)$ gives $D_RG_R\sqrt T$. Show locally that $R(x)=\frac12\|x-x_0\|_2^2$ yields projected OGD and that negative entropy on the simplex yields the normalized multiplicative Hedge update; prove any entropy-diameter $\log n$ claim rather than importing Hazan's exercise.
