Give AdaGrad's algorithmic updates only: $G_t=G_{t-1}+g_tg_t^\top$, choose the full-matrix scale $H_t=G_t^{1/2}$ or a diagonal scale $H_t=\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{(G_t)_{ii}})$, set $y_{t+1}=x_t-\eta H_t^\dagger g_t$, and choose $x_{t+1}\in\arg\min_{x\in K}\|y_{t+1}-x\|_{H_t}^2$, where $H_t^\dagger$ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse and a minimizer is selected if nonunique. The source prints its diagonal scale as $\operatorname{diag}(G_t^{1/2})$; resolve the notation by proving the coordinatewise diagonal update locally. Do not state AdaGrad's regret rate unless the omitted optimizer calculation is proved locally. Proposed interfaces: `def-mirror-map-and-bregman-divergence`, `alg-online-mirror-descent`, `thm-mirror-descent-regret`, `alg-adagrad`. **Proof route:** derive the Bregman three-point identity and projection Pythagorean inequality from their definitions; combine convexity, local dual-norm Cauchy–Schwarz, nonnegativity, and telescoping. Verify the entropy normalization and diameter separately on the simplex; do not rely on the book's exercises. For AdaGrad, prove the matrix optimizer identity and changing-metric telescoping bounds before adding any theorem. **Examples:** entropy on the simplex, Euclidean OGD, a singular adaptive metric. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; ML-66 — Hedge and online gradient descent; `def-convex-and-strictly-convex-functions-on-euclidean-sets`, `def-subgradient-and-subdifferential`, and `thm-differentiable-convex-functions-and-gradient-inequality`. **Sources:** Hazan PDF pp. 93–122 (printed pp. 71–100): regularizer/Bregman definitions and RFTL, pp. 93–99; OMD Algorithm 14, Theorem 5.6 and displayed proof, pp. 100–103; Euclidean/entropy reductions, pp. 103–105; AdaGrad Algorithm 19 and Theorem 5.12 proof, pp. 113–118; optimizer Proposition 5.16 and related derivations deferred to Exercise 11, pp. 117–121. The selected AdaGrad optimizer proof is an exercise, so its convergence theorem remains an open local proof obligation.

### ML-68 — Stochastic finite-armed bandits

**Pages:** stochastic-finite-armed-bandits / stochastic-finite-armed-bandits-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite $k$-armed stationary bandit with finite reward means, conditional reward laws, gaps $\Delta_i=\mu^*-\mu_i$, pseudo-regret and expected regret. For the source model, assume a jointly independent reward table $(X_{s,i})_{s\le n,i\le k}$ with each $X_{s,i}\sim P_i$, and centered 1-sub-Gaussian rewards in the exact MGF sense $\mathbb E e^{\lambda(X_{s,i}-\mu_i)}\le e^{\lambda^2/2}$ for every $\lambda\in\mathbb R$. For ETC with $1\le m\le n/k$, state $R_n\le m\sum_i\Delta_i+(n-mk)\sum_i\Delta_i e^{-m\Delta_i^2/4}$. For UCB with horizon $n\ge k$, initialize by pulling each arm once; thereafter use $\hat\mu_i+\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)/T_i}$ and set the unobserved-arm index to $+\infty$. For $n\ge2$, with $\delta=1/n^2$, state expected gap-dependent regret $R_n\le\sum_i3\Delta_i+\sum_{i:\Delta_i>0}16\log(n)/\Delta_i$ and gap-free regret $R_n\le\sqrt{8nk\log n}+3\sum_i\Delta_i$. These are expected-regret results; do not present them as high-probability bounds. Proposed interfaces: `def-stochastic-bandit-and-pseudo-regret`, `def-centered-subgaussian-reward`, `lem-independent-subgaussian-prefix-tail`, `alg-explore-then-commit`, `thm-explore-then-commit-expected-regret`, `alg-upper-confidence-bound-bandit`, `thm-ucb-gap-dependent-regret`, `thm-ucb-gap-free-regret`. **Proof route:** decompose regret into pulls of suboptimal arms. Locally prove the fixed-prefix sub-Gaussian tail from the MGF definition by product joint law, Tonelli, Markov's inequality and exponential optimization; apply it at each deterministic reward-table prefix and prove the finite union bound before bounding expected pulls. Do not apply a fixed-$n$ tail bound at a random pull count. **Examples:** two-arm gap, tied optimal arms, heavy-tailed rewards outside the stated model. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; `def-independent-random-elements`, `thm-independent-random-elements-have-product-joint-law`, `def-expectation-of-a-nonnegative-or-integrable-random-variable`, `cor-markov-inequality-for-random-variables`, `thm-tonelli-theorem-for-sigma-finite-product-spaces`, `prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral`, `cor-additivity-of-the-nonnegative-lebesgue-integral`, and `thm-exponential-is-strictly-increasing`. The published Hoeffding/Azuma items for bounded variables are not used for unbounded 1-sub-Gaussian rewards. **Sources:** Lattimore–Szepesvári PDF pp. 65–82 (printed pp. 56–73), concentration definitions/inequalities pp. 83–97 (printed pp. 74–88), explore-then-commit pp. 100–110 (printed pp. 91–101), and UCB pp. 111–125 (printed pp. 102–116). Chapter 5's independent-sum sub-Gaussian lemma is an exercise; the local MGF/tail proof and fixed-prefix union step remain author obligations.

### ML-69 — Adversarial bandits and importance-weighted feedback

**Pages:** adversarial-bandits-and-importance-weighted-feedback / adversarial-bandits-and-importance-weighted-feedback-examples. **Scope:** For an oblivious reward sequence $x_t\in[0,1]^k$, define $P_t\in\Delta_k$, sample $A_t\sim P_t$, observe $X_t=x_{t,A_t}$, and measure expected regret $R_n=\max_i\sum_{t=1}^n x_{t,i}-\mathbb E\sum_{t=1}^nX_t$. Set $Y_t=1-X_t$, $\widehat Y_{t,i}=\mathbf1\{A_t=i\}Y_t/P_{t,i}$ and $\widehat X_{t,i}=1-\widehat Y_{t,i}$, where $P_{t,i}>0$ under Exp3 and, for $y_{t,i}=1-x_{t,i}$, $\mathbb E[\widehat Y_{t,i}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=y_{t,i}$ and $\mathbb E[\widehat Y_{t,i}^2\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=y_{t,i}^2/P_{t,i}$, and $\widehat S_{t,i}=\sum_{s\le t}\widehat X_{s,i}$. Exp3 has $P_{t,i}>0$ by construction, so these conditional formulas are defined. Exp3 uses $P_{t,i}=\exp(\eta\widehat S_{t-1,i})/\sum_j\exp(\eta\widehat S_{t-1,j})$. With $k>1$, $\eta=\sqrt{2\log(k)/(nk)}$, state the source's expected-regret bound $R_n\le\sqrt{2nk\log k}$. This is expected regret over learner randomization, not high-probability realized regret. The source notes an expected-regret extension to reward vectors selected from past history before the current action; do not transfer it to current-action-reactive policy regret. Proposed interfaces: def-bandit-importance-weighted-estimator, alg-exp3, thm-exp3-expected-regret. **Proof route:** prove conditional unbiasedness of the loss-based estimator and its exact second-moment bound; use the one-sided exponential inequality for the loss estimate, then the exponential-weights potential and tuned expectation bound. Do not invoke ML-66's bounded-loss theorem on the unbounded importance-weighted estimate. **Examples:** rare-arm high-variance estimate, deterministic rewards, Exp3 versus full-information Hedge. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; ML-66 — exponential-weights update notation only, not its bounded-loss regret theorem. **Sources:** Lattimore–Szepesvári PDF pp. 157–173 (printed pp. 148–164): adversarial protocol and expected-regret definition, pp. 157–158; importance-weighted estimator and conditional variance, pp. 159–161; Exp3 algorithm and Theorem 11.2 proof, pp. 161–165; past-adaptive adversary and IX exercise caveats, pp. 167–168. Exp3-IX high-probability analysis is an exercise.

### ML-70 — Contextual expert bandits

**Pages:** contextual-expert-bandits / contextual-expert-bandits-examples. **Scope:** Let $k>1$ actions, $M>1$ experts, an obliviously fixed reward sequence $x_t\in[0,1]^k$, and expert advice $E(t)\in\Delta_k^M$ revealed before each action and independent of the learner's current/past actions. Maintain $Q_t\in\Delta_M$, play $P_t=Q_tE(t)$ and $A_t\sim P_t$, and observe $X_t=x_{t,A_t}$. With $P_{t,i}=0$ use the zero quotient convention on the impossible event $A_t=i$. For Exp4 ($\gamma=0$), estimate $\widehat X_{t,i}=1-\mathbf1\{A_t=i\}(1-X_t)/P_{t,i}$, set $\widetilde X_{t,m}=\sum_iE(t)_{m,i}\widehat X_{t,i}$, and update $Q_{t+1,m}\propto Q_{t,m}\exp(\eta\widetilde X_{t,m})$. Define expected regret against the best fixed expert as $R_n=\mathbb E[\max_{m\in[M]}\sum_{t=1}^n\langle E(t)_m,x_t\rangle-\sum_{t=1}^nX_t]$. With $\eta=\sqrt{2\log(M)/(nk)}$, Theorem 18.1 gives $R_n\le\sqrt{2nk\log M}$. Do not claim a stochastic contextual generalization bound, high-probability Exp4-IX, or current-action-reactive expert guarantee. Proposed interfaces: def-contextual-bandit-protocol, alg-exp4, thm-exp4-expected-regret. **Proof route:** derive conditional unbiasedness for $\gamma=0$ and the expert reward estimates; prove the source's Lemma 18.2 potential inequality locally (its proof is Exercise 18.2), then bound the estimator second moments and take expectations. Do not apply bounded-loss Hedge directly to unbounded importance weights. **Examples:** finite expert set, duplicated experts, contexts with no shared policy structure. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; ML-66 — exponential-weights update notation only, not its bounded-loss regret theorem; ML-69 — importance-weighted estimator convention. **Sources:** Lattimore–Szepesvári PDF pp. 233–246 (printed pp. 224–237): contextual protocol and benchmark, pp. 233–235; finite expert advice and obliviousness, pp. 237–238; Exp4 algorithm and Theorem 18.1, pp. 238–240; omitted Lemma 18.2 proof and Exp4-IX Exercise 18.3, pp. 243–244. The expected theorem is source-stated, but the potential lemma must be supplied locally.

### ML-71 — Linear contextual bandits and LinUCB

**Pages:** linear-contextual-bandits-and-linucb / linear-contextual-bandits-and-linucb-examples. **Scope:** At round $t$, observe context $C_t$ and a nonempty available action-feature set $\mathcal A_t\subset\mathbb R^d$ before choosing a predictable feature vector $A_t=\psi(C_t,a_t)\in\mathcal A_t$ with $\|A_t\|_2\le L$ for every candidate; require the optimism maximum to be attained (finite or compact action-feature sets suffice). Choose $A_t$ before reward $X_t=\langle\theta^*,A_t\rangle+\eta_t$. With $\mathcal F_t$ containing the history, context and selected feature before $\eta_t$, require $\mathbb E[e^{\lambda\eta_t}\mid\mathcal F_t]\le e^{\lambda^2/2}$ for every real $\lambda$, and $\|\theta^*\|_2\le m_2$. Define $V_t=\lambda I+\sum_{s\le t}A_sA_s^\top$ for ridge $\lambda>0$ and $\widehat\theta_t=V_t^{-1}\sum_{s\le t}A_sX_s$. The simultaneous confidence radius from Theorem 20.5 is $\sqrt{\beta_t}=\sqrt\lambda m_2+\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)+\log(\det V_{t-1}/\lambda^d)}$, giving $\theta^*\in C_t=\{\theta:\|\theta-\widehat\theta_{t-1}\|_{V_{t-1}}^2\le\beta_t\}$ for all $t$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. LinUCB chooses $A_t\in\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal A_t}\langle\widehat\theta_{t-1},a\rangle+\sqrt{\beta_t}\|a\|_{V_{t-1}^{-1}}$. For the source regret theorem also require nondecreasing $\beta_t\ge1$ and the per-round optimal-mean range $\sup_{a,b\in\mathcal A_t}\langle\theta^*,a-b\rangle\le1$. Then, with probability $1-\delta$, $\widehat R_n=\sum_{t=1}^n[\max_{a\in\mathcal A_t}\langle\theta^*,a\rangle-\langle\theta^*,A_t\rangle]$ and, with probability $1-\delta$, $\widehat R_n\le\sqrt{8n\beta_n\log(\det V_n/\det V_0)}\le\sqrt{8dn\beta_n\log(1+nL^2/(d\lambda))}$. The source's expected-regret corollary takes $\delta=1/n$ and states $R_n=\mathbb E[\widehat R_n]\le C d\sqrt n\log(nL)$ with fixed problem parameters; keep it distinct from the high-probability pseudo-regret bound. Proposed interfaces: def-linear-contextual-bandit-model, alg-linucb, thm-self-normalized-linear-confidence-ellipsoid, thm-linucb-regret. **Proof route:** derive the conditional exponential supermartingale from the MGF assumption, integrate against a Gaussian prior and prove the time-uniform self-normalized confidence set; derive its crossing bound by bounded optional sampling/Markov under the matching nonnegative-supermartingale conditions. For regret, combine optimism, the confidence ellipsoid, Cauchy–Schwarz and the determinant/elliptical-potential sum. The source supplies the full scalar proof; no matrix self-normalized theorem beyond its finite-dimensional linear setting is imported. **Examples:** linear realizability, feature-norm violation, unbounded parameter norm, action sets where the optimal-mean range exceeds one. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocol and regret conventions; ML-12 — linear-model vocabulary only; state the sample-sum ridge normalization above locally. Also reuse def-tikhonov-regularised-least-squares, def-filtration-and-filtered-probability-space, def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra, def-martingale-submartingale-and-supermartingale, thm-optional-sampling-for-bounded-stopping-times, cor-markov-inequality-for-random-variables, thm-determinant-multiplicative, lem-determinant-rank-one-update-over-a-commutative-ring, and thm-cholesky-factorisation-exists-iff-hermitian-positive-definite-and-is-unique. Carry the Axiom of Choice required by the martingale and optional-sampling interfaces; the exact Gaussian-mixture, conditional-supermartingale and determinant calculations remain local. **Sources:** Lattimore–Szepesvári PDF pp. 247–275 (printed pp. 238–266): conditional linear model and feature-map contract, pp. 247–249; ridge estimate and confidence-set setup, pp. 250–251; Assumption 19.1, Theorem 19.2, determinant lemma/regret proof and parameter terms, pp. 252–254; self-normalized Theorems 20.4–20.5 and complete Gaussian-mixture proof, pp. 267–270. The source's expected-regret corollary suppresses fixed problem parameters in its universal constant; retain the explicit beta formula for review.

### ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators

**Pages:** finite-mdps-and-bellman-operators / finite-mdps-and-bellman-operators-examples. **Scope:** Let $S$, the reward set $R$, and the state-dependent nonempty action sets $A(s)$ be finite; give each $(s,a)$ a joint law $p(s',r\mid s,a)$ and define bounded expected reward $r(s,a)$ and transition probabilities $P(s'\mid s,a)$. Define a stationary Markov policy $\pi(a\mid s)$, discounted return $G_t=\sum_{j\ge0}\gamma^jR_{t+j+1}$ for $0\le\gamma<1$, and $V^\pi(s)=\mathbb E_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s]$. Define $T^\pi V(s)=\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V(s')]$ and $T^*V(s)=\max_{a\in A(s)}[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V(s')]$. Prove both are sup-norm contractions with the nonnegative constant $\gamma$, hence have unique fixed points; a deterministic policy greedy with respect to $V^\pi$ is no worse than $\pi$. State finite policy iteration only with exact evaluation and deterministic tie retention (keep the current action whenever it is already greedy), so every changed policy strictly improves and termination follows from finitely many deterministic policies. Value iteration $V_{m+1}=T^*V_m$ converges to $V^*$, with $\|V_m-V^*\|_\infty\le\gamma^m\|V_0-V^*\|_\infty$. Keep average reward, undiscounted continuing models and improper episodic tasks separate. Proposed interfaces: def-finite-markov-decision-process, def-discounted-return-and-value-functions, def-policy-bellman-operator, def-bellman-optimality-operator, thm-bellman-contraction-and-unique-fixed-point, alg-value-iteration, thm-policy-improvement. **Proof route:** construct the finite expected-reward and transition kernels; bound the policy and max operators in sup norm by $\gamma$; apply Banach's theorem with its exact $0\le q<1$ hypothesis; prove pointwise greedy improvement and finite termination under the tie rule. **Examples:** $\gamma=1$ failure, reward shift, finite value-iteration error, policy ties causing cycling without tie retention. **Requires:** published def-measure-kernel-and-probability-kernel, def-time-homogeneous-markov-chain-with-transition-kernel, and thm-banach-fixed-point. A fixed policy induces the cited time-homogeneous Markov chain; its definition assumes the Axiom of Choice, which must be carried. The Banach supplier takes a nonnegative contraction constant $q$; here $q=\gamma$, with no absolute-constant reinterpretation. **Sources:** Sutton and Barto, *Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction*, PDF pp. 67–110 (printed pp. 47–90): finite MDP dynamics and joint reward/transition kernel, pp. 68–69; discounted returns/value functions and Bellman equations, pp. 73–92; policy improvement and exact finite policy-iteration discussion, pp. 98–101. Brown University report CS-96-08, body pp. 3–27, supplies complementary finite-MDP semantics. Sutton cites/sketches general value-iteration convergence rather than proving the contraction argument; give that proof locally, and note its policy-iteration pseudocode's tie-cycling bug is an exercise.

### ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction

**Pages:** tabular-prediction-and-control / tabular-prediction-and-control-examples. **Scope:** Define episodic returns and TD errors; give first-visit Monte Carlo prediction, on-policy Sarsa, and off-policy Q-learning updates; distinguish behavior/target policies and require target support under the behavior law for importance sampling. Monte Carlo algorithms assume episodes terminate; a control convergence theorem must separately state its exploration assumption. For tabular Q-learning, require a stationary finite discounted MDP with bounded rewards, $\gamma<1$, every state-action pair visited infinitely often, per-pair Robbins–Monro steps $\sum_n\alpha_n(s,a)=\infty$ and $\sum_n\alpha_n(s,a)^2<\infty$, and bounded martingale-difference target noise (or the exact conditional second-moment condition used by the chosen stochastic-approximation theorem). Keep that theorem distinct from the algorithm and from constant-step-size tracking. State ordinary importance sampling can have infinite variance; weighted importance sampling has different finite-sample bias. Sutton leaves the exploring-starts Monte Carlo control convergence proof as an exercise/open proof question; do not claim it as a completed source proof. Proposed interfaces: alg-monte-carlo-policy-evaluation, alg-td-zero, alg-sarsa, alg-q-learning, thm-tabular-q-learning-convergence-under-robbins-monro-conditions. **Proof route:** prove a finite-dimensional stochastic-approximation/supermartingale lemma; combine the martingale-difference noise, infinite visitation, Robbins–Monro steps, and the $\gamma$-contraction. Sutton gives algorithm derivations and convergence citations/sketches, not this complete proof. **Examples:** insufficient exploration, constant step size, target actions outside behavior support. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; def-filtration-and-filtered-probability-space, def-adapted-and-integrable-stochastic-process, def-martingale-difference-sequence, def-martingale-submartingale-and-supermartingale, def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra, thm-tower-property-of-conditional-expectation, and a locally proved stochastic-approximation result. **Sources:** Sutton & Barto PDF pp. 111–138 (printed pp. 91–117) for episodic MC and control/exploring-starts caveat; pp. 139–160 (printed pp. 119–140) for TD, Sarsa, Q-learning and their cited convergence conditions. The book explicitly cites external Q-learning/Sarsa convergence proofs rather than supplying them.

### ML-74 — Optimistic exploration and PAC-MDP sample complexity

**Pages:** pac-mdp-exploration / pac-mdp-exploration-examples. **Scope:** For a trajectory generated by an algorithm in a finite discounted MDP, define the bad-step count $N_\epsilon=\#\{t:V^{\pi_t}(s_t)<V^*(s_t)-\epsilon\}$; an $(\epsilon,\delta)$ PAC-MDP bound controls this count with probability at least $1-\delta$, and does not require all mistakes to occur before a learner outputs a permanently correct policy. Define an admissible upper heuristic $U(s,a)\ge Q^*(s,a)$ with $0\le U\le V_{\max}\le(1-\gamma)^{-1}$, R-MAX, and Delayed Q-learning. State their results separately for finite $S,A$, rewards in $[0,1]$, known $\gamma\in[0,1)$, $0\le\epsilon<(1-\gamma)^{-1}$, and the theorem’s confidence/sample thresholds; preserve the heuristic-dependent bounds. With the uninformative $V_{\max}=(1-\gamma)^{-1}$ heuristic and logarithmic factors suppressed, the article reports leading sample-complexity orders $\widetilde O(S^2A/(\epsilon^3(1-\gamma)^6))$ for R-MAX and $\widetilde O(SA/(\epsilon^4(1-\gamma)^8))$ for Delayed Q-learning. Proposed interfaces: def-pac-mdp-sample-complexity, def-admissible-mdp-heuristic, alg-r-max, alg-delayed-q-learning, thm-pac-mdp-escape-event-reduction, thm-rmax-pac-bound, thm-delayed-q-pac-bound. **Proof route:** establish the known-state/action model, optimism, model/value accuracy after sufficient samples, the bounded number of model changes or successful updates, and the escape-event reduction. Prove adaptive online reward and transition concentration with conditional martingales or a local coordinate union bound; the article refers its adaptive-sampling argument elsewhere. **Examples:** heuristic failure, cumulative bad-step count versus a permanent-stopping claim, and discount-factor dependence. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction; lem-conditional-hoeffding-bound-for-bounded-martingale-differences and thm-azuma-hoeffding-inequality only when their bounded-increment hypotheses match. **Sources:** Strehl–Li–Littman, *Reinforcement Learning in Finite MDPs: PAC Analysis*, complete JMLR PDF pp. 1–32 (printed pp. 2413–2444): MDP/heuristic/PAC-MDP definitions, PDF pp. 4–7; R-MAX and Delayed Q algorithms, pp. 9–13; escape-event theorem and proofs, pp. 16–23; R-MAX and Delayed Q bounds, pp. 23–27; lower bound, pp. 28–31. Sutton & Barto Ch. 8 supplies planning context, not the PAC theorem.

### ML-75 — Average-reward regret and UCRL2

**Pages:** average-reward-regret-and-ucrl2 / average-reward-regret-and-ucrl2-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite communicating MDP with finite diameter $D$, finite state/action sets, reward observations in $[0,1]$, average reward $\rho^*$, and regret $\Delta(M,\mathrm{UCRL2},s,T)=T\rho^*(M)-\mathbb E[\sum_{t=1}^T r_t]$; the optimal average-reward benchmark is independent of the initial state. Define UCRL2's confidence sets, doubling episodes, and extended value iteration. State Theorem 2 only under the paper's communicating finite-diameter assumptions: for every initial state and horizon $T\ge1$, with probability at least $1-\delta$, $\Delta\le34DS\sqrt{AT\log(T/\delta)}$. State Theorem 5's lower bound only for integer $S,A\ge10$, $D\ge20\log_A S$, and $T\ge DSA$: some such MDP has expected regret at least $0.015\sqrt{DSAT}$. This is not discounted-return RL, and the theorem does not extend to uncommunicating/transient-state models without changes. Proposed interfaces: def-communicating-mdp-and-diameter, def-average-reward-and-regret, alg-ucrl2, thm-ucrl2-high-probability-regret, thm-ucrl2-regret-lower-bound. **Proof route:** establish reward and categorical-$L^1$ confidence events, span-of-bias $\le D$, extended-value-iteration approximation, failed-confidence episode bounds, and the doubling-count sum; derive the lower bound from the source's hard-MDP construction. **Examples:** noncommunicating model, large diameter, discounted/average-reward target mismatch. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; def-time-homogeneous-markov-chain-with-transition-kernel, def-filtration-and-filtered-probability-space, def-martingale-difference-sequence, def-martingale-submartingale-and-supermartingale, lem-conditional-hoeffding-bound-for-bounded-martingale-differences, and thm-azuma-hoeffding-inequality; carry the Axiom of Choice required by the Markov-chain supplier. **Sources:** Jaksch–Ortner–Auer, *Near-optimal Regret Bounds for Reinforcement Learning*, complete JMLR PDF pp. 1–38 (printed pp. 1563–1600): model/diameter and Theorem 2, pp. 2–5; UCRL2 and confidence/episode construction, pp. 7–12; regret proof, pp. 12–21; Theorem 5 and hard-instance proof, pp. 22–27. The theorem's probability and initial-state quantifiers are retained.

### ML-76 — Policy gradients, REINFORCE, and actor–critic

**Pages:** policy-gradients-reinforce-and-actor-critic / policy-gradients-reinforce-and-actor-critic-examples. **Scope:** Define a differentiable episodic stochastic policy $\pi_\theta$, fixed start-state objective $J(\theta)=v^{\pi_\theta}(s_0)$, and trajectory score. Under episode termination, common-support differentiability, finite expected return/score products, and justified differentiation through the trajectory law, derive the episodic score-function identity $\nabla J(\theta)=\mathbb E_{\pi_\theta}[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)\,G_t]$. An integrable baseline $b(S_t)$ independent of the sampled action preserves the expected direction because its score term has conditional mean zero. Define REINFORCE and one-step actor–critic separately; the bootstrapped critic target can introduce approximation bias, and an expected update identity is not convergence of a nonlinear/deep optimizer. Continuing average-reward variants require the source's stationary ergodic state-distribution premise. Proposed interfaces: def-differentiable-stochastic-policy, thm-policy-gradient-theorem, alg-reinforce, alg-reinforce-with-baseline, alg-one-step-actor-critic, lem-action-independent-baseline-does-not-bias-policy-gradient. **Proof route:** differentiate the episodic trajectory likelihood under the stated integrability/dominated-differentiation assumptions, apply the log-derivative identity, telescope rewards-to-go, and show $\mathbb E[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)b(S_t)\mid S_t]=0$. **Examples:** episodic REINFORCE, an action-independent variance-reducing baseline, a biased bootstrapped target. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction; thm-chain-rule-for-total-derivatives and def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra. **Sources:** Sutton & Barto PDF pp. 343–361 (printed pp. 323–341), Chapter 13: episodic objective and theorem proof, pp. 343–347; score-function REINFORCE and baseline, pp. 347–352; actor–critic algorithms, pp. 357–359. The displayed episodic theorem proof is source-backed; the explicit differentiation/interchange conditions remain to be stated and proved locally. No deep actor–critic convergence theorem is imported.

### ML-77 — Function approximation, off-policy instability, and deep-RL limits

**Pages:** rl-function-approximation-and-off-policy-limits / rl-function-approximation-and-off-policy-limits-examples. **Scope:** Define the state-weighting distribution, Bellman error, and projected Bellman error under a fixed weighting. Reproduce Sutton–Barto’s seven-state, two-action, zero-reward Baird example with its stated feature vectors, discount $\gamma=0.99$, behavior probabilities $6/7$ and $1/7$, solid-action target policy, and initial weight vector $w=(1,1,1,1,1,1,10,1)$; its text says the semi-gradient TD(0) weights diverge for every positive step size and also shows divergence under the expected DP update. State that source result in its expected/deterministic-update convention and do not claim almost-sure stochastic-trajectory divergence without a separate proof. Separately reproduce Sutton–Barto’s two observationally indistinguishable Markov reward processes in §11.6: their observable feature/reward-sequence laws agree while Bellman-error values and minimizing parameters differ, so the Bellman error and its minimizer are not learnable from those observations. An MRP has no action-dependent policy; keep this example separate from ML-79’s unseen-action offline-MDP policy impossibility. Treat the “deadly triad” as an explanatory interaction among off-policy sampling, bootstrapping, and approximation, not a universal theorem. Proposed interfaces: def-projected-bellman-error, thm-bairds-counterexample-off-policy-td-diverges, thm-bellman-error-unlearnability-example. **Proof route:** write the finite-state feature/behavior/target matrices and exact expected semi-gradient update, calculate its unstable eigenvalue (or iterate the source’s displayed matrix update) with the source’s initial vector; keep the stochastic pathwise claim separate. For §11.6, specify the state, transition, reward, feature and weighting data of both MRPs, verify that the observable sequence laws agree, then compute the two Bellman-error objectives and their minimizers. **Examples:** Baird’s star under the stated behavior/target policies, a stable on-policy linear-TD comparison, and the matched-observation MRP pair. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction; reuse def-eigenvalue-eigenvector-eigenspace-and-spectrum for the finite matrix vocabulary. **Sources:** Sutton & Barto PDF pp. 283–285 (printed pp. 263–265) for Baird’s construction and expected-update caveat; pp. 296–298 (printed pp. 276–278) for §11.6 and Example 11.4. The book gives the example and learning-limit argument but this scaffold must supply the exact finite calculations. No general deep-RL convergence or divergence result is implied.

### ML-78 — Model-based planning, Dyna, and model exploitation

**Pages:** model-based-planning-dyna-and-model-exploitation / model-based-planning-dyna-and-model-exploitation-examples. **Scope:** Define a learned transition/reward model, tabular Dyna-Q, RTDP, finite-horizon model-predictive control, model error, and model exploitation. Sutton’s Dyna-Q example uses a deterministic table model and samples only previously experienced state-action pairs; do not silently turn it into an accurate stochastic model or a performance guarantee. For RTDP distinguish its generic trajectory-sampling algorithm from the source’s almost-sure optimal-relevant-state result: the latter is an undiscounted episodic stochastic optimal-path task with absorbing zero-reward goals, a proper policy reaching a goal almost surely from every start, strictly negative nonggoal rewards, zero initial goal values, initial values at least optimal, and the stated start-state sampling and greedy tie rule. State a finite-horizon single-policy simulation bound only under uniform reward error $|r-\hat r|\le\epsilon_r$ and transition total-variation error $\|P-\hat P\|_{TV}:=\frac12\sum_{s'\in S}|P(s'|s,a)-\hat P(s'|s,a)|\le\epsilon_p$ on all state-action pairs reachable in either model within the horizon: for rewards in $[0,1]$ and a common initial law, the proposed bound is $|J_H^\pi-\hat J_H^\pi|\le H\epsilon_r+\binom{H}{2}\epsilon_p$. This local finite-horizon statement does not certify the model-optimized policy under distribution shift. Treat Sutton’s MCTS/planning examples and Levine’s learned-model discussion as algorithms/context unless a separate guarantee is proved. Ensemble disagreement is not calibrated uncertainty by definition. Proposed interfaces: def-learned-mdp-model, alg-dyna-q, alg-model-predictive-control, thm-single-policy-finite-horizon-simulation-bound. **Proof route:** for a finite horizon, telescope true and learned Bellman operators or couple the two trajectories, retaining the visited-pair and horizon assumptions; state the resulting error terms for reward mismatch and transition total variation. Prove no model-optimized policy or off-support guarantee from a bound for one fixed policy. **Examples:** accurate model on behavior support, model exploitation off support, short versus long rollout. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction; def-measure-kernel-and-probability-kernel. **Sources:** Sutton & Barto PDF pp. 179–213 (printed pp. 159–193), Chapter 8: Dyna-Q model assumptions/algorithm, pp. 182–185; RTDP and its restricted convergence theorem, pp. 197–199; MCTS/search algorithm descriptions, pp. 203–213. Levine et al. §§2.1 and 5.1–5.3 provides offline model-exploitation context, not a proof of the proposed simulation bound.

### ML-79 — Offline reinforcement learning and off-policy evaluation

**Pages:** offline-reinforcement-learning-and-off-policy-evaluation / offline-reinforcement-learning-and-off-policy-evaluation-examples. **Scope:** Define a fixed logged dataset of trajectories generated under a behavior policy, target/behavior trajectory and per-decision likelihood ratios, ordinary and weighted importance sampling, and a doubly robust return estimator. For unbiased finite-horizon off-policy evaluation require the target trajectory law to be absolutely continuous with respect to the behavior law, known/correct behavior propensities, and integrable returns/weights; specify whether episodes are independent draws or a conditionally generated sample. State the doubly robust identity only under its exact alternative—correct propensity ratios or a correct finite-horizon value model at every step, including the terminal convention—and localize the finite-horizon residual correction. Explain product-ratio variance, weighted-estimator finite-sample bias, clipping bias, support mismatch, pessimism/conservative policy constraints, and model exploitation. Give a separate local two-MDP example with identical logged-data laws on behavior support but different optimal actions when a target action is unseen. Do not claim generic offline-RL safety or performance. Proposed interfaces: def-offline-reinforcement-learning-dataset, alg-trajectory-importance-sampling-ope, alg-doubly-robust-ope, thm-offline-coverage-impossibility-example. **Proof route:** factor the finite-horizon trajectory density with the chain rule for Radon–Nikodym derivatives and integrate against it; prove doubly robust unbiasedness by conditioning under the correct-propensity and correct-model cases separately; construct and compare the two finite MDPs for the support impossibility. **Examples:** full support versus missing support, exploding trajectory weights, and correct-model versus correct-propensity cases. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-73 — Tabular prediction and control by interaction; def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra; def-absolutely-continuous-with-respect-to-a-positive-measure; def-radon-nikodym-derivative; thm-radon-nikodym-density-exists-and-is-unique-up-to-almost-everywhere-equality; thm-integration-against-a-density; and thm-chain-rule-for-radon-nikodym-derivatives. Carry each supplier’s sigma-finiteness, integrability, absolute-continuity, and Choice premises when used. **Sources:** Levine et al., “Offline Reinforcement Learning: Tutorial, Review, and Perspectives on Open Problems,” complete 2020 review PDF pp. 1–43: finite-horizon trajectory law and RL setup, pp. 3–5; importance sampling, per-decision/weighted estimators and doubly robust OPE, pp. 10–12; distribution shift and policy/support constraints, pp. 18–24; model exploitation, pp. 26–28; open problems, pp. 24–25 and 28–30. The review attributes formal OPE and offline-RL guarantees to cited papers rather than proving the proposed elementary estimators or indistinguishability result; those proofs remain local obligations.

### ML-80 — POMDPs and belief-state planning

**Pages:** pomdps-and-belief-state-planning / pomdps-and-belief-state-planning-examples. **Scope:** Let finite state, action, and observation sets and a known transition, reward, and observation model define the POMDP. Define the belief $b(s)=\Pr(S_t=s\mid$ observation/action history$)$ and, when $P(o\mid b,a)>0$, update $b'(s')=\frac{O(o\mid s')\sum_sT(s'\mid s,a)b(s)}{\sum_z O(o\mid z)\sum_sT(z\mid s,a)b(s)}$; zero-probability observations are outside the a.s. update and require a stated filler convention if an everywhere-defined map is desired. The belief is a sufficient statistic and induces a fully observed belief MDP. For a finite horizon, represent each policy tree by an $\alpha$-vector; each tree value is linear in $b$, so the optimal value is the maximum of finitely many linear forms and is piecewise-linear convex. State witness pruning and its horizon/tree-count restrictions. Explain that policy-tree and witness counts can grow exponentially, and that this paper assumes a known model rather than learning it. Proposed interfaces: def-finite-pomdp, def-belief-state-and-bayes-filter, thm-belief-mdp-sufficient-statistic, thm-finite-horizon-pomdp-piecewise-linear-convex-value, alg-pomdp-witness-backup. **Proof route:** derive the finite Bayes update from the joint transition/observation kernel; prove sufficient-statistic Markovity by conditioning on the history; induct on the finite horizon to obtain the policy-tree linear forms and their finite maximum. **Examples:** two hidden states, observation aliasing, zero-likelihood observations, and exponential policy-tree growth. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; def-measure-kernel-and-probability-kernel, thm-measurability-of-integration-against-a-kernel, def-conditional-probability-given-a-sigma-algebra, and thm-bayes-formula-for-dominated-kernels, specialized to the finite sets and positive observation normalizer. **Sources:** Kaelbling–Littman–Cassandra, Brown CS-96-08, full PDF pp. 1–29; mathematical body pp. 3–27 (PDF p. 2 blank): belief update and belief-MDP reduction, PDF pp. 9–11; policy trees and linear belief value, pp. 12–16; finite-horizon piecewise-linear convex value and witness pruning, pp. 16–19. The text layer loses mathematical symbols, so equations were checked in the cached PDF.

### ML-81 — Imitation learning and DAgger

**Pages:** imitation-learning-and-dagger / imitation-learning-and-dagger-examples. **Scope:** Define horizon-$T$ task cost, expert surrogate loss, behavior cloning, learner-induced state distributions $d_{\pi_i}$, dataset aggregation, and the DAgger reduction. For the task-cost conversion, carry the source premise that the surrogate loss upper-bounds expert action disagreement and that replacing the expert action by any action changes the remaining expert cost-to-go by at most $u$ at every state/time. State the infinite-sample no-regret guarantee only for bounded strongly convex surrogate losses, learner-generated policies $\hat\pi_i$, expert labels on states sampled under interaction policies $\pi_i$, and the source’s exponentially decaying expert-use weights $\beta_i\le(1-\alpha)^{i-1}$ for fixed $\alpha>0$ (hence $\bar\beta_N=N^{-1}\sum_i\beta_i\to0$). With $\epsilon_N=\min_{\pi\in\Pi}N^{-1}\sum_i\mathbb E_{s\sim d_{\pi_i}}[\ell(s,\pi)]$, Theorem 3.2 gives an output policy with $J(\hat\pi)\le J(\pi^*)+uT\epsilon_N+O(1)$ after $\widetilde O(uT)$ iterations. Keep Theorem 3.3’s separate finite-sample statement distinct: for $N=O(T^2\log(1/\delta))$ iterations and $m=O(1)$ trajectories per iteration, its Azuma argument gives with probability at least $1-\delta$ an output policy whose induced surrogate loss is at most the empirical $\hat\epsilon_N+O(1/T)$. Expert labels under learner-induced states require interaction; this is not an offline guarantee from fixed demonstrations. Proposed interfaces: def-imitation-learning-and-expert-surrogate-loss, alg-dagger, thm-dagger-no-regret-reduction. **Proof route:** telescope hybrid expert/learner policies, use the one-step cost-to-go bound to convert action disagreement to task cost, then combine online regret over collected distributions with the vanishing expert-mixing average. Apply Azuma only under the finite-sample trajectory sampling and bounded-loss premises. **Examples:** behavior-cloning covariate shift, interactive expert labels, and a surrogate loss that fails to control task cost. **Requires:** ML-65 — Online protocols and regret benchmarks; ML-66 — Hedge and online gradient descent for the no-regret comparison; ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; thm-azuma-hoeffding-inequality for the finite-sample statement. **Sources:** Ross–Gordon–Bagnell, *A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning*, complete AISTATS/JMLR W&CP 15 PDF pp. 1–9 (printed pp. 627–635): cost-to-go reduction and one-step condition, pp. 2–3; DAgger, expert mixing, Theorems 3.1–3.4, pp. 4–6; no-regret proof, pp. 6–8. Experiments are not theorem evidence.

### ML-82 — Constrained MDPs and policy safety

**Pages:** constrained-mdps-and-policy-safety / constrained-mdps-and-policy-safety-examples. **Scope:** Define a finite discounted CMDP with bounded reward and cost, discount $\gamma\in[0,1)$, expected discounted return/cost, feasible policies, and a trust-region update. State Achiam et al.’s Theorem 1 in its average-divergence form: for $f:S\to\mathbb R$, define $\delta_f(s,a,s')=r(s,a,s')+\gamma f(s')-f(s)$, $\epsilon_f=\max_s|\mathbb E_{a\sim\pi',s'\sim P}[\delta_f(s,a,s')]|$, $L_{\pi,f}(\pi')=(1-\gamma)^{-1}\mathbb E_{s\sim d^\pi,a\sim\pi',s'\sim P}[\delta_f(s,a,s')]$, and $D^\pm_{\pi,f}(\pi')=L_{\pi,f}(\pi')\pm\frac{2\gamma\epsilon_f}{(1-\gamma)^2}\mathbb E_{s\sim d^\pi}[D_{\rm TV}(\pi'\|\pi)[s]]$, where $d^\pi$ is the normalized discounted occupancy and $D_{\rm TV}(p\|q)=\frac12\sum_a|p(a)-q(a)|$. The theorem states $D^-_{\pi,f}(\pi')\le J(\pi')-J(\pi)\le D^+_{\pi,f}(\pi')$; the cost analogue follows by applying it to each cost return. The main paper sends Theorem 1’s proof to its supplement, which is not included in the cached main paper; retain the full local derivation obligation or acquire/read that supplement before calling the result proved. Separate the idealized trust-region bound from CPO’s sampled linearization, conjugate-gradient solve, line search, and cost shaping. Expected discounted-cost control is not pathwise safety or guaranteed safe exploration. Proposed interfaces: def-constrained-markov-decision-process, def-discounted-cost-constraint, alg-constrained-policy-optimization, thm-policy-performance-bound-by-average-divergence, rem-cpo-implementation-is-approximate. **Proof route:** derive the performance-difference identity, bound the state-distribution change by expected TV and then average KL using Pinsker/Jensen, and apply the same calculation to each cost. Reuse published `thm-pinsker` with event-supremum TV<=sqrt(KL/2), natural logarithms and AC for the finite-action TV/KL inequality; finite-sample and policy-linearization errors remain separate local estimates. **Examples:** an expected-cost constraint with rare violations, ideal update versus sampled line-search update, and empirical near-satisfaction. **Requires:** ML-72 — Finite MDPs and Bellman operators; ML-76 — Policy gradients, REINFORCE, and actor–critic; def-measure-kernel-and-probability-kernel, thm-measurability-of-integration-against-a-kernel, and def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra; prove finite-action Pinsker/Jensen locally. **Sources:** Achiam et al., *Constrained Policy Optimization*, complete cached ICML/PMLR 70 main-paper PDF pp. 1–10: Theorem 1 and average-divergence definitions, pp. 3–4; TV-to-KL comparison and trust-region Proposition 1, pp. 4–5; CPO constrained update and practical approximation, pp. 5–8. The theorem statement and Proposition 1 are source-backed; the Theorem 1 proof is not in the main paper and the supplement was not inspected.

### ML-83 — Empirical-Bernstein confidence sequences under adaptive sampling

**2026-10-18 interface repair:** The earlier MSF-34 and SME sequential interfaces are now published carriers, not future proposals. Reuse `def-nonnegative-test-supermartingale`, `thm-villes-inequality`, `thm-mixture-of-null-supermartingales`, `def-confidence-sequence`, and `thm-confidence-sequence-by-test-inversion` where they resolve in the current supplier inventory. Scalar empirical-Bernstein self-normalization remains a local result; an unresolved or renamed exact supplier must be reconciled before dispatch.

**Pages:** anytime-valid-scalar-confidence-sequences / anytime-valid-scalar-confidence-sequences-examples. **Scope:** Reuse the earlier confidence-sequence and test-supermartingale interfaces. Let $(\mathcal F_t)$ be a filtration, $X_t$ be $\mathcal F_t$-measurable with $X_t\in[a,b]$ almost surely, and let the forecast $\widehat X_t\in[a,b]$ be $\mathcal F_{t-1}$-measurable. Define $\bar X_t=t^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^tX_i$, $\mu_t=t^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^t\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal F_{i-1}]$, and the observed, adapted empirical squared-error process $V_t=\sum_{i=1}^t(X_i-\widehat X_i)^2$; $V_t$ is not the predictable conditional-variance process. For any valid one-sided sub-exponential uniform boundary $u$ with crossing probability $\alpha$ and scale $c=b-a$, Theorem 4 gives $\Pr(\forall t\ge1:|\bar X_t-\mu_t|<u(V_t)/t)\ge1-2\alpha$. For nominal error $\delta$, set $\alpha=\delta/2$. The forecast need not equal the conditional mean, and the theorem requires no common conditional mean; identifying $\mu_t$ with one fixed mean requires the extra premise $\mathbb E[X_i\mid\mathcal F_{i-1}]=\mu$ for every $i$. Keep matrix claims out: Howard et al.’s matrix results rely on separate facts. Distinguish all-times validity/arbitrary stopping from adaptive regret and from validity under unmodeled distribution shift. Proposed new items: alg-empirical-bernstein-confidence-sequence; thm-empirical-bernstein-confidence-sequence. **Proof route:** consume Ville, mixture closure, and confidence-sequence inversion from upstream. Reproduce Appendix A.8’s scalar self-normalization: after scaling to $[0,1]$, set $Y_t=X_t-\mathbb E_{t-1}X_t$ and $\delta_t=\widehat X_t-\mathbb E_{t-1}X_t$; verify $Y_t-\delta_t=X_t-\widehat X_t\in[-1,1]$. For $\xi\ge-1$ and $0\le\lambda<1$, prove the Fan inequality $\exp(\lambda\xi-\psi_E(\lambda)\xi^2)\le1+\lambda\xi$, where $\psi_E(\lambda)=[-\log(1-\lambda)-\lambda]$, using $(x-\log(1+x))/x^2=\int_0^1t/(1+tx)\,dt$ and its monotonicity. Conditional expectation then gives the exponential supermartingale for each sign; apply the chosen one-sided boundary to the positive and negative processes and union bound, yielding the two-sided Theorem 4 statement. Do not restate a generic confidence-sequence definition or re-prove the generic mixture theorem here. **Examples:** repeated fixed-level tests versus an anytime-valid boundary; a predictable regression forecast whose observed squared errors define $V_t$; a matrix confidence sequence requiring a separate source. **Requires:** Reuse published def-filtration-and-filtered-probability-space, def-adapted-and-integrable-stochastic-process, def-predictable-discrete-time-process, def-martingale-submartingale-and-supermartingale, def-conditional-expectation-given-a-sigma-algebra, and thm-tower-property-of-conditional-expectation for the local scalar construction. Also consume the published earlier-category interfaces MSF-34 (Category 1) — Sequential Testing and Anytime-Valid Inference (def-nonnegative-test-supermartingale, thm-villes-inequality, thm-mixture-of-null-supermartingales, def-confidence-sequence, thm-confidence-sequence-by-test-inversion), and SME-58 (Category 2) — Discrete Test Supermartingales and Ville plus SME-60 (Category 2) — Sub-Gaussian Mixture Confidence Sequences, with their current published item contracts. Their cross-category contracts supply scalar Ville and mixture-sequence infrastructure; use the mixture boundary only when its product-measurability and supermartingale hypotheses hold. The local empirical-Bernstein extension does not feed back as a supplier to an earlier category. **Sources:** Howard et al., *Time-uniform, nonparametric, nonasymptotic confidence sequences*, arXiv v9, Theorem 4 PDF p. 14 and complete scalar self-normalization proof Appendix A.8 PDF p. 35; the paper is cached in full (PDF pp. 1–48). The source’s theorem uses the observed forecast errors and explicitly does not require a common mean. Exclude matrix conclusions whose proof depends on unread Howard et al. (2020) results.
## Additional algorithm and frontier designs (2026-10-18)

The following ten pairs extend the preserved ML-01–83 inventory. They have no backward dependency into ML-01–83: those pairs may link them contextually, but do not use them as proof suppliers. Their order is a topological order among the additions. Source-reading extents below are recorded in the source dossier; citation is not a claim of a complete-paper audit. Every displayed claim has a concrete proof design. None is an assertion that its future items already exist or have accepted proofs. Empirical foundation-model evidence stays distinct from proved finite mathematical claims. Examples pages are leaves.

### ML-84 — Sketched kernel learning and certified approximate solves

**Pages:** sketched-kernel-learning-and-certified-solves / sketched-kernel-learning-and-certified-solves-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-regularized-kernel-prediction-system`, `alg-nystrom-kernel-ridge-fit`, `thm-kernel-ridge-gram-perturbation-bound`, `thm-kernel-ridge-residual-prediction-certificate`, `rem-statistical-and-numerical-kernel-errors`. For a finite real PSD Gram matrix K, response y in R^n and tau=n lambda>0 under mean-square empirical loss plus lambda times squared RKHS norm, define a=(K+tau I)^{-1}y and fitted vector u=Ka=y−tau a. The algorithm takes K,y,tau, a conformal finite sketch S, a solve tolerance epsilon>=0, and a finite solve budget; invoke published PSD Nyström to form Ktilde=KS(S^TKS)^dagger S^TK, solve (Ktilde+tau I)atilde=y, and return the coefficients, Ktilde atilde, and actual residual or `budget-exhausted` with the available residual. A budget exit is not a certified solve; finite comparison always returns a status. Use direct factorization or a separately specified iterative solver, never an instruction merely to “invert accurately.” For approximate coefficient b with residual r=y−(K+tau I)b, ||b−a||<=||r||/tau and ||Kb−Ka||<=||K|| ||r||/tau. For any two PSD K,Ktilde with ||K−Ktilde||<=epsilon, ||a−atilde||<=epsilon ||y||/tau^2 and ||u−utilde||<=epsilon ||y||/tau. Include these precise deterministic guarantees separately from a statistical risk bound.

**Argument:** The PSD spectral theorem gives both inverse norms <=1/tau. The resolvent identity a−atilde=(K+tau I)^{-1}(Ktilde−K)(Ktilde+tau I)^{-1}y gives the coefficient estimate; u−utilde=−tau(a−atilde) gives the fitted-vector estimate. Subtracting linear systems gives b−a=−(K+tau I)^{-1}r. Reuse `thm-psd-nystrom-order-and-exactness` and `thm-nystrom-deterministic-spectral-residual-bound` for PSD and spectral approximation certificates, with its full-row-rank leading sketch premise. Gaussian range-finder expectations in the published randomized low-rank page are only expectations for their exact real/complex Gaussian sketch regimes; do not silently turn them into high-probability or leverage-score results. Out-of-sample prediction additionally needs a cross-kernel approximation certificate; the training-vector estimate alone supplies none.

**Prerequisites:** ML-14/15; published Nyström definition/projector/order/error items, finite SVD and regularized least squares. **Examples:** zero/singular K; exact captured range; small lambda amplifying coefficient error; low rank with poor test-point cross-kernel. **Sources:** actual published Nyström theorem bodies read in this refresh; inherited GPML kernel-ridge normalization. No new statistical effective-dimension theorem is imported.

### ML-85 — Bilevel learning, hypergradients, and implicit layers

**Pages:** bilevel-learning-hypergradients-and-implicit-layers / bilevel-learning-hypergradients-and-implicit-layers-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-smooth-bilevel-learning-problem`, `alg-adjoint-hypergradient-with-residual`, `thm-smooth-bilevel-hypergradient-formula`, `thm-adjoint-hypergradient-linear-solve-error`, `rem-truncated-training-and-exact-hypergradients`. Let theta in an open subset of R^p, w in R^d, inner loss f be C2, outer loss g be C1. At (theta0,w0) suppose grad_w f=0 and H=partial^2_ww f is invertible. The local stationary branch w*(theta) exists and is C1; it need not be a global inner optimizer. With B=partial_theta grad_w f, J(theta)=g(w*(theta),theta), the column-gradient formula is grad J=grad_theta g−B^T H^{-T}grad_w g. If H>=mu I for mu>0 and f is globally strongly convex in w on R^d, this branch is the unique inner optimizer wherever its stationary point exists. Keep that additional regime separate.

The algorithm takes a supplied inner state w, the matrices/oracles H,B, the two derivatives of g, mu>0 with a valid H>=mu I certificate, a tolerance and finite linear-solve budget. Solve H^T v=grad_w g by an admissible SPD solver; return grad_theta g−B^T vtilde, actual residual rho=grad_w g−H^T vtilde and success/budget status. At the exact inner state, the hypergradient error is <=||B|| ||rho||/mu. At an approximate inner state add a separately proved state/sensitivity perturbation bound, requiring a common invertible chart, Lipschitz derivatives and bounded inverse; do not identify a low training residual with a hypergradient guarantee.

**Argument:** Apply published `thm-euclidean-implicit-function-theorem` to R(theta,w)=grad_w f; Dw*=−H^{-1}B. Apply the total-derivative chain rule and transpose to obtain the formula. Solve-error bound follows from v−vtilde=H^{-T}rho. Reuse the full-chart hypotheses of `lem-implicit-sensitivity-error-controlled-by-residual`. Published `thm-finite-iteration-sensitivity-bound-and-residual-limitation` explicitly distinguishes differentiating K training steps from differentiating an exact fixed point: its contraction q<1, derivative-Lipschitz constants and C1 initialization give q^K d0+C K q^(K−1) delta0; zero primal residual alone need not give a correct derivative. The same issue occurs for meta-learning and equilibrium networks. **Prerequisites:** published implicit-function theorem, AD and Hessian-vector interfaces, SPD linear solves; ML-37 for neural specialization. **Examples:** ridge hyperparameter; singular Hessian at a nonisolated optimum; exact state with wrong truncated derivative. **Sources:** actual published sensitivity proof bodies read; no uninspected deep-equilibrium global-convergence theorem is claimed.

### ML-86 — Exact tiled attention and memory-aware differentiation

**Pages:** exact-tiled-attention-and-memory-aware-differentiation / exact-tiled-attention-and-memory-aware-differentiation-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-attention-block-summary`, `alg-exact-tiled-softmax-attention`, `thm-tiled-attention-exactness`, `thm-tiled-attention-workspace-and-work`, `rem-attention-io-model-and-roundoff`. Take finite real Q,K in R^(n times d), V in R^(n times v), scale c, a fixed admissible-key mask with at least one key in every query row, and positive integer key-block size b. Return rowwise softmax(c QK^T+mask)V. For one row initialize an empty summary, not the undefined exponential of minus infinity minus minus infinity. For each nonempty allowed block J form scores s_j, m_J=max_J s_j, l_J=sum_J exp(s_j−m_J), and z_J=sum_J exp(s_j−m_J)V_j. If the old summary is empty, copy these values. Otherwise set m'=max(m,m_J), l'=exp(m−m')l+exp(m_J−m')l_J and z'=exp(m−m')z+exp(m_J−m')z_J. Skip empty masked blocks; return z/l after the last block. Specify the query and key loops and stop after the finite block partition. No all-masked-row distribution is defined without a separate convention.

**Argument:** Induct on processed allowed keys: m is their maximum, l=sum exp(s_j−m), z=sum exp(s_j−m)V_j. The merge equations preserve each invariant by factoring exp(m_old−m_new). A nonempty final row has l>0; z/l is exact dense attention in real arithmetic. Evaluating one query at a time needs O(n(d+v)) stored inputs/output plus O(b+v) extra score/summary workspace, arithmetic O(n^2(d+v)); there is no materialized n by n score/probability array. A blocked query implementation trades SRAM/workspace against rereads. For backward differentiation replay the same fixed mask and recompute blocks, applying published AD/chain rule; if dropout is added its random mask must also be replayed. Exact real arithmetic does not imply bitwise equality of floating-point accumulation orders or a hardware speedup.

**Prerequisites:** ML-35 and published reverse-mode correctness; finite sums, exponentials, matrix multiplication. **Sources:** Dao et al., FlashAttention, full text retrieved; §§2–3 (pp. 3–6) and Appendix C proofs (pp. 22–24) consulted, selected algorithm appendices pp. 20–21. This refresh's provable workspace/work claim is for the explicitly defined algorithm above. The paper’s SRAM/HBM bound Theta(n^2 d^2/M), for its particular tiling/memory model and range d<=M<=nd, is contextual until that exact carrier is authored; its endpoint input-size lower bound is not a universal lower bound at every fixed M. **Examples:** one key; an empty first masked block; two merge orders; replaying a different dropout mask.

### ML-87 — Low-rank adaptation and the geometry of parameter updates

**Pages:** low-rank-adaptation-and-parameter-update-geometry / low-rank-adaptation-and-parameter-update-geometry-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-low-rank-adaptation-of-a-linear-layer`, `alg-finite-budget-low-rank-adaptation`, `thm-low-rank-adapter-merging-and-gradients`, `thm-low-rank-adapter-factorization-ambiguity`, `rem-adaptation-rank-and-empirical-transfer`. Freeze W0 in R^(d times k); choose rank budget 1<=r<=min(d,k), nonzero real scale c, A in R^(r times k), B in R^(d times r), and W=W0+cBA. Define layer output and train only A,B on a supplied differentiable finite empirical loss with fixed minibatch stream, admissible update method/steps and finite horizon T. State initial A (random seed/law if used), B=0, fixed dropout convention if any, and return A_T,B_T and optionally merged W. Each finite update must return a failure status if a supplied primitive is not defined. No neural convergence result is assumed.

**Argument:** rank(W−W0)<=r by image inclusion; trainable scalar count is r(d+k), excluding optional biases and frozen W0 storage. Exact merging preserves every layer output by distributivity, but switching/roundoff/device latency are separate matters. If G=partial L/partial W is the d by k matrix gradient, then grad_B L=c G A^T and grad_A L=c B^T G by the Frobenius inner product and chain rule. With B=0 the initial grad_A is zero, explaining why simultaneously zero A,B stalls this factorization. For any invertible r by r M, BA=(BM)(M^{-1}A), so factors are unidentifiable; this is not convexity of factor training. Any update matrix of rank<=r admits such a factorization by finite SVD; a rank budget below the required update rank is an expressivity obstruction. No theorem says the update needed by every downstream task has small rank or that low rank prevents catastrophic forgetting.

**Prerequisites:** ML-30/35/37, published SVD and matrix chain rule. **Sources:** Hu et al., LoRA arXiv:2106.09685, §§3–4 and beginning of §5, PDF pp. 3–5 read; not a complete-paper reading. Low-intrinsic-rank and measured speed/memory/task results are empirical motivation, not premises of the algebra above. **Examples:** identity adapter change of basis; zero/zero initialization; a full-rank desired linear update; merged versus unmerged arithmetic.

### ML-88 — Preference learning and direct preference optimization

**Pages:** preference-learning-and-direct-preference-optimization / preference-learning-and-direct-preference-optimization-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-bradley-terry-preference-model`, `def-kl-regularized-policy-improvement`, `alg-direct-preference-optimization-finite-budget`, `thm-finite-action-gibbs-policy-optimum`, `thm-dpo-reward-policy-reparameterization`, `rem-preference-coverage-and-model-misspecification`. For a fixed prompt, finite nonempty response set Y, strictly positive reference q, reward r:Y->R and beta>0, maximize F(p)=sum_y p_y r_y−beta sum_y p_y log(p_y/q_y) on the probability simplex, taking 0 log0=0. Let Z=sum_y q_y exp(r_y/beta), p*_y=q_y exp(r_y/beta)/Z. Then p* uniquely maximizes F and F(p*)−F(p)=beta KL(p||p*). Bradley–Terry assumes P(a preferred to b)=sigmoid(r_a−r_b); it is a model for comparisons, not a universal property of human preferences. Substitution gives sigmoid(beta log(p*_a/q_a)−beta log(p*_b/q_b)); adding a prompt-only constant to all rewards leaves both preferences and p* unchanged.

The algorithm takes a finite ordered preference dataset (x,y_w,y_l), a fixed full-support reference, positive differentiable conditional model on every observed response, beta>0, fixed minibatch indices, a stated optimizer/step schedule and finite T. Compute u=beta[log pi_theta(y_w|x)−log q(y_w|x)−log pi_theta(y_l|x)+log q(y_l|x)], evaluate stable loss log(1+exp(−u)), its gradient, perform the specified updates and return parameters/loss/status. This is supervised finite-budget optimization; its termination is separate from attainment or statistical optimality.

**Argument:** Expand beta KL(p||p*)=beta KL(p||q)−sum p r+beta log Z; finite log-sum/Gibbs nonnegativity and equality prove uniqueness. Subtract log p* equations to cancel log Z. Differentiate the finite loss to obtain −beta sigmoid(−u)[grad log pi_w−grad log pi_l]. Finite response support avoids an unproved infinite partition-function assumption; extension to countable strings requires 0<Z<infinity and integrability. Empirical preference MLE recovers p* only under extra identifiability, connected comparison support, realizability and consistency conditions; no such statistical theorem is imported here. A disconnected comparison graph permits component offsets that affect policy mass while preserving observed preferences.

**Prerequisites:** ML-07/13/37, published KL nonnegativity or its elementary finite log-sum specialization. **Sources:** Rafailov et al., DPO arXiv:2305.18290v3 (2024), §§1–5 pp. 1–6 and Appendix A pp. 15–19, implementation p. 20 read; experiments/completion examples not fully read. The appendix gradient has sign/orientation transcription inconsistencies; the local derivative above is derived directly from the displayed preferred-minus-dispreferred loss. **Examples:** two responses; disconnected comparison support; reward offset; zero reference support.

### ML-89 — Discrete masked diffusion and finite-state reverse generation

**Pages:** discrete-masked-diffusion-and-reverse-generation / discrete-masked-diffusion-and-reverse-generation-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-finite-masking-diffusion-chain`, `alg-finite-grid-masked-diffusion-sampler`, `thm-masking-chain-posterior`, `thm-masked-diffusion-kl-reduces-to-cross-entropy`, `thm-finite-chain-reverse-kernel-error-telescope`, `rem-discrete-diffusion-factorization-and-endpoint-limits`. Fix finite clean vocabulary V and separate mask m; length L>=1, grid 0=t0<...<tN=1, survival 1=alpha0>alpha1>...>alphaN=0. Independently mask each surviving token with forward probability 1−alpha_j/alpha_(j−1). For one token x and s<t, an observed unmasked z_t is copied backward; when z_t=m, the true conditional distribution given x is mass (alpha_s−alpha_t)/(1−alpha_t) at x and (1−alpha_s)/(1−alpha_t) at m. These are conditionals on positive-probability events; do not divide by zero at time 0.

The learned reverse sampler starts all masked. At each finite reverse-grid step, for each masked position compute a probability vector h_theta(z_t,t) on clean V with no mask mass; sample an unmask event with probability (alpha_s−alpha_t)/(1−alpha_t) and, if unmasking, sample a token from h; otherwise keep m. Copy already unmasked positions. Declare conditional independent sampling across positions as part of this model, all random seeds/kernels, and stop at t0. At alpha0=1 every remaining mask is removed. This defines a normalized finite generative law. It does not make that law the exact reversal of an arbitrary dependent sequence distribution: joint newly unmasked tokens can be conditionally dependent.

**Argument:** Bayes on the two possible precursor states gives the true single-token posterior. Write c=(alpha_s−alpha_t)/(1−alpha_t). With h(x)>0 the conditional KL of this posterior against the SUBS learned kernel, at a masked state and clean truth x, is −c log h(x): the mask probabilities agree and the only true clean mass is at x. Unmasked states contribute zero. Average over independently forward-masked training examples and sum positions/steps for the finite latent-chain ELBO; reuse published `thm-elbo-kl-decomposition` and its admissibility, retaining reconstruction/prior terms when endpoints are not exactly clean/all-masked. Infinite-time limits need dominated convergence/uniform integrability and are not inferred from the finite identity. If true and learned reverse kernels on the entire finite sequence state space satisfy sup_z TV(Q_j(z,.),P_j(z,.))<=epsilon_j, starting laws differ by epsilon_init, then endpoint TV<=epsilon_init+sum_j epsilon_j. Prove by adding/subtracting one kernel at a time and the TV contraction of a Markov kernel; clamp the bound at one. Marginal token error alone is not a full-sequence kernel error.

**Prerequisites:** ML-01, published finite Markov kernels/law construction and Bayesian ELBO, elementary finite conditional probability; no continuous reverse-SDE supplier is needed. **Sources:** Sahoo et al., Simple and Effective Masked Diffusion Language Models, arXiv:2406.07524; §§2.2–4 pp. 3–6, Appendix A/B pp. 18–22 read. Section 3.3 explicitly calls its parameterization simplification “Rao-Blackwellization” in a looser sense; do not claim a variance-reduction theorem without an actual conditional-expectation estimator pair. **Examples:** one-token exact posterior; two correlated tokens versus factorized reverse draws; model-error accumulation with zero mesh error.

### ML-90 — Monotone risk calibration and finite-sample deployment guarantees

**Pages:** monotone-risk-calibration-and-deployment-guarantees / monotone-risk-calibration-and-deployment-guarantees-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-finite-nested-decision-family-and-bounded-risk`, `alg-finite-grid-conformal-risk-control`, `thm-finite-grid-conformal-risk-control`, `thm-finite-grid-monotone-envelope-risk-control`, `rem-marginal-risk-control-and-adaptive-reuse`. Fix a deterministic finite ordered grid Lambda={lambda1<...<lambdaM}, n>=1, alpha in [0,B), B>0. Let the n+1 random loss vectors (L_i(lambda_j))_j be jointly exchangeable across i, measurable, coordinatewise nonincreasing with values in [0,B], and L_i(lambdaM)<=alpha almost surely. Conditional on an independently trained model the same conditions may be required under an actual regular conditional exchangeable law. Scan j increasingly and return the first j with [sum_(i<=n) L_i(lambda_j)+B]/(n+1)<=alpha; if none, return lambdaM. Output the selected prediction rule and pass/fallback status after at most M tests. Then E L_(n+1)(lambda_hat)<=alpha, with expectation over calibration and test data. The guarantee is not P(risk conditional on the realized calibration set<=alpha)>=1−delta, subgroup coverage, or validity after arbitrary reuse of calibration data.

**Argument:** Let lambda' be the first grid member with sum_(i<=n+1) L_i(lambda)/(n+1)<=alpha; it exists because the maximal member satisfies the endpoint condition. If the calibration scan passes at a member, its true all-n+1 average also passes since L_(n+1)<=B; hence lambda'<=lambda_hat, also on fallback. Monotonicity gives L_(n+1)(lambda_hat)<=L_(n+1)(lambda'). The latter selector is symmetric in all n+1 vectors. Exchangeability therefore makes E L_(n+1)(lambda') equal to E of their average at lambda', which is <=alpha. This finite proof needs no selection theorem, right-continuity, or conditional law given a random multiset. For nonmonotone finite losses define the tail-maximum envelope Ltilde_i(lambda_j)=max_(k>=j)L_i(lambda_k); applying the same algorithm to these monotone exchangeable finite vectors bounds the original test loss by pointwise domination, provided their maximal endpoint obeys the safe bound. The price can be conservative predictions.

**Prerequisites:** published exchangeability and finite measurable-selection/rank interfaces from mathematical statistics; ML-64 for the distinction from conformal miscoverage. **Sources:** Angelopoulos et al., Conformal Risk Control arXiv:2208.02814v4 (13 June 2025), entire 19-page extracted text read. Only the upper-control design is imported, with the finite-grid proof above. Printed Theorem 2's unconditional lower bound is excluded: constant-zero losses satisfy its printed premises but violate alpha−2B/(n+1)>0. Finite grids also avoid additional measurability issues from arbitrary indexing. No lower-tightness theorem is promised without repaired endpoint hypotheses; arbitrary-index robust envelopes additionally need measurable suprema. **Examples:** multilabel false-negative loss with an explicit convention for empty truth sets; all-zero loss; calibration fallback when alpha<B/(n+1); nonmonotone threshold failure.

### ML-91 — Verifiable generation, group-relative updates, and test-time sampling

**Pages:** verifiable-generation-and-test-time-sampling / verifiable-generation-and-test-time-sampling-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-verifier-and-generation-protocol`, `alg-budgeted-verifier-guided-generation`, `alg-finite-group-relative-policy-update`, `thm-independent-generation-success-and-verifier-error`, `thm-group-centered-score-estimator-factor`, `rem-reasoning-training-evidence-and-evaluation-targets`. Fix a prompt, finite truncated response space, sampling policy pi, measurable binary correctness C and verifier A, maximum N independent draws, and a deterministic tie rule for a supplied score. Rejection search returns the first verified response or explicit failure after N attempts; best-of-N samples all N and returns a maximal-score response. Declare the length/token budget and truncation token; these affect the distribution being evaluated. If C has success probability p and the verifier equals C, rejection-search success is 1−(1−p)^N. For an imperfect verifier, the probability of outputting a false acceptance is at most N epsilon when each candidate has P(A=1,C=0)<=epsilon; this union bound does not require independence, whereas the success formula does. An adaptive sampler needs conditional success lower bounds; if P(C_i=1|history)>=p on every history with no previous success, its failure probability is at most (1−p)^N by iterated conditioning.

Define a finite-budget GRPO-style procedure, separately from any guarantee: at each outer iteration freeze old policy with positive probabilities, draw G>=2 responses per prompt, compute finite rewards r_i, mean and population standard deviation s, set A_i=(r_i−mean r)/s if s>0 and A_i=0 if s=0, and keep these values fixed while optimizing a specified clipped importance-ratio empirical objective for K finite inner steps. The clipping threshold, sequence versus token ratio, reference policy, KL-estimator convention, optimizer, step schedule and failure status must be stated. This procedure does not promise monotone policy improvement or unbiased REINFORCE.

**Argument:** Independence yields the success formula by multiplying failure probabilities; false acceptance is a union of per-draw events, giving the verifier bound. For unnormalized group centering and iid responses from a positive differentiable finite policy, score S_i=grad log pi(Y_i), mean rbar, E S_i=0 and independent cross terms imply E[(1/G)sum_i(r_i−rbar)S_i]=(1−1/G)grad E r. Replacing rbar by the leave-one-out mean removes this multiplicative factor. Dividing by random group s, using clipped importance ratios or reusing old-policy samples changes the target again; none inherits unbiasedness from the baseline identity. State exact zero-variance handling to avoid dividing by zero. Correctness under a verifier is distinct from the verifier's own soundness and from mathematical proof checking.

**Prerequisites:** ML-76/79 and published finite policy-gradient/change-of-measure identities, differentiation of finite sums; ML-83 only if making an actual optional-stopping statistical statement. **Sources:** DeepSeek-AI, DeepSeek-R1 arXiv:2501.12948v1 (2025), §§2.2.1–2.2.3 pp. 5–6, §§2.3.2–2.4 pp. 10–11, §§4.2–5 pp. 15–16 read in the original 22-page version; the later 86-page revision was retrieved but not read and supplies no claim. GRPO/clipping, rule-based rewards, rejection filtering, distillation and search limitations are descriptions/empirical evidence. No universal reasoning-scaling or training-convergence law is inferred. The zero-s branch and estimator-factor calculations above are explicit local completions. **Examples:** all group rewards equal; perfect versus false-positive verifier; repeated dependent copies with no sampling gain; empirical pass@1 versus oracle pass@N versus verifier-selected accuracy.

### ML-92 — Active querying, version spaces, and abstention

**Pages:** active-querying-version-spaces-and-abstention / active-querying-version-spaces-and-abstention-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-pool-based-realizable-active-learning`, `alg-finite-pool-version-space-query`, `thm-active-version-space-invariant-and-query-bound`, `thm-disagreement-abstention-correctness`, `rem-active-label-complexity-and-noisy-oracles`. Fix a nonempty finite classifier list H, finite ordered unlabeled pool U, deterministic unknown target h* in H and exact label oracle. The learner starts V=H, scans the smallest unprocessed pool index, predicts its unanimous V label without querying, otherwise queries its oracle label and deletes every disagreeing hypothesis. Stop after all pool points or a supplied query budget; on budget exhaustion return current V and mark unresolved points as abstentions. The output is a version space, labeled pool entries and query log, not a distribution-free population guarantee. It retains h*, never becomes empty, and every unanimous prediction is correct. At most min(|U|,|H|−1) queried disagreements occur because each query removes at least one hypothesis. No logarithmic bound in |H| follows unless every queried label branch is known to shrink the current V by a fixed factor; e.g. branch sizes <=rho|V| for 0<rho<1 gives at most log(|H|)/log(1/rho) disagreement queries.

**Argument:** Induct on oracle updates: realizability retains h*, so a unanimous prediction equals h*. On a disagreement both label sets are nonempty and the wrong set is removed, proving strict cardinality decrease. Iterate the optional rho bound separately, stopping when |V|=1. For a fresh point, predicting only where V is unanimous gives zero error on answered points under the exact target assumption; its abstention probability is P_X(DIS(V)), not a bound automatically controlled by |V|. Without realizability/noiseless labels the invariant fails and a robust learner needs a new model and concentration argument. **Prerequisites:** ML-01–03 and finite sets; no additional unproved disagreement-coefficient theorem. **Examples:** thresholds with binary-search query selection; a finite star class with only one hypothesis eliminated per queried positive target response; empty-pool and one-hypothesis boundaries. **Sources:** inherited finite-class PAC framework; all finite algorithm properties are justified by the complete arguments here. Research connections include selective prediction and adaptive acquisition, with no unaudited optimal active-learning rate claim.

### ML-93 — Semi-supervised graph propagation and pseudo-labeling limits

**Pages:** semi-supervised-graph-propagation-and-pseudolabeling-limits / semi-supervised-graph-propagation-and-pseudolabeling-limits-examples. **Scope and interfaces:** `def-graph-harmonic-label-extension`, `alg-graph-harmonic-label-propagation`, `thm-graph-harmonic-extension-uniqueness-and-maximum-principle`, `alg-finite-round-confidence-pseudolabeling`, `thm-unlabeled-law-alone-does-not-identify-labels`, `rem-semisupervised-cluster-assumptions`. Let W be a finite symmetric nonnegative weight matrix, L=D−W, nonempty labeled subset B, unlabeled subset U and prescribed real labels y_B. Require every connected component containing an unlabeled vertex to meet B; an all-unlabeled component returns `unanchored-component` instead of a singular solve. Minimize E(f)=one-half f^T L f with f_B=y_B. The unique unlabeled solution is f_U=−L_UU^{-1}L_UB y_B. The algorithm validates symmetry/nonnegativity and component anchors, forms the principal linear system, calls an SPD solver with finite budget/residual and returns values/status. Multiclass one-hot labels can be propagated one coordinate at a time; each predicted row lies in the probability simplex under the same exact anchored solve. A residual certificate is numerical; it does not certify a true class label.

**Argument:** For a vector v zero on B, v^T L v=one-half sum_ij w_ij(v_i−v_j)^2. It vanishes only if v is constant on every component; an anchor forces each relevant constant to zero. Thus L_UU is SPD, giving existence/uniqueness and the normal equations. Each unlabeled value equals a nonnegative weighted average of its neighbors. A strict interior maximum above the boundary maximum propagates to every connected neighbor, eventually contradicting an anchor; the minimum is analogous. Apply this to each one-hot coordinate and to their sum, whose unique harmonic boundary data are one, to obtain nonnegative coordinates summing to one. Pseudo-labeling is a separate heuristic: for T finite rounds retrain with a stated learner on true labels plus selected high-confidence predictions, specify threshold, no-relabel rule/ties, weights and stop when no points are added. Finite termination follows from the supplied budget/no-relabel rule, not from improved test risk. Without a structural link between P_X and P_(Y|X), two laws can have identical P_X, identical labels on every observed labeled input and opposite deterministic labels at an unobserved input of positive P_X mass. Any same output predictor has error at least half that unseen mass under one of the two laws. This elementary two-point experiment proves nonidentification; it does not refute gains under explicit cluster/smoothness/low-density-separation assumptions.

**Prerequisites:** ML-20 weighted-Laplacian identity, published SPD solves, ML-01 risk. **Examples:** path with endpoint labels; unanchored component; confident wrong pseudo-label locked in by the no-relabel rule; indistinguishable unlabeled laws. **Sources:** the weighted graph and finite decision-theoretic suppliers already specified in ML-20/MSF-03; harmonic-extension uniqueness, maximum principle and nonidentification have complete supporting arguments above. No continuum graph-limit, semi-supervised minimax improvement or consistency theorem is imported without a separate sampling/scaling/prerequisite design.

## Prerequisite pairs and category ownership

Owner direction (2026-10-17): the Machine Learning and Adaptive Decision Methods
category originally contained 90 A/B pairs: ML-01–ML-83 plus P-KR-01, P-KR-02, P-SDE-02,
P-WGF-01, P-WGF-02, P-RMT-01 and P-RMT-02. These seven prerequisite pairs
belong to `machine-learning` and precede their consumers. The 2026-10-18 additions ML-84–93 bring the category total to 100 pairs. Their mathematical
scope remains general: category placement does not introduce a dependency on
learning-specific results. The other five prerequisite designs below retain
their existing probability/numerical homes and are reused without duplication.

Current status: P-SDE-01 strong well-posedness/moments and all four P-NUM-ODE/SDE pairs have published carriers in their existing homes. P-SDE-01 density regularity has a separate eight-item Gaussian-smoothing addendum specified in this refresh; broader density assumptions stay explicit. The seven machine-learning-category prerequisites remain new authoring obligations; their general mathematical scope is retained.
Place each before its consumers; Category 3 may reuse a general supplier but
must never depend on a learning-specific result. Source anchors identify what
